Triangulated categories of logarithmic motives over a field
Federico Binda, Doosung Park, Paul Arne Østvær

Abstract
. In this work, we develop a theory of motives for logarithmic schemes over fields in the sense of Fontaine, Illusie, and Kato. Our construction is based on the notion of finite log correspondences, the dividing Nisnevich topology on log schemes, and the basic idea of parameterizing homotopies by □ ¯ \overline{\Box} ; the projective line with respect to its compactifying logarithmic structure at infinity. We show that Hodge cohomology of log schemes is a □ ¯ \overline{\Box} -invariant theory that is representable in the category of logarithmic motives. The category of logarithmic motives is closely related to Voevodsky’s category of motives and 𝔸 1 \mathbb{A}^{1} -invariant theories. Assuming resolution of singularities, we identify the latter with the full subcategory comprised of 𝔸 1 \mathbb{A}^{1} -local objects in the category of logarithmic motives. Fundamental properties such as □ ¯ \overline{\Box} -homotopy invariance, Mayer-Vietoris for coverings, and the analogs of the Gysin sequence and the Thom space isomorphism witness the robustness of the setup.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 14A21, 14A30, 14F42, 18N40, 18N55 ; Secondary 18F10, 18G35, 19E15

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Overview

Logarithmic structures and log geometry developed by Deligne, Faltings, Fontaine, Illusie, Kato, Tsuji,
and many others
[
76
]
can be thought of as an enlargement of algebraic geometry.
Having its origin in the theory of logarithmic differentials,
log schemes make the notion of schemes with a boundary precise and have implications, inter alia, towards moduli spaces, deformation theory, and
p
-adic Hodge theory.
The primary purpose of this work is to build a theory of motives in the setting of logarithmic algebraic geometry.

Depending on the point of view, there are several desiderata for a theory of motives of log schemes.
For starters, one should find a way to linearize log schemes such that the values of standard cohomology theories,
e.g.,
crystalline, de Rham-Witt, and Hodge cohomology,
occur as hom-groups in the same linear tensor category.
One can hope that log motives give rise to new invariants for logarithmic schemes and a better understanding of “old” invariants for usual schemes.
For sanity checks, one should have available various realization functors for log motives and their regulator maps.
By equipping schemes with trivial log structures, one should be able to recover motives in the sense of algebraic geometry.
Finally,
an advanced understanding of the basic properties should involve the formalism of six operations.
In this work, we begin this exploration by constructing the triangulated category of effective log motives
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
,
extending Voevodsky’s category
{\bf DM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
,
where
\Lambda
is a commutative unital ring.

There have been several constructions of triangulated categories of motives in algebraic geometry,
including Levine
[
62
]
and Voevodsky
[
89
]
,
which, together with motivic homotopy theory, have led to Voevodsky’s spectacular proofs of the Milnor and Bloch-Kato conjectures
[
90
]
,
[
93
]
.
The fact that the affine line parametrizes homotopies restricts the theory to
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy invariant phenomena.
Since there are many nontrivial étale
\mathbb{Z}/p
-coverings of the affine line,
where
p
is the characteristic of the base field
k
,
this restriction limits the reach of the theory.
Indeed,
the derived category
\mathbf{DM}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p)
of étale motives with mod-
p
coefficients is trivial
[
4
]
.

In Voevodsky’s construction of motivic categories, one of the first problems is to find an algebraic avatar for the unit interval
[0,1]\subset\mathbb{R}
in topology.
In our approach to log motives, we encounter the same problem.
One option is to proceed with the affine line
\mathbb{A}^{1}
equipped with the trivial log structure,
see
[
45
]
,
[
77
]
.
There is,
however,
another less obvious choice : in the log geometric setting, it is natural to compactify the affine line,
i.e.,
replace it by the projective line
\mathbb{P}^{1}
pointed at infinity, denoted by
\overline{\Box}=(\mathbb{P}^{1},\{\infty\})
.
A priori this is not a unit interval in the traditional sense since the multiplication map on
\mathbb{A}^{1}
extends only to a rational map

\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}.

This pronounced difference is also noticeable in the theory of algebraic cycles with moduli conditions
[
14
]
.

Let us write
lSm/k
for the category of fine and saturated log schemes that are log smooth and separated over a fixed field
k
equipped with the trivial log structure.
To carry out our constructions, we turn
lSm/k
into a Grothendieck site using the
dividing Nisnevich topology
.
The coverings in this topology arise from distinguished squares of different origins :

dividing squares
,
typically involving a log blow-up or the star subdivision of a monoid,
and
strict Nisnevich squares
,
an adaption of the Nisnevich topology to log geometry.
Conceptually one can view the strict Nisnevich coverings as a witness for the scheme-theoretic data and the dividing coverings as a witness for the log structures.
The properties of these topologies are used throughout the proofs of our results.

Another fundamental ingredient in the theory of log motives is the notion of
finite log correspondences
in
lSm/k
.
With this in hand, we define the additive category
lCor/k
of finite log correspondences over
k
,
having the same objects as
lSm/k
and with morphisms given by finite log correspondences.
There is a faithful embedding
lSm/k\hookrightarrow lCor/k
induced by taking the graph of morphisms in
lSm/k
.
Passing to the underlying schemes yields a functor to the category
Cor/k
of finite correspondences defined by Suslin-Voevodsky
[
94
]
.
Moreover,
the compositions in
lCor/k
and
Cor/k
are compatible in the obvious sense.

Let
\Lambda
be a commutative unital ring.
A
presheaf of
\Lambda
-modules with log transfers
on
lSm/k
is a contravariant additive functor from
lCor/k
to
\Lambda
-modules.
By the linear setup, every presheaf equipped with log transfers determines “wrong way” maps between
\Lambda
-modules parametrized by finite log correspondences.
As in the case of usual schemes, the structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}
and the sheaf of global units
\mathcal{O}^{\times}
are basic examples of presheaves with log transfers.
For any log scheme
(X,\mathcal{M}_{X})
in
lSm/k
the group completion
\mathcal{M}^{gp}
is also a presheaf with log transfers.

Definition 1.1.1
.

Suppose
Z=n_{1}Z_{1}+\cdots+n_{r}Z_{r}
is an effective Cartier divisor on a scheme
X
over
k
.
Let
I_{i}
be the invertible sheaf of ideals defining
n_{i}Z_{i}
for each irreducible component
Z_{i}
.
Then
(X,Z)
is an fs log scheme over
k
whose log structure is given by the Deligne-Faltings structure
[
76
, §III.1.7]
associated to the inclusions
I_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}
,

i=1,\ldots,r
.
In particular,
for the projective line,
we set

\overline{\Box}:=(\mathbb{P}^{1},\{\infty\})\in lSm/k.

(1.1.1)

For most part of this paper we us
e the multiplicity one case
n_{1}=\cdots=n_{r}=1
.
In this case the log structure of
(X,Z)
is the compactifying log structure
[
76
, §III.1.6]
associated to the open immersion
X-Z\rightarrow X
.

Example 1.1.2
.

Following Definition
1.1.1
in the case of a normal strict crossing divisor, we set

\overline{\Box}^{n}=((\mathbb{P}^{1})^{n},\underbrace{\infty\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{P}^{1}}_{n}+\underbrace{\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\infty\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{P}^{1}}_{n}+\underbrace{\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\infty}_{n}).

This is the
n
th self product of
\overline{\Box}
for the standard monoidal structure on
lSm/k
.
The notation means that on
(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{n}
we consider the strict normal crossing divisor given by setting the coordinate
z_{i}=\infty
for
i=1,\ldots n
.

We note there are canonically defined morphisms

(\mathbb{A}^{1},\emptyset)\to\overline{\Box}\to(\mathbb{P}^{1},\emptyset)

between distinctly different objects in
lSm/k
.
We emphasize that
\overline{\Box}
has a nontrivial log structure.

The triangulated category of effective log motives of
k
with
\Lambda
-coefficients

{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)

is obtained from the category of chain complexes of presheaves with log transfers on
lSm/k
by imposing descent for all dividing Nisnevich coverings,
and homotopy invariance with respect to
\overline{\Box}
.
To every fs log scheme
X\in lSm/k
we can associate the motive

M(X)\in{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda).

This construction is our main object of study in this work.

Notwithstanding the technicalities,
the next four statements are straightforward consequences of the construction of
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
.

—

(Monoidal structure) For every
X,Y\in lSm/k
there is a naturally induced isomorphism of log motives

M(X\times Y)\simeq M(X)\otimes M(Y).

—

(Homotopy invariance) For every
X\in lSm/k
there is a naturally induced isomorphism of log motives

M(X\times\overline{\Box})\simeq M(X).

—

(Mayer-Vietoris) For every strict Nisnevich distinguished square in
lSm/k

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

there is a naturally induced homotopy cartesian square of log motives

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(Y^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(X^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(X).}

—

(Log modification)
Every log modification
f:Y\rightarrow X
of fs log schemes log smooth over
k
in the sense of Kato
[
52
]
induces an isomorphism of log motives

M(f)\colon M(Y)\simeq M(X).

To an fs log scheme
X\in lSm/k
and a vector bundle
\xi\colon\mathcal{E}\to X
we associate the Thom motive

MTh_{X}(\mathcal{E})\in{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)

via the blow-up of
\mathcal{E}
along its
0
-section.
We refer to Definitions
5.2.7
,
7.1.2
, and
7.4.3
for precise statements.
The construction of Thom motives turns out to be an important one for several reasons.
One should note that the Betti realization of the log motivic Thom space of
\xi\colon\mathcal{E}\to X
is homotopy equivalent to the quotient of the unit disk bundle by the unit sphere bundle for the Betti realization of
\xi\colon\mathcal{E}\to X
.
In the presence of a Euclidean metric, the latter is one formulation of Thom spaces in topology.

We employ motivic Thom spaces of vector bundles to show a Gysin isomorphism in Theorem
7.5.4
.
A closely related result is the Gysin triangle for strict normal crossing divisors.

—

(Gysin triangle)
Suppose
Z,Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{r}
are smooth divisors forming a strict normal crossing divisor on
X\in Sm/k
,
and set
Y:=(X,Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{r})
.
Let
E
be the exceptional divisor of the blow-up
B_{Z}Y
of
Y
along
Z
, and let
N_{Z}Y
denote the normal bundle of
Z
in
Y
.
Then there is a distinguished triangle

M(B_{Z}Y,E)\rightarrow M(Y)\rightarrow MTh(N_{Z}Y)\rightarrow M(B_{Z}Y,E)[1]

of log motives in
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
.

In Theorem
7.3.3
we show the following blow-up exact triangle.

—

(Blow-up triangle) Let
X
be a smooth scheme over
k
, and let
X^{\prime}
be the blow-up of
X
along a smooth center
Z
.
Then there is a distinguished triangle

M(Z\times_{X}X^{\prime})\rightarrow M(X^{\prime})\oplus M(Z)\rightarrow M(X)\rightarrow M(Z\times_{X}X^{\prime})[1]

of log motives in
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
.

In
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory,
the affine space
\mathbb{A}^{n}
can be viewed as a disk.
Similarly,

\overline{\Box}
in Definition
1.1.1
and more generally
\overline{\Box}^{n}
in Example
1.1.2
can be viewed as a disk in our setting.
For our purposes,
however,
it is also natural to consider the pair of projective spaces
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
,
where
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
is considered as a hyperplane in
\mathbb{P}^{n}
.

In Proposition
7.3.1
we prove the following fundamental invariance for log motives.

—

(
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
-invariance) For every
X\in lSm/k
there is a naturally induced isomorphism of log motives

M(X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1}))\simeq M(X).

Following the convention in
[
89
]
we set

\Lambda(0):=M(\operatorname{Spec}(k))=\Lambda,

and define the Tate object
\Lambda(1)
to be the shifted cone

\Lambda(1):=M(\operatorname{Spec}(k)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{0}}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathbb{P}^{1})[-2]

in
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
.
Here
i_{0}:\operatorname{Spec}(k)\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
is the
0
-section, and both schemes are equipped with a trivial log structure.
Moreover,
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
we form the derived tensor product

\Lambda(n):=\underbrace{\Lambda(1)\otimes\cdots\otimes\Lambda(1)}_{n}.

More generally,
the
n
th Tate twist of an object
M\in{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
is defined as

M(n):=M\otimes\Lambda(n).

In Theorem
8.3.5
we conclude a projective bundle theorem for the projectivization of vector bundles.
We refer the reader to Definition
7.6.3
for the precise meaning of resolution of singularities over fields as in
[
60
]
.

—

(Projective bundle theorem) Assume that
k
admits resolution of singularities.
Let
\mathcal{E}
be a vector bundle of rank
n+1
over
X\in lSm/k
.
In
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
there is a canonical isomorphism

M(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}))\simeq\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n}M(X)(i)[2i].

As a consequence of the projective bundle theorem, we can relate Thom motives to Tate twists via the Thom isomorphism,
see Theorem
8.3.7
.

—

(Thom isomorphism)
Assume that
k
admits resolution of singularities.
Let
\mathcal{E}
be a vector bundle of rank
n
over
X\in lSm/k
.
In
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
, there is a canonical isomorphism

MTh(\mathcal{E})\simeq M(X)(n)[2n].

Associated with any log structure on a scheme, there is a corresponding notion of a boundary enabling us to think about log schemes as analogous to manifolds with boundaries.
In our setting, the boundary corresponds to an effective Cartier divisor on the underlying scheme.

Definition 1.1.3
.

For an fs log scheme
X
over
k
, let
\partial X
denote the set of points of
X
with a nontrivial log structure.
That is,

x\in\partial X
if the stalk
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{X,x}
of the characteristic monoid

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{X}:=\mathcal{M}_{X}/\mathcal{M}_{X}^{\ast}

at
x\in X
is nontrivial.

We note that
\partial X
is a closed subset of
X
according to
[
76
, Proposition III.1.2.8]
so that
X-\partial X
is an open subscheme of
X
.
If
X\in lSm/k
, then
X-\partial X
is smooth over
k
and
\partial X
is an effective Cartier divisor on
X
.
Theorem
7.6.7
shows the following fundamental admissible blow-up property telling us how the motive of
X
depends on the boundary
\partial X
.

—

(Admissible blow-ups) Assume that
k
admits resolution of singularities.
Let
f:Y\rightarrow X
be a proper morphism of fs log schemes that are log smooth over
k
.
If the naturally induced morphism

Y-\partial Y\rightarrow X-\partial X

is an isomorphism of
k
-schemes,
then there is a naturally induced isomorphism

M(Y)\rightarrow M(X)

of log motives in
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
.

In Section
8
we compare
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
with Voevodsky’s triangulated category of effective motives
{\bf DM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
introduced in
[
89
]
and reviewed in
[
35
]
.
For proper smooth schemes, we identify particular hom-objects in
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
and
{\bf DM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
.
More precisely,
Theorem
8.2.11
shows the following useful comparison result.

—

(Comparison with Voevodsky’s derived category of effective motives) Assume that
k
admits resolution of singularities.
Let
X
and
Y
be fs log schemes that are log smooth over
k
.
If
X
is proper over
k
,
then for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is a naturally induced isomorphism of abelian groups

\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)}(M(Y)[i],M(X))\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf DM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)}(M(Y-\partial Y)[i],M(X-\partial X)).

As an immediate application we can identify the endomorphism ring of the unit object in
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
with the coefficients

\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)}(\Lambda(0),\Lambda(0))\simeq\Lambda.

(1.1.2)

Assuming
k
admits resolution of singularities, we show there is an adjoint functor pair

\omega_{\sharp}:{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)\rightleftarrows{\bf DM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda):R\omega^{*}.

Theorem
8.2.13
shows that the right adjoint functor
R\omega^{*}
is fully faithful.
However,
as witnessed by the sheaf of logarithmic differentials
\Omega^{i}_{/k}
,
the functor
R\omega^{*}
is not essentially surjective.

While
{\bf DM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
admits a fully faithful embedding into
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
,
the latter also contains interesting invariants that are not
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant.
The latter is one way of justifying our choice of
\overline{\Box}
as the preferred unit interval in
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
.

To identify the essential image of
R\omega^{*}
we follow
[
89
]
and declare that

\mathcal{F}\in{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)

is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-local
if for every
X\in lSm/k
and
i\in\mathbb{Z}
the projection
X\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\rightarrow X
induces an isomorphism of abelian groups

\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)}(M(X)[i],\mathcal{F})\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)}(M(X\times\mathbb{A}^{1})[i],\mathcal{F}).

Theorem
8.2.16
shows that every
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-local effective log motive is in the essential image of
R\omega^{*}
.
That is,
Voevodsky’s category of derived motives
{\bf DM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
is equivalent to the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-localization
of
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
.

Let
{\bf logDM}_{\rm prop}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
be the smallest triangulated subcategory of
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
that is closed under small sums and contains every motive
M(X)
,
where
X\in lSm/k
is proper as a scheme over
\operatorname{Spec}(k)
.
We identity
{\bf logDM}_{\rm prop}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
with the essential image of
R\omega^{*}
under the assumption that
k
admits resolution of singularities.
This shows there is an equivalence of triangulated categories

{\bf logDM}_{\rm prop}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)\simeq{\bf DM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda),

(1.1.3)

which gives another log geometric description of Voevodsky’s category
{\bf DM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
. Note that the equivalence (
)
does not hold
with integral coefficients if we replace the dividing Nisnevich topology with its étale counterpart. See Remark
5.3.6
and
1.3.2
below for a discussion about this.

1.2.
Outline of the paper

In what follows, we give a more detailed overview of our work.

1.2.1.
Correspondences on logarithmic schemes

In Section
2
we introduce the notion of
finite log correspondences
.
Our approach is fairly classical :
informally,
an elementary finite log correspondence
Z
from
X
to
Y
(smooth log schemes over
k
) is the datum of an elementary finite correspondence in the sense of Suslin-Voevodsky
between the underlying subschemes
\underline{X}
and
\underline{Y}
that is equipped with the minimal log structure necessary to ensure the projection
Z\to X
is a strict morphism.
However,
to get a morphism to the product
X\times Y
in the category of log schemes, we require the existence of a morphism from the normalization
Z^{N}
to
Y
as part of the data.
The said condition on the normalization of an elementary finite log correspondence is reminiscent of the modulus condition on correspondences discussed in
[
49
]
.
We work with the normalizations to establish a well-defined associative composition of finite log correspondences and turn
lCor/k
into a category.
In turn,
this requires a certain amount of technicalities involving solid log schemes ;
a notion with strong permanence inherited from
X
and
Y
by the log structure on the normalization
Z^{N}
of the elementary finite log correspondence.
We refer the reader to Section
2
(more specifically Definition
2.1.1
) for details.

1.2.2.
cd structures on log schemes and sheaves with log transfers

Suppose
X
is a log scheme with log structure given by an open immersion
j\colon U\to X
corresponding to the complement of an effective Cartier divisor
\partial X
.
Then the motive of
X
is expected to help us understand the cohomology of
U
.
To achieve this, we need to impose a suitable invariance of the motives under the choice of the “compactification”
X
.
The solution we offer involves the notion of
a dividing cover
,
i.e.,
a surjective proper log étale monomorphisms
f\colon Y\to X
(see Definition
3.1.4
and
[
78
]
).

Intuitively, we can think of such morphisms as blow-ups with their center in the boundary
\partial X
.
We also consider strict Nisnevich covers,
i.e.,
Nisnevich covers coming from the underlying schemes,
These two types of coverings conspire into a cd-structure in the sense of
[
91
]
,
and we call the associated topology the
dividing Nisnevich topology
.
As discussed in Section
3
, the dividing Nisnevich cd-structure is not bounded.
For this reason, we need to generalize Voevodsky’s results to
quasi-bounded density structures
,
which is the central technical notion of the section (see Definition
3.3.22
).
Theorem
3.4.6
proves that for any quasi-bounded, regular, and complete cd structures on a category
\mathcal{C}
the associated cohomology groups vanish above the

density dimension
of any object.
Corollary
3.4.11
allows us to define a suitable descent structure in the sense of
[
19
]
.

In Section
4
we study how finite log correspondences relate to topologies.
Our main findings can be summarized in the following combination of Proposition
4.2.12
, Proposition
4.5.4
, Proposition
4.3.2
and Theorem
4.5.7

(see below for a reminder on Kummer étale maps).

Theorem 1.2.1
.

Let
t
be one of the following topologies on
lSm/k
: strict or dividing Nisnevich, strict or dividing étale, Kummer étale, or log étale.

(i)

The topology
t
is compatible with log transfers on
lSm/k
.

(ii)

The category
\mathbf{Shv}_{t}^{\rm ltr}(k,\Lambda)
is a Grothendieck abelian category.

(iii)

For every complex of
t
-sheaves
\mathcal{F}
with log transfers and every
X\in lSm/k
,
there is a canonical isomorphism

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{Shv}^{\mathrm{ltr}}_{t}(k,\Lambda))}(a_{t}^{*}\Lambda_{\mathrm{ltr}}(X),\mathcal{F}[i])\simeq\mathbf{H}_{t}^{i}(X,\mathcal{F}).

The dividing topology on
lSm/k
is not subcanonical (like the
h
-topology on
Sm/k
).
However,
there is an algebraic cycle description of the sections of the sheafification of the representable presheaf
a_{t}^{*}\Lambda_{\mathrm{ltr}}(X)
of any
X
(here
t
is one of the dividing topologies on
lSm/k
).
To that end,
we introduce the notion of
dividing log correspondences
between fs log schemes in Definition
4.6.1
,
which up to log modifications can be viewed as finite log correspondences in the sense of Definition
2.1.1
.
In fact,
it turns out that the resulting categories of sheaves are equivalent ; see Proposition
4.6.6
.

Dividing log correspondences allow us to further restrict the class of log schemes that is needed to build
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
.
For
t=dNis
,
d\acute{e}t
,
l\acute{e}t
, the results in Section
4.7
give an equivalence of categories

\iota^{*}\colon\mathbf{Shv}_{t}^{\rm ltr}(k,\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathbf{Shv}_{t}^{\rm ltr}(SmlSm/k,\Lambda).

(1.2.1)

Here
\iota\colon SmlSm/k\hookrightarrow lSm/k
is the full subcategory of
lSm/k
consisting of fs log schemes of the form
(X,\partial X)
,
where
X
is smooth over
k
and
\partial X
is a strict normal crossing divisor on
X
(see Lemma
A.5.10
).
This gives a way of computing dividing cohomology groups ; see Theorem
5.1.8
.

Theorem 1.2.2
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a bounded below complex of strict Nisnevich (resp. strict étale, resp. Kummer étale) sheaves on
SmlSm/k
.
Then for every
X\in SmlSm/k
and integer
i\in\mathbb{Z}
there is an isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{dNis}^{i}(X,a_{dNis}^{*}\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathop{\rm colim}_{Y\in X_{div}^{Sm}}\mathbf{H}_{sNis}^{i}(Y,\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\text{(resp.\ }\mathbf{H}_{d\acute{e}t}^{i}(X,a_{d\acute{e}t}^{*}\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathop{\rm colim}_{Y\in X_{div}^{Sm}}\mathbf{H}_{s\acute{e}t}^{i}(Y,\mathcal{F}),

\displaystyle\text{resp.\ }\mathbf{H}_{l\acute{e}t}^{i}(X,a_{l\acute{e}t}^{*}\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathop{\rm colim}_{Y\in X_{div}^{Sm}}\mathbf{H}_{k\acute{e}t}^{i}(Y,\mathcal{F})\text{)}.

We remark that the equivalence (
1.2.1
) provides a canonical extension to
lSm/k
of any sheaf with log transfers a priori defined on
SmlSm/k
.

1.2.3.
Motivic categories and properties of motives

Defining
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
is straightforward at this point.
The local objects for the
\overline{\Box}
-descent model structure are precisely the complexes of strictly
\overline{\Box}
-invariant
t
-sheaves with log transfer (especially for
t=dNis
),
and the properties established in Sections
3
-
5
automatically give the first set of axioms
(
\overline{\Box}
-invariance, Mayer-Vietoris, log modification invariance).
We present next a leitfaden for the less transparent properties.

First, we extend the log modification invariance of motives to blow-ups along smooth centers contained in
\partial X
.
This is the content of Section
7.2
,
and it is required for the following reason.
Like any other smooth curve,
the affine line
\mathbb{A}^{1}
affords a unique compactification to an object in
lSm/k
, namely
\overline{\Box}
.
In higher dimensions this construction is no longer canonical.
For example,
in dimension
2
,
the product
\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}=\mathbb{A}^{2}
can be compactified to
\overline{\Box}\times\overline{\Box}=\overline{\Box}^{2}
, or to
(\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathbb{P}^{1})
, where the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
is viewed as a line at infinity.

The construction of
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
makes the first choice automatically contractible, but from the construction, it is unclear what happens to
(\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathbb{P}^{1})
.
Inspired by the properties of log differentials observed in
[
14
, §6.2]
,
see also Section
9.2
,
it is reasonable to expect that
(\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathbb{P}^{1})
is contractible too.
In order to compare
\overline{\Box}^{2}
and
(\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathbb{P}^{1})
,
we consider the blow-up
X
of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
at a point contained in its boundary,
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
This latter object can more easily be compared with the blow-up of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
at the point
(\infty,\infty)
.
The map
X\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
, however, is not a log modification.
Our crucial observation is that a combination of Mayer-Vietoris,
\overline{\Box}
-invariance, and dividing-invariance properties are, in fact, enough to establish that this map is an equivalence in
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
.
See Theorem
7.2.10
and Theorem
7.3.3
for precise statements.
A key point of the proof is to deal with the case of surfaces ; see Proposition
7.2.5
.
This finally allow us to prove the
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
-invariance property of motives and also the blow-up formula in Proposition
7.3.1
and Theorem
7.3.3
,
respectively.

Section
7.4
discusses Thom motives,
i.e.,
the motives of Thom spaces of vector bundles
\mathcal{E}
in the logarithmic setting.
To get the correct homotopy type, the definition we use involves the blow-up of
\mathcal{E}
along the zero section (and it is therefore quite different from the corresponding notion in Voevodsky’s category).
The critical technical result here is Proposition
7.4.15
, in which we show the existence of a canonical isomorphism

MTh_{X_{1}}(\mathcal{E}_{1})\otimes MTh_{X_{2}}(\mathcal{E}_{2})\rightarrow MTh_{X_{1}\times_{S}X_{2}}(\mathcal{E}_{1}\times_{S}\mathcal{E}_{2}).

for vector bundles
\mathcal{E}_{i}
on
X_{i}
.
This part is quite involved in the log setting compared to the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant setting.

Having Thom motives at our disposal, we finally prove the Gysin isomorphism and the existence of Gysin triangles in Theorem
7.5.4
.
Assuming resolution of singularities, we show an even finer invariance under admissible blow-ups in Section
7.6
.
Our comparison with Voevodsky’s category of motives uses the latter, from which we deduce the projective bundle formula in Theorem
8.3.5
as well as the Thom isomorphism Theorem
8.3.7
.

In Section
6
we review the construction of a
Sing
functor,
inspired by the Suslin complex,
for a symmetric monoidal category
\mathcal{A}
equipped with a weak interval object
I
.
This seeming detour is necessary since
\overline{\Box}
is not an interval in the sense of
[
68
]
for
lCor/k
:
the graph of the multiplication map
\mu\colon\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
does not extend to an admissible correspondence in our sense.

Our solution,
see also
[
9
, §4]
,
is to enlarge the category using calculus of fractions
\mathcal{A}[T^{-1}]
for a suitable class of maps
T
to include the multiplication for
I
,
build the singular functor there,
and then restrict back to the original category.
Applied to the category of chain complexes
\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{Psh}^{\rm ltr}(k))
,
the said construction produces a
\overline{\Box}
-local object,
albeit not very computationally-friendly.
Nevertheless, it plays a key role in the comparison between
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
and
{\bf DM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
, see Propositions
8.2.3
and
8.2.4
.

By ignoring transfer structures altogether, we present a simpler construction
\mathbf{logDA}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
that is a direct analogue of Ayoub’s
\mathbf{DA}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)

(see
[
3
]
or
[
4
]
for a short overview).
However,
using correspondences allows us to prove much stronger results, including the comparison with Voevodsky’s
{\bf DM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
and the existence of Gysin triangles.

1.2.4.
Applications : Hodge sheaves and cyclic homology

Prototype examples of objects that are not available in Voevodsky’s derived category of motives are the additive group scheme
\mathbb{G}_{a}
and the differentials
\Omega^{i}_{-/k}

(or their absolute counterpart).
We show in Section
9
that this problem disappears in the log setting (see Corollary
9.2.8
, Theorem
9.6.4
).

Theorem 1.2.3
.

Suppose
k
is a perfect field and
X\in lSm/k
.
Let
t
be one of the topologies on log schemes in Theorem
1.2.1
or the Zariski topology.
Then the assignment

X\mapsto\Omega^{j}_{X/k}

extends to a
t
-sheaf with log transfers.
Moreover, for every
i
,
j
,
n\geq 0
,
there is an isomorphism

H_{t}^{i}(X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1}),\Omega_{X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})}^{j})\simeq H_{t}^{i}(X,\Omega_{X}^{j}).

In particular, the sheaves
\Omega^{j}_{-/k}
are strictly
\overline{\Box}
-invariant
t
-sheaves with log transfers.

The transfer structure is obtained by extending Rülling-Chatzistamatiou’s work
[
17
]
to the logarithmic setting.
By extending from
SmlSm/k
to
lSm/k
via Construction
5.4.3
,
we conclude there exists a natural isomorphism

\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)}(M(X),\Omega^{j}_{-/k}[i])\simeq H^{i}_{Zar}(\underline{X},\Omega^{j}_{X/k}).

(1.2.2)

Representing Hodge cohomology in
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
refines the analogous result for de Rham cohomology in
\mathbf{DM}^{\rm eff}(k)
; see
[
61
]
.

When the characteristic of the base field
k
is zero,
combining (
1.2.2
) with the Hochschild-Kostant-Rosenberg theorem yields an isomorphism for every
X\in Sm/k

HC_{n}(X)\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathbf{logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\mathbb{Q})}}(M(X),\bigoplus_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}\Omega^{\leq 2p}_{-/k}[2p-n]).

This shows representability of cyclic homology in
{\mathbf{logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\mathbb{Q})}
,
see Section
9.7
,
an example of a non-
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant theory on
Sm/k
and thus out of reach of
\mathbf{DM}^{\rm eff}(k)
.
We plan to address the question of representing the cohomology of Hodge-Witt sheaves in characteristic
p>0
in later work.

1.2.5.
Appendices

Besides reminding the reader of some necessary background material in log geometry, the main aim of Appendix A is to minimize confusion in other chapters.
Appendix A can be consulted if the reader doubts notation or the precise form of basic log geometric notions.
Appendix B briefly introduces model structures on chain complexes and thereby introduces notation employed elsewhere in the text.

1.3.
Outlook and future developments

We conclude the introduction with some remarks and conjectures that put our theory in a broader framework.

1.3.1.
Relationship with reciprocity sheaves

The idea of extending Voevodsky’s framework to encompass non-
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant phenomena have been investigated by many authors in the past decade,
starting with the work of Bloch-Esnault
[
16
]
,
[
15
]
on additive Chow groups and Chow groups with modulus.

The theory of
reciprocity sheaves
by Kahn-Saito-Yamazaki
[
51
]
,
and the closely related theory of
modulus sheaves
by Kahn-Miyazaki-Saito-Yamazaki
[
49
]
,
[
50
]
,
has provided a unified framework for both
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant objects as well as interesting non-
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant ones.
The category
\mathbf{RSC}_{\rm Nis}
of (Nisnevich) reciprocity sheaves is a full subcategory of
\mathbf{Shv}^{\rm tr}_{Nis}(k,\mathbb{Z})
that contains
\mathbf{HI}_{\rm Nis}
,
i.e.,

\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant Nisnevich sheaves with transfers but also the additive group scheme
\mathbb{G}_{a}
,
the sheaf of absolute Kähler differentials
\Omega^{i}
,
and the de Rham-Witt sheaves
W_{m}\Omega^{i}
.
The connection between
\mathbf{RSC}_{\rm Nis}
and our theory has recently been provided by Saito
[
82
]
.

Theorem 1.3.1

(Saito)
.

There exists a fully faithful exact functor

\mathcal{L}og\colon\mathbf{RSC}_{\rm Nis}\to\mathbf{Shv}_{dNis}^{\rm ltr}(k,\mathbb{Z})

such that
\mathcal{L}og(F)
is strictly
\overline{\Box}
-invariant for every
F\in\mathbf{RSC}_{\rm Nis}
.
Moreover, for each
X\in Sm/k
, there is a natural isomorphism

H^{i}_{Nis}(X,F_{X})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)}(M(X),\mathcal{L}og(F)[i]).

The isomorphism shows that Nisnevich cohomology of reciprocity sheaves is representable in
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
,
giving log motivic interpretations (at least for reduced modulus) of properties such as the Gysin exact sequence,
the projective bundle formula (assuming resolution of singularities),
the blow-up formula,
and the
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
-invariance of the cohomology of reciprocity sheaves provided in
[
13
]
.
This applies to
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
-invariance and the existence of pushforward in Hodge cohomology ; see Section
9
.
At least for
X\in SmlSm/k
, this is a consequence of Theorem
1.3.1
, and the corresponding formulas in
[
13
]
.
We view this as an essential step in connecting the theory of motives with modulus to logarithmic motives.

A different incarnation of the category of reciprocity sheaves (again assuming resolution of singularities) have been recently introduced in
[
11
]
. Thanks to Saito’s theorem, together with a purity theorem for
\overline{\Box}
-local complexes of sheaves with log transfers
[
11
, Thm. 5.10]
, the category of log-reciprocity sheaves contains the category
\mathbf{RSC}_{\rm Nis}
as a full subcategory.

This is in turn essential to develop an analogue of the theory of higher Albanese sheaves of Ayoub, Barbieri-Viale and Kahn, see
[
12
]
, thanks to the existence of a
homotopy
t
-structure
(analogue of the Morel-Voevodsky
t
-structure) on the category of log motives by
[
11
, Thm. 5.7]
.

1.3.2.
Conjectures and speculations

Suppose that
\Lambda
is an
N
-torsion ring,
where
N>0
is coprime to the exponential characteristic of the ground field
k
.
Voevodsky proved the following version of Suslin’s rigidity theorem for the small étale site of
k
, see
[
64
, Theorem 9.35]
.

Theorem 1.3.2
.

The morphism of sites
\pi\colon k_{\acute{e}t}\to(Sm/k)_{\acute{e}t}
induces an equivalence

\pi_{\sharp}\colon\mathbf{D}(k_{\acute{e}t},\Lambda)\rightarrow{\bf DM}_{\acute{e}t}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda).

There are many other instances of rigidity theorems comparing small and big étale sites in the motivic context.
See
[
5
]
for
\mathbf{DA}_{\acute{e}t}(S,\Lambda)
,

[
20
]
for motives with transfer,
and
[
7
]
for the rigid analytic setting.

Recall that a morphism of fs log schemes
f\colon X\to Y
is called Kummer étale if it is exact and log étale,
see Proposition
A.8.4
.
A typical example is given by
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
,
t\mapsto t^{n}
,
where
n\geq 1
and
(n,{\rm char}(k))=1
.
Here
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
is endowed with the log structure given by the origin, written
\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{N}}=\operatorname{Spec}(k[\mathbb{N}])
in the following.
Proposition
8.5.6
shows that every locally constant log étale sheaf on
lSm/k
,
see Definition
8.5.1
,
admits a unique transfer structure.
This allow us to define a functor

\eta_{\sharp}\colon\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{Shv}(k_{\acute{e}t},\Lambda))\rightarrow{\bf logDM}_{l\acute{e}t}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda),

(1.3.1)

which is easily seen to be fully faithful.
On the right-hand side we consider the log étale topology,
i.e.,
the smallest Grothendieck topology finer than the Kummer étale topology and the dividing topology
(on the left-hand side there are no non-trivial dividing covers on the small site, so it makes no difference).
Conjecture
8.5.8
is an analog of the Suslin-Voevodsky rigidity theorem.

Conjecture 1.3.3
.

The functor (
) is an equivalence.

More generally,
if
\Lambda
is a torsion ring of exponent invertible in
k
,
we expect there are equivalences

{\bf logDM}_{d\acute{e}t}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)\leftarrow{\bf logDM}_{d\acute{e}t,prop}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)\rightarrow{\bf DM}_{\acute{e}t}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda).

Conjecture
8.6.3
predicts a similar result in the log étale topology.
This expectation is justified by examples of strictly
\overline{\Box}
-invariant complexes of sheaves that are not strictly
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant,
such as
\Omega^{i}_{-}
and
W_{m}\Omega^{i}_{-}
,
are nullified under our assumption on
\Lambda
.
This is similar in spirit to
[
10
, Theorem 3.5]
and
[
67
]
.

Let us discuss the case when the ground field
k
has positive characteristic
p>0
.
Then the category
\mathbf{DM}_{\acute{e}t}(k,\mathbb{Z})
of étale motives has trivial
p
-torsion and is in fact a
\mathbb{Z}[1/p]
-linear category.
On the other hand,
the étale (or Lichtenbaum) motivic cohomology groups with
\mathbb{Z}/p^{r}
-coefficients are not
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant,
so they cannot be hom-groups in one of Voevodsky’s categories.

Currently, there is no interpretation of the category of
integral
étale motivic complexes in terms of algebraic cycles.
Milne-Ramachandran
[
65
]
proposed a construction of
\mathbf{DM}^{\rm gm}_{\acute{e}t}(k,\mathbb{Z})
as a dg-pullback of
\mathbf{DM}_{\acute{e}t}(k,\mathbb{Z}[1/p])

and
\mathbf{D}^{b}_{c}(R)
,
the derived category of coherent complexes of graded modules over the Raynaud ring
R
, along the rigid realisation functor.
The category of étale
log
motives constructed in this work can be a candidate for a cycle-theoretic incarnation of Milne-Ramachandran’s category

\mathbf{DM}^{\rm gm}_{\acute{e}t}(k,\mathbb{Z})
.
To support this belief,
note that
\mathbf{logDM}_{d\acute{e}t,prop}^{\rm eff}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p^{r})
is nontrivial because
\mathbb{Z}/p\not\simeq 0
,
while its
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant counterpart is trivial because of the Artin-Schreier sequence.
It would be interesting to compare
\mathbf{logDM}_{d\acute{e}t,prop}^{\rm eff}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p^{r})
with
\mathbf{D}^{b}_{c}(R,\mathbb{Z}/p^{r})
via a log-crystalline realisation functor.

1.4.
Notations and terminology

Every log scheme in this paper is equipped with a Zariski log structure unless otherwise stated.
Recall that for every fs log scheme
X
with an étale log structure,
there exists a log blow-up
Y\rightarrow X
such that
Y
has a Zariski log structure thanks to work by Niziol
[
73
, Theorem 5.10]
.
Since motives are invariant under log blow-ups,
this shows that the restriction to fs log schemes with Zariski log structures is no loss of generality.

We employ the following notation (see the Index for a more comprehensive list).

k

(perfect) field

Sm/S

category of smooth and separated schemes of finite type over

a scheme
S

lSm/S

category of log smooth and separated fs log schemes of finite

type over an fs log scheme
S

lSch/S

category of separated fs log schemes of finite type over an fs

log scheme
S

lCor/k

category of finite log correspondences over
k

SmlSm/S

the full subcategory of
lSm/S
consisting of
X
such that
\underline{X}
is

smooth over
\underline{S}

\Lambda

commutative unital ring

\Lambda\text{-}\mathbf{Mod}

category of
\Lambda
-modules

\mathcal{A}

abelian category

\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{A})

category of unbounded chain complexes of
\mathcal{A}

{\bf K}(\mathcal{A})

\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{A})
modulo chain homotopy equivalences

\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{A})

derived category of
\mathcal{A}

2.
Finite logarithmic correspondences

2.1.
Definition of finite log correspondences

This section introduces the notion of finite log correspondences relative to any base field
k
equipped with its trivial log structure.

1
1

1

An alternate notion of finite correspondences between log schemes, free of any normalization constraints, was considered by K. Rülling in
[
81
]
.
We thank him for sharing his note with us.

Our approach is an adaptation to the log setting of the original definition of finite correspondences due to Suslin and Voevodsky
[
94
]
,
[
64
]
.
As for usual schemes, one can intuitively regard finite log correspondences as multivalued functions, but some subtleties are arising involving the log structure.
Building on this concept, we turn
lSm/k
into an additive category
lCor/k
of finite log correspondences over
k
.
The objects of
lCor/k
are fine and saturated log schemes that are log smooth over
k
,
i.e.,
the same objects as in
lSm/k
.
As morphisms, we take the free abelian group generated by elementary log correspondences (see
[
64
, Lecture 1]
).

If
X\in lSm/k
we write
\underline{X}
for the corresponding underlying scheme.

Definition 2.1.1
.

For
X,Y\in lSm/k
, an
elementary log correspondence

Z
from
X
to
Y
consists of

(i)

an integral closed subscheme
\underline{Z}
of
\underline{X}\times\underline{Y}
that is finite and surjective over a connected component of
\underline{X}
, and

(ii)

a morphism
Z^{N}\rightarrow Y
of fs log schemes,
where
Z^{N}
denotes the fs log scheme whose underlying scheme is the normalization of
\underline{Z}
and whose log structure is induced by the one on
X
.
More precisely, if
p:\underline{Z^{N}}\rightarrow\underline{X}
denotes the induced scheme morphism, then the log structure
\mathcal{M}_{Z^{N}}
is given as
p_{log}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{X}
.

A
finite log correspondence
from
X
to
Y
is a formal sum
\Sigma n_{i}Z_{i}
of elementary log correspondences
Z_{i}
from
X
to
Y
.
Let
\operatorname{lCor}_{k}(X,Y)
denote the free abelian group of finite log correspondences from
X
to
Y
.

The above definitions coincide with the ones in
[
94
]
and
[
64
]
when
X
and
Y
have trivial log structures.
We shall omit the subscript
k
and write
\operatorname{lCor}(X,Y)
when no confusion seems likely to arise.
Note that if
X=\amalg_{i\in I}X_{i}
is a decomposition into connected components,
then there is a direct sum decomposition

\operatorname{lCor}(X,Y)=\bigoplus_{i\in I}\operatorname{lCor}(X_{i},Y).

Remark 2.1.2
.

(i)

With reference to Definition
2.1.1
,
let
p:\underline{Z^{N}}\rightarrow\underline{X}
and
q:\underline{Z^{N}}\rightarrow\underline{Y}
denote the induced scheme morphisms.
Then condition (ii) is equivalent to the existence of a morphism of log structures

q_{log}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{Y}\rightarrow p_{log}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{X}

on the underlying scheme
\underline{Z^{N}}
(see Definition
A.2.4
).
Indeed, to give a morphism
Z^{N}\rightarrow Y
, we only need to specify a morphism
q_{log}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{Z^{N}}
.
We have that
\mathcal{M}_{Z^{N}}=p_{log}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{X}
.

(ii)

Our notion of an elementary log correspondence involves considering a log structure on the normalization of the underlying elementary correspondence between the underlying schemes.
The reason for this extra complication will be explained below in the construction of compositions of finite log correspondences.

(iii)

Let
V\in\operatorname{lCor}(X,Y)
be an elementary log correspondence.
Then condition (ii) in Definition
2.1.1
implies the inclusion
V\cap((X-\partial X)\times Y)\subset V\cap(X\times(Y-\partial Y))
.

(iv)

Suppose
F/k
is a finite separable field extension.
Then for every
U\in lSm/F
, we have
U\times_{k}Y\simeq U\times_{F}Y_{F}
, where
Y_{F}:=\operatorname{Spec}(F)\times_{k}Y
.
Hence there is a base change isomorphism

\operatorname{lCor}_{k}(U,X)\simeq\operatorname{lCor}_{F}(U,X_{F}).

Example 2.1.3
.

(1)

Suppose
X
has a trivial log structure.
Then
Z^{N}
has a trivial log structure, and
Z^{N}\rightarrow Y
factors through
Y-\partial Y
.
Hence there is an isomorphism of abelian groups

\operatorname{lCor}(X,Y)\simeq\operatorname{Cor}(X,Y-\partial Y).

In particular,

\operatorname{lCor}(\operatorname{Spec}(k),Y)
can be identified with the group of
0
-cycles on the open complement
Y-\partial Y
.

(2)

If
Y
has a trivial log structure, then the condition (ii) is automatically satisfied.
It follows that
\operatorname{lCor}(X,Y)\simeq\operatorname{Cor}(\underline{X},Y)
.
In particular,
\operatorname{lCor}(X,\operatorname{Spec}(k))
is the free group generated by the irreducible components of
X
.

(3)

By combining (1) and (2) we see that if
X
and
Y
have trivial log structures, then
\operatorname{lCor}(X,Y)\simeq\operatorname{Cor}(X,Y)
.

(4)

Let
i:Z\rightarrow X\times Y
be a closed immersion of fs log schemes,
i.e.,

\underline{Z}
is a closed subscheme of
\underline{X}\times\underline{Y}
and the induced homomorphism
i_{log}^{*}(\mathcal{M}_{X\times Y})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{Z}
is surjective.
In addition,
assume that
Z
is strict (i.e.,
p_{log}^{*}(\mathcal{M}_{X})\simeq\mathcal{M}_{Z}
, where
p
is the projection from
X\times Y
onto
X
), finite, and surjective over a component of
X
.
Then the induced morphism
Z\rightarrow Y
gives rise to a morphism
Z^{N}\rightarrow Y
.
Hence we can consider
Z
as a finite log correspondence from
X
to
Y
.

The reader might object that our definition of log correspondence is too restrictive since we require the underlying subscheme
\underline{Z}
of any elementary log correspondence to be finite over
\underline{X}

(and not, for example, only over
X-\partial X
, cf.
[
49
]
). We “correct” this by introducing a suitable topology on log schemes, the dividing topology.
By sheafifying the representable presheaf
\operatorname{lCor}(-,X)
for the dividing topology,
we can consider correspondences that are finite over
X-\partial X
and become finite globally after a suitable log modification.
This will be discussed in Section
4.6
.

2.2.
Solid log schemes

For the notion of finite log correspondences to have any meaning, we need to define a sensible composition of finite log correspondences
V\in\operatorname{lCor}(X,Y)
and
W\in\operatorname{lCor}(Y,Z)
, where
X,Y,Z\in lSm/k
.
To that end, it turns out that we need to construct log structures on normalizations of cycles on
\underline{X}\times\underline{Z}
.
This can be achieved using cycles on
\underline{X}\times\underline{Y}\times\underline{Z}
provided that
X
,
Y
, and
Z
are solid log schemes,
as defined below.

Definition 2.2.1
.

Let
X
be a coherent log scheme over
k
.
We say that
X
is
solid
if for any point
x\in X
,
the induced map

\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{X,x})\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{M}_{X,x})

is surjective.

According to
[
76
, Proposition III.1.10.8]
this definition of solidness is equivalent to the one given in
[
76
, Definition III.1.10.1(2)]
.

Lemma 2.2.2
.

Let
f:Y\rightarrow X
be a strict open morphism of coherent log schemes. If
X
is solid, then
Y
is solid.

Démonstration.

Consider the induced commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{Y,y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{M}_{Y,y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{X,x})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{M}_{X,x})}

of topological spaces where
y\in Y
is a point and
x:=f(y)
.
The lower horizontal arrow is surjective since
X
is solid.
The left vertical arrow is surjective by
[
30
, IV.1.10.3]
since
f
is open.
Moreover,
the right vertical arrow is an isomorphism since
f
is strict.
Thus the upper horizontal arrow is surjective.
∎

Lemma 2.2.3
.

Let
X
and
Y
be log schemes, and let
g:\underline{X}\rightarrow\underline{Y}
be a morphism of schemes.
If the log structure homomorphisms
\alpha_{X}:\mathcal{M}_{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}
and
\alpha_{Y}:\mathcal{M}_{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{Y}
are injective,
then there is at most one morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
of log schemes such that
\underline{f}=g
.

Démonstration.

Such a morphism
f
consists of
\underline{f}:\underline{X}\rightarrow\underline{Y}
and a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{f}}_{*}\mathcal{M}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{f}}_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{f}}_{*}\alpha_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{f}}^{\sharp}}

of sheaves of monoids on
X
.
The vertical arrows are injective by assumption.
Thus there are at most one possible
f^{\flat}
if
\underline{f}=g
.
∎

Lemma 2.2.4
.

Let
\theta:P\rightarrow Q
be an exact homomorphism of integral monoids.
If
\theta^{\rm gp}
is an isomorphism, then
\theta
is an isomorphism.

Démonstration.

Since
\theta
is exact, the induced monoid homomorphism
P\rightarrow P^{\rm gp}\times_{Q^{\rm gp}}Q
is an isomorphism.
Thus
\theta
is an isomorphism since
\theta^{\rm gp}
is an isomorphism.
∎

Lemma 2.2.5
.

Suppose that
X
is a solid fs log scheme.
Then the log structure homomorphism
\alpha_{X}:\mathcal{M}_{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}
is injective.

Démonstration.

Assume for contradiction that
\alpha_{X}
is not injective.
Then there exists a point
x
of
X
such that
\alpha_{X,x}:\mathcal{M}_{X,x}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
is not injective.
We choose different elements
p
and
p^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{M}_{X,x}
such that
\alpha_{X,x}(p)=\alpha_{X,x}(p^{\prime})
.
Let
P
be the submonoid of
\mathcal{M}_{X,x}^{\rm gp}
generated by
\mathcal{M}_{X,x}
,
p-p^{\prime}
, and
p^{\prime}-p
.
Note that
\alpha_{X,x}
factors through
P
.

Since
X
is solid,
the induced map
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{X,x})\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{M}_{X,x})
is surjective.
Thus the induced map
\operatorname{Spec}(P)\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{M}_{X,x})
is surjective.
Then by
[
76
, Proposition I.4.2.2]
, the inclusion
\mathcal{M}_{X,x}\rightarrow P
is exact,
which is an isomorphism by Lemma
2.2.4
.
This contradicts the assumption that
p
and
p^{\prime}
are different elements.
∎

Lemma 2.2.6
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
.
If an element
x
of the multiplicative group of units
K^{*}
has order
r
, then
p
does not divide
r
.

Démonstration.

If
r=pr^{\prime}
, then
(x^{r^{\prime}}-1)^{p}=0
and hence
x^{r^{\prime}}=1
.
This contradicts the assumption that
x
has order
r
.
∎

Lemma 2.2.7
.

Let
X
be an fs log scheme log smooth over
k
. Then
X
is solid.

Démonstration.

Since
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{X,x}^{\mathrm{gp}}
is torsion free for every point
x\in X
by
[
76
, Proposition I.1.3.5(2)]
, the torsion subgroups of
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}^{*}
and
\mathcal{M}_{X,x}^{\mathrm{gp}}
are isomorphic.
The question is Zariski local on
X
,
so we may assume that
X
has a neat fs chart
f:X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{P}
at a point
x\in X
such that
f
is smooth by
[
76
, Theorem IV.3.3.1]
and Lemma
2.2.6
.
Then
f
is an open dominant morphism.
Let
F
be a face of
P
,
and let
Y
be the corresponding strict closed subscheme of
\mathbb{A}_{P}
.
Then
f(X)\cap Y
is a dense open subset of
Y
, so the image of
f
contains the generic point
y
of
Y
.

The prime ideal
P-F
of
P
is equal to
g(y)
for the induced map
g:\mathbb{A}_{P}\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}(P)
.
Since
y
is also in the image of
f
,
P-F
is in the image of the composite
gf
.
It follows that
gf
is surjective.
∎

Lemma 2.2.8
.

Let
X
be an fs log scheme log smooth over
k
. Then
X
is normal.

Démonstration.

The question is Zariski local on
X
, so we may assume that
X
has an fs chart
f:X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{P}
such that
f
is étale by
[
76
, Theorem IV.3.3.1]
.
Since
\mathbb{A}_{P}
is normal by
[
76
, Proposition I.3.4.1(2)]
,
X
is normal due to
[
30
, IV.11.3.13]
.
∎

Lemma 2.2.9
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a strict finite and surjective morphism of fs log schemes. If
Y
is log smooth over
k
, then
X
is solid.

Démonstration.

According to Lemmas
2.2.2
and
2.2.7
, it suffices to show that
f
is an open morphism.
Note that
f
is equidimensional by assumption.
Since
Y
is normal by Lemma
2.2.8
,
f
is universally open by Chevalley’s criterion (
[
30
, IV.14.4.4]
).
∎

Lemma 2.2.10
.

Let
X
be an fs log scheme with a neat fs chart
P\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}
at a point
x\in X
.
Then
\mathcal{M}_{X,x}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{X,x}^{*}\oplus P
.

Démonstration.

Consider the exact sequence of monoids

0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X,x}^{*}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{X,x}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{X,x}\rightarrow 0.

By assumption,
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{X,x}\simeq P
, and the homomorphism
\mathcal{M}_{X,x}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{X,x}
admits a section
P\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{X,x}
.
∎

Lemma 2.2.11
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be finite dominant morphism of integral schemes, and let
\alpha_{Y}:\mathcal{M}_{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{Y}
be an fs log structure on
Y
.
Assume that
\alpha_{Y}
is injective.
If
Y
is normal, then the unit of the adjunction

\mathcal{M}_{Y}\rightarrow f_{*}^{log}f_{log}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{Y}

is an isomorphism.

Démonstration.

The question is Zariski local on
Y
, so we may assume that
Y
is a local affine scheme
\operatorname{Spec}(B)
and that
\mathcal{M}_{Y}(Y)\simeq B^{*}\oplus P
by Lemma
2.2.10
.
Since
f
is a dominant affine morphism,
\underline{f}=\operatorname{Spec}(g)
for some injective homomorphism
g:B\rightarrow A
of rings.
We only need to show that the induced commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\oplus B^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\oplus A^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

(2.2.1)

of monoids is cartesian.

To that end, let
(p,a)
be an element of
P\oplus A^{*}
such that
pa\in B
.
Since
\alpha_{Y}
is injective we see that
p\neq 0
in
B
.
Thus
a,a^{-1}\in{\rm Frac}(B)
, and hence
a,a^{-1}\in B
since
B
is normal and
f
is finite.
We conclude that
a\in B^{*}
and the assertion follows.
∎

Example 2.2.12
.

Let us give an example showing that the conclusion in Lemma
2.2.11
is false when
Y
is non-normal.
We set
X=\operatorname{Spec}(A)
,
Y=\operatorname{Spec}(B)
, where

B:=k[x,y]/(y^{2}-x^{3})_{(x-1)}\text{ and }A:=k[t]_{(t^{2}-1)}.

Set
f=\operatorname{Spec}(g)
, where
g:B\rightarrow A
is the inclusion mapping
x
to
t^{2}
and
y
to
t^{3}
.

Let
\alpha:\mathbb{N}\oplus B^{*}\rightarrow B
be the log structure mapping
(n,f)
to
x^{n}f
.
Then
x(1+t)
is in the image of the induced homomorphism
\mathbb{N}\oplus A^{*}\rightarrow A
.
But since
x(1+t)=x+y\in B
and
1+t\notin B^{*}
, the diagram (
2.2.1
) of monoids is not cartesian.

2.3.
Compositions of finite log correspondences

Using the observations in Section
2.2
we are now ready to define the composition of finite log correspondences.
We begin with the following Lemma.

Lemma 2.3.1
.

Let
X
and
Y
be fs log schemes log smooth over
k
, and suppose that
\underline{Z}
is a closed subscheme of
\underline{X}\times\underline{Y}
.
Then there exists at most one elementary log correspondence from
X
to
Y
whose underlying scheme is
\underline{Z}
.

Démonstration.

Let
Z^{N}
be the fs log scheme whose underlying scheme is the normalization
\underline{Z}^{N}
of
\underline{Z}
.
We equip it with the log structure induced from
X
via the canonical map
\underline{Z}^{N}\to\underline{Z}\to\underline{X}
,
as in Definition
2.1.1
(ii).
Let
q:\underline{Z^{N}}\rightarrow\underline{Y}
be the induced morphism of schemes.
We need to show that there is at most one morphism
r\colon Z^{N}\rightarrow Y
of fs log schemes such that
\underline{r}=q
.
Lemma
2.2.7
shows that
Y
is solid.
Since
Z^{N}
is finite over
X
, we see that
Z^{N}
is solid on account of Lemma
2.2.9
.
To conclude we apply Lemmas
2.2.3
and
2.2.5
.
∎

Lemma 2.3.2
.

Let
X
and
Y
be fs log schemes log smooth over
k
.
Then the homomorphism

(-)^{\circ}\colon\operatorname{lCor}(X,Y)\rightarrow\ Cor(X-\partial X,Y-\partial Y)

of abelian groups mapping
V\in\operatorname{lCor}(X,Y)
to
V^{\circ}:=V-\partial V
is injective.

Démonstration.

We may assume
X
and
Y
are integral.
Let

V=n_{1}V_{1}+\cdots+n_{r}V_{r}\in\operatorname{lCor}(X,Y)

be a finite log correspondence with
r>0
where
n_{i}\neq 0
for every
i
and the
V_{i}
’s are distinct.
If
V-\partial V=0
,
then
V_{i}-\partial V_{i}=V_{j}-\partial V_{j}
for some
i\neq j
.
Since the closures of
V_{i}-\partial V_{i}
and
V_{j}-\partial V_{j}
in
\underline{X}\times\underline{Y}
are equal to
\underline{V_{i}}
and
\underline{V_{j}}
respectively,
we have that
\underline{V_{i}}=\underline{V_{j}}
.
By uniqueness,
Lemma
2.3.1
implies
V_{i}=V_{j}
.
Since this contradicts our assumption, we conclude that
V-\partial V\neq 0
.
∎

Lemma 2.3.3
.

For every
X,Y,Z\in lSm/k
and finite log correspondences
\alpha\in\operatorname{lCor}(X,Y)
,
\beta\in\operatorname{lCor}(Y,Z)
,
there exists a well-defined composition
\beta\circ\alpha\in\operatorname{lCor}(X,Z)
.
The assignment

(\alpha,\beta)\mapsto\beta\circ\alpha

is associative.
Under the morphism
(-)^{\circ}
of Lemma
2.3.2
,
the composite
\beta\circ\alpha
is sent to the corresponding composite of finite correspondences.

Démonstration.

We may assume
\alpha=[V]
and
\beta=[W]
are elementary log correspondences.
In that case, we can form the finite correspondences

V-\partial V\in\operatorname{Cor}(X-\partial X,Y-\partial Y)\text{ and }W-\partial W\in\operatorname{Cor}(Y-\partial Y,Z-\partial Z).

The intersection product

(V-\partial V\times Z)\cdot(X\times W-\partial W)

can be expressed as a formal sum

n_{1}U_{1}+\cdots+n_{r}U_{r}

of reduced cycles in
X-\partial X\times Y-\partial Y\times Z-\partial Z
.
Let
\underline{T_{i}}
be the closure of
U_{i}
in
\underline{X}\times\underline{Y}\times\underline{Z}
.
By Remark
2.1.2
(iv),
the irreducible components of

(\underline{V}\times\underline{Z})\cap(\underline{X}\times\underline{W})

are precisely the closures of the irreducible components of

(V-\partial V\times Z-\partial Z)\cap(X-\partial X\times W-\partial W).

We claim that
\underline{T_{i}}
is finite and surjective over a component of
\underline{X}
.
To that end, we may assume
X
,
Y
, and
Z
are irreducible.
Since
Y
is an fs log scheme, log smooth over
k
,
\underline{Y}
is normal by Lemma
2.2.8
.
Thus by
[
64
, Lemma 1.6]
,
\underline{T_{i}}
is finite and surjective over
\underline{V}
, and so
\underline{T_{i}}
is finite and surjective over
\underline{X}
.
In particular,
we can consider

[\underline{W}]\circ[\underline{V}]=\sum_{i=1}^{r}n_{i}p_{*}[\underline{T_{i}}],

where
p\colon X\times Y\times Z\to X\times Z
is the projection, and

p_{*}[\underline{T_{i}}]=[k(\underline{T_{i}}):k(p(\underline{T_{i}}))]\cdot p(\underline{T_{i}})=:d_{i}\cdot p(\underline{T_{i}})

is the corresponding push forward.
Note that
p(\underline{T_{i}})
is finite and surjective over a component of
\underline{X}
by
[
64
, Lemma 1.4]
.
It remains to verify that
p(\underline{T_{i}})
is admissible in the sense of Definition
2.1.1
,
which is a condition on the log structure.

To simplify our notation, we write
\underline{R_{i}}
for the image
p(\underline{T_{i}})
in
\underline{X}\times\underline{Z}
.
Let
R_{i}^{N}
denote the fs log scheme whose underlying scheme is the normalization of
\underline{R_{i}}
and whose log structure is induced by
X
, and let
\underline{T_{i}^{N}}
denote the nomalization of
\underline{T_{i}}
.
There is an induced commutative diagram of schemes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{T_{i}^{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{V^{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{R_{i}^{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{W^{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{Z}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{3}}

By Remark
2.1.2
(i), part (ii) in Definition
2.1.1
of elementary log correspondences is tantamount to the existence of structure morphisms

\alpha_{12}:(q_{1})_{log}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{Y}\rightarrow(p_{1})_{log}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{X}\text{ and }\alpha_{23}:(q_{3})_{log}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{Z}\rightarrow(p_{3})_{log}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{Y}.

The composite morphism
\alpha_{123}
given by

(q_{3}r_{3})_{log}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{Z}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha_{23}}}{{\longrightarrow}}(p_{3}r_{3})_{log}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}(q_{1}r_{1})_{log}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha_{12}}}{{\longrightarrow}}(p_{1}r_{1})_{log}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{X}

can be rewritten as

\alpha_{123}\colon(q_{2}r_{2})_{log}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{Z}\to(p_{2}r_{2})_{log}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{X}.

Since
\underline{R^{N}}
is finite and surjective over a component of
\underline{X}
,
and
X
is log smooth over
k
by assumption,

R^{N}
is solid by Lemma
2.2.9
.
Thus by Lemma
2.2.5
, the log structure map
\alpha_{R_{i}^{N}}
is injective.
Since
\underline{R_{i}}^{N}
is normal, we can apply Lemma
2.2.11
to deduce there exists a unique morphism

\alpha_{13}\colon(q_{2})_{log}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{Z}\rightarrow(p_{2})_{log}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{X}

of log structures on
\underline{R_{i}^{N}}
such that
(r_{2})_{log}^{*}\alpha_{13}=\alpha_{123}
.
Thus the pair
(R_{i}^{N},\alpha_{13})
forms an elementary log correspondence
R_{i}
from
X
to
Z
.

Owing to the above, we obtain the finite log correspondence

[W]\circ[V]:=\sum_{i=1}^{r}n_{i}d_{i}[R_{i}]\in\operatorname{lCor}(X,Z).

By construction, there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{lCor}(Y,Z)\times\operatorname{lCor}(X,Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{lCor}(X,Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Cor}(Y-\partial Y,Z-\partial Z)\times\operatorname{Cor}(X-\partial X,Y-\partial Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Cor}(X-\partial X,Z-\partial Z)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\circ(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\circ(-)}

(2.3.1)

where the vertical morphisms are induced by the homomorphism
(-)^{\circ}
in Lemma
2.3.2
.
Since the vertical morphisms are injective by Lemma
2.3.2
,
the associative law for the composition follows from the case of finite correspondences (for references see
[
64
, Lecture 1]
).
∎

For every
X\in lSm/k
, the
identity

{\rm id}_{X}\in\operatorname{lCor}(X,Y)
is the finite log correspondence associated with the diagonal embedding
X\rightarrow X\times X
.

Lemma 2.3.4
.

For every
X,Y\in lSm/k
and finite log correspondence
\alpha\in\operatorname{lCor}(X,Y)
we have that

{\rm id}_{Y}\circ\alpha=\alpha=\alpha\circ{\rm id}_{X}.

Démonstration.

Owing to Lemma
2.3.2
we reduce to the case when
X=X-\partial X
and
Y=Y-\partial Y
, i.e.,
X
and
Y
are usual schemes, and we can appeal to
[
64
, Lecture 1]
.
∎

Definition 2.3.5
.

Let
lCor/k
denote the category with objects
lSm/k
and morphisms finite log correspondences between fs log schemes log smooth over
k
.
Composition of morphisms are defined in Lemma
2.3.3
.
It is an additive category with zero object
\emptyset
(the empty log scheme) and coproduct
\amalg
(disjoint union).

Remark 2.3.6
.

(i)

For
X,Y,Z\in SmlSm/k
, the composition

\operatorname{Cor}(Y,X)\times\operatorname{Cor}(Z,Y)\rightarrow\operatorname{Cor}(X,Z)

in
[
64
, §1]
coincides with the above composition.

(ii)

If
f\colon X\to Y
is a morphism in
lSm/k
, its graph
\Gamma_{f}
defines an element of
\operatorname{lCor}(X,Y)
.
Note that
\underline{X}
is normal by Lemma
2.2.8
, and
\underline{\Gamma_{f}}
is isomorphic to
\underline{X}
via the projection morphism.

(iii)

If
W\in\operatorname{lCor}(Y,Z)
is an elementary log correspondence, then
W\circ\Gamma_{f}
is obtained by the compositions

\underline{X}\times_{\underline{Y}}\underline{W}\rightarrow\underline{X}\times_{\underline{Y}}(\underline{Y}\times\underline{Z})\simeq\underline{X}\times\underline{Z},\;(X\times_{Y}W^{N})^{N}\rightarrow X\times_{Y}W^{N}\rightarrow X\times Z.

(iv)

If
V\in\operatorname{lCor}(U,X)
is an elementary log correspondence, then
\Gamma_{f}\circ V
is obtained by the compositions

\underline{V}\rightarrow\underline{U}\times\underline{X}\rightarrow\underline{U}\times\underline{Y},\;V^{N}\rightarrow U\times X\rightarrow U\times Y.

Definition 2.3.7
.

For
X,Y\in SmlSm/k
, there is a canonical homomorphism

\underline{(-)}:\operatorname{lCor}(X,Y)\rightarrow\operatorname{Cor}(\underline{X},\underline{Y})

mapping any elementary log correspondence
V
to the elementary correspondence
\underline{V}
.
This homomorphism is well defined since the underlying closed subscheme
\underline{V}
of an elementary log correspondence
V
is finite and surjective over (a component of)
\underline{X}
by Definition
2.1.1
(i).

Lemma 2.3.8
.

For
X,Y,Z\in SmlSm/k
, there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{lCor}(Y,Z)\times\operatorname{lCor}(X,Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{lCor}(X,Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Cor}(\underline{Y},\underline{Z})\times\operatorname{Cor}(\underline{X},\underline{Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Cor}(\underline{X},\underline{Z}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\circ(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{(-)}\circ\underline{(-)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{(-)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\circ(-)}

Démonstration.

Let
u:X-\partial X\rightarrow X
and
v:Y-\partial Y\rightarrow Y
be the structure open immersion.
Since
\operatorname{Cor}(-,\underline{Z})
is an étale sheaf by
[
64
, Lemma 6.2]
,

(-)\circ\Gamma_{\underline{u}}:\operatorname{Cor}(\underline{X},\underline{Z})\rightarrow\operatorname{Cor}(X-\partial X,\underline{Z})

is injective.
Hence it suffices to check the commutativity of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{lCor}(Y,Z)\times\operatorname{lCor}(X,Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{lCor}(X,Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Cor}(\underline{Y},\underline{Z})\times\operatorname{Cor}(X-\partial X,\underline{Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Cor}(X-\partial X,\underline{Z}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\circ(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{(-)}\times(\underline{(-)}\circ\Gamma_{\underline{u}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{(-)}\circ\Gamma_{\underline{u}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\circ(-)}

Due to the commutativity of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{lCor}(Y,Z)\times\operatorname{lCor}(X,Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Cor}(Y-\partial Y,\underline{Z})\times\operatorname{Cor}(X-\partial X,Y-\partial Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Cor}(\underline{Y},\underline{Z})\times\operatorname{Cor}(X-\partial X,\underline{Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Cor}(X-\partial X,\underline{Z}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{(-)}\times(\underline{(-)}\circ\Gamma_{\underline{u}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\underline{(-)}\circ\Gamma_{\underline{v}})\times(-)^{\circ}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\circ(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\circ(-)}

it suffices to check the commutativity of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{lCor}(Y,Z)\times\operatorname{lCor}(X,Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{lCor}(X,Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Cor}(Y-\partial Y,\underline{Z})\times\operatorname{Cor}(X-\partial X,Y-\partial Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Cor}(X-\partial X,\underline{Z}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\circ(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\underline{(-)}\circ\Gamma_{\underline{v}})\times(-)^{\circ}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{(-)}\circ\Gamma_{\underline{u}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\circ(-)}

Since the left (resp. right) vertical morphism factors through

\operatorname{Cor}(Y-\partial Y,Z-\partial Z)\times\operatorname{Cor}(X-\partial X,Y-\partial Y)\textrm{ (resp.\ }\operatorname{Cor}(X-\partial X,Z-\partial Z)),

we are done by the commutativity of (
).
∎

We conclude this section by defining the monoidal structure on
lCor/k
.

Definition 2.3.9
.

The tensor product of
X,Y\in\operatorname{lCor}/k
is given by the fiber product of fs log schemes

X\otimes Y:=X\times_{k}Y.

If
V
is an elementary log correspondence from
X
to
X^{\prime}
and
W
is an elementary log correspondence from
Y
to
Y^{\prime}
,
then
V\times_{k}W
defines an elementary log correspondence from
X\otimes Y
to
X^{\prime}\otimes Y^{\prime}
.
The tensor product defines a symmetric monoidal structure on
lCor/k
.

3.
Topologies on fine saturated logarithmic schemes

We want our construction of motives for fs log schemes to depend on intrinsic local data for logarithmic structures.
To achieve this, we need to provide an appropriate Grothendieck topology on our ground category categories
lSm/S
and
lSch/S
,
where throughout this section,
S
is a finite-dimensional noetherian base fs log scheme.
To establish the basic desiderata for log motives, we are basically forced to consider a suitable version of the Nisnevich topology for logarithmic schemes called the
strict Nisnevich topology
.
We also introduce a new topology in
lSch/S
, which we call the
dividing topology
.
The coverings in the dividing topology include log modifications in the sense of F. Kato
[
52
]
.
The dividing topology arises from a cd-structure in the sense of Voevodsky
[
91
]
called the
dividing cd-structure
.

The general theory of cd-structures was developed by Voevodsky
[
91
]
. Suppose that
S
is a scheme.
Primary examples include the Zariski and Nisnevich topologies on
Sm/S
, the cdh-topology on
Sch/S
, and their generalizations to algebraic stacks.
To prove basic properties about the induced topology, it suffices to check whether a given cd-structure is bounded, complete, and regular.
Unfortunately, the dividing cd-structure does not satisfy the boundedness condition,
but instead, we consider the weaker condition of being
quasi-bounded
.
For this reason, we revisit Voevodsky’s work in
[
91
]
to ensure that the set-up applies to fs log schemes.

3.1.
cd-structures on fs log schemes

Recall the following definition from
[
91
]
.

Definition 3.1.1

(
[
91
, Definition 2.1]
)
.

A
cd-structure

P
on a category
\mathcal{C}
with an initial object is a collection of commutative squares in
\mathcal{C}

(3.1.1)

that are stable under isomorphisms.
The squares in the collection
P
are called the
distinguished squares of
P
.
The topology
t_{P}
associated with
P
is the smallest Grothendieck topology such that
Y\amalg X^{\prime}
is a covering sieve of
X
for every distinguished square
Q
in
P
,
and the empty family is a covering sieve of the initial object.

Definition 3.1.2

(
[
91
, Definitions 2.2, 2.3]
)
.

Let
P
be a cd-structure on a category
\mathcal{C}
with an initial object.
The class
S_{P}
of
simple covers
is the smallest class of families of morphisms
\{U_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i\in I}
satisfying the following two conditions.

(i)

Any isomorphism is in
S_{P}
.

(ii)

For any distinguished square of the form (
3.1.1
), if
\{p_{i}^{\prime}:X_{i}^{\prime}\rightarrow X^{\prime}\}_{i\in I}
and
\{q_{j}:Y_{j}\rightarrow Y\}_{j\in J}
are in
S_{P}
, then
\{g\circ p_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i\in I}\cup\{f\circ q_{j}\}_{j\in J}
is in
S_{P}
.

We say that
P
is
complete
if any covering sieve admits a refinement by a simple cover.

Definition 3.1.3

(
[
91
, Definitions 2.10]
)
.

Let
P
be a cd-structure on a category
\mathcal{C}
with an initial object.
Let
L(T)
denote the
t_{P}
-sheaf associated with the presheaf represented by
T\in\mathcal{C}
.
We say that
P
is
regular
if the following conditions are satisfied.

(i)

For any distinguished square
Q
of the form (
3.1.1
),
Q
is cartesian,
g
is a monomorphism.

(ii)

The morphism of
t_{P}
-sheaves

\Delta\amalg(L(g^{\prime})\times_{L(g)}L(g^{\prime})):L(Y)\amalg(L(Y^{\prime})\times_{L(X^{\prime})}L(Y^{\prime}))\rightarrow L(Y)\times_{L(X)}L(Y)

is an epimorphism.

(
[
78
]
)
.

{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -14.39 -24.46 L 17.72 -24.46}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{13.00279pt}{-17.67757pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-0.67343pt}{-13.96371pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(3.1.2)

is called a

(i)

Zariski distinguished square
if
f
and
g
are open immersions.

(ii)

strict Nisnevich distinguished square
if
f
is strict étale,
g
is an open immersion,

g(X^{\prime})\cup f(Y)=X
,
and
f
induces an isomorphism

f^{-1}(\underline{X}-g(\underline{X^{\prime}}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\underline{X}-g(\underline{X^{\prime}})

with respect to the reduced scheme structures.

(iii)

dividing distinguished square
if
Y^{\prime}=X^{\prime}=\emptyset
and
f
is a surjective proper log étale monomorphism.
See §
A.6
for properties of log étale morphisms and §
A.11
for properties of log étale monomorphisms.
According to Proposition
A.11.9
, this condition is equivalent to asking that
f
be a log modification in the sense of F. Kato.

Associated to the collection of Zariski distinguished squares (resp. strict Nisnevich distinguished squares, resp. dividing distinguished squares)
we have the corresponding
Zariski cd-structure
(resp.
strict Nisnevich cd-structure
, resp. the
dividing cd-structure
).
These cd-structures give rise to the
Zariski topology
,
strict Nisnevich topology
, and
dividing topology
on
lSch/S
,
respectively.
We let
Zar
(resp.
sNis
, resp.
div
) be shorthand for the Zariski topology (resp. strict Nisnevich topology, resp. dividing topology).

The
dividing Zariski cd-structure
(resp.
dividing Nisnevich cd-structure
) is the union of the Zariski (resp. strict Nisnevich) and the dividing cd-structures.
We refer to the associated topology as the
dividing Zariski topology
(resp.
dividing Nisnevich topology
).
We let
dZar
(resp.
dNis
) be shorthand for the dividing Zariski topology (resp. dividing Nisnevich topology).

Definition 3.1.5
.

The
strict étale topology
(resp.
Kummer étale topology
, resp. log étale topology) on
lSch/S
is the smallest Grothendieck topology generated by strict étale covers
(resp. Kummer étale covers, resp. log étale covers) in
lSch/S
.
The
dividing étale topology
on
lSch/S
is the smallest Grothendieck topology finer than the strict étale topology and the dividing topology.
We let
s\acute{e}t
(resp.
k\acute{e}t
,
l\acute{e}t
,
d\acute{e}t
) be shorthand for the strict étale topology
(resp. Kummer étale topology, resp. log étale topology, resp. dividing étale topology).

We note that none of these étale topologies are associated with a cd-structure.
The log étale topology is the smallest Grothendieck topology that is finer than the Kummer étale topology and the dividing topology,
see also
[
47
, §9.1]
whose proof appears in
[
70
, Proposition 3.9]
.

Example 3.1.6
.

Every Nisnevich distinguished square of usual schemes is also a strict Nisnevich distinguished square.
If the square
Q
in (
3.1.2
) is a strict Nisnevich distinguished square, then the square

\underline{Q}=\hbox to71.22pt{\vbox to47.34pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 35.61107pt\lower-21.99724pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
