-sep)

(
\overline{\Box}
-inv)

(
sNis
-des)

(
div
-des)

\mathscr{S}=lSm/S
(
Zar
-sep)

(
sNis
-des)

(
(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})
-inv)

\mathscr{S}=SmlSm/S
>
Trivial if
S=k
<
Theorem
7.6.7
(assuming ROS)
>
Proposition
7.3.1
<
Construction
7.8.4

Definition 7.0.1
.

The Tate object in
\mathcal{T}
is defined by

M(S)(1):=M(S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{0}}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathbb{P}_{S}^{1})[-2],

where
i_{0}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{P}_{S}^{1}
is the
0
-section.

For
n\geq 0
, we set

M(S)(n):=\underbrace{M(S)(1)\otimes\cdots\otimes M(S)(1)}_{n\text{ times}},

where
M(S)(0):=M(S)
by convention.
We define the
n
th Tate twist of
K\in\mathcal{T}
as

K(n):=K\otimes M(S)(n).

Proposition 7.0.2
.

The conditions
(Sq)
and
(MSq)
of Appendix
C
and the properties
(
Zar
-sep)
,
(
\overline{\Box}
-inv)
,
(
sNis
-des)
,
and
(
div
-des)
hold in the triangulated categories

\displaystyle{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda),\;{\bf logDA}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda),\;{\bf logDM}_{d\acute{e}t}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda),\;{\bf logDA}_{d\acute{e}t}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda),

\displaystyle{\bf logDM}_{l\acute{e}t}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda),\;{\bf logDA}_{l\acute{e}t}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda).

Démonstration.

We work in
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
since the proofs for the other categories are similar.
For every
n
-square
C
, we set

M(C):={a_{dNis}^{*}}{\rm Tot}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{ltr}}(C)).

For every
n
-square
C
with
n\geq 1
, there exists a naturally induced sequence

{\rm Tot}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{ltr}}(s_{i,0}^{*}C))\rightarrow{\rm Tot}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{ltr}}(s_{i,1}^{*}C))\rightarrow{\rm Tot}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{ltr}}(C))\rightarrow{\rm Tot}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{ltr}}(s_{i,0}^{*}C))[1].

Moreover, for every
n
-square
C
and
m
-square
D
, there exists a canonical isomorphism (not just a quasi-isomorphism) of complexes

{\rm Tot}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{ltr}}(C))\otimes{\rm Tot}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{ltr}}(D))\rightarrow{\rm Tot}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{ltr}}(C\otimes D)).

The latter is reminiscent of an Eilenberg-Zilber map, but it is, in fact, much simpler since no shuffles are involved.
We can readily deduce that the conditions (Sq) and (MSq) hold.

Property (
\overline{\Box}
-inv) holds by construction.
Proposition
5.2.10
implies (
sNis
-des) and (
div
-des).
To show (
Zar
-sep),
let
\mathscr{X}
be the Čech nerve of the covering
\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{r}\}
,
and set

\mathscr{X}^{\prime}:=X^{\prime}\times_{X}\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y}:=Y\times_{X}\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y}^{\prime}:=Y^{\prime}\times_{X}\mathscr{X}.

Then, by assumption, we have the homotopy cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(\mathscr{Y}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(\mathscr{Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(\mathscr{X}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(\mathscr{X}).}

This allows us to conclude that (
Zar
-sep) holds owing to the isomorphisms

M(X)\simeq M(\mathscr{X}),M(Y)\simeq M(\mathscr{Y}),M(X^{\prime})\simeq M(\mathscr{X}^{\prime}),\text{ and }M(Y^{\prime})\simeq M(\mathscr{Y}^{\prime}).

∎

7.1.
Some toric geometry and examples of bundles

Let
d\geq 0
and suppose
\sigma
is a
d
-codimensional cone of a fan
\Sigma
.
We can naturally associate a
d
-dimensional closed subscheme
V(\sigma)
of
\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma}}
, which is the closure of the orbit space
{\rm orb}(\sigma)
in
\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma}}
, see
[
75
, Proposition 1.6, Corollary 1.7]
.
Let us explain the structure in the case that
\Sigma=\operatorname{Spec}(P)
for some fs monoid
P
.
Note that the only maximal cone of
\Sigma
is
P^{\vee}
.

The cone
\sigma
is associated with a face
F
of
P^{\vee}
,
which corresponds by duality to the face

G:=F^{\bot}\cap P=\{x\in P:\langle x,y\rangle=0\text{ for all }y\in F\}.

Then
{\rm orb}(\sigma)
is identified with

\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[F^{\bot}\cap P^{\mathrm{gp}}])\simeq\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[G^{\mathrm{gp}}]),

see
[
36
, p. 52]
.
Owing to
[
76
, Proposition I.3.3.4]
the closed immersion

V(\sigma)\rightarrow\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma}}

is induced by the natural epimorphism

\mathbb{Z}[P]\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}[P]/\mathbb{Z}[P-G],

where we regard
\mathbb{Z}[P-G]
as an ideal of
\mathbb{Z}[P]
as in
[
76
, Section I.3.3]
.

If more specifically
P=\mathbb{N}^{p}\oplus\mathbb{N}^{q}
and
F
is the face
\mathbb{N}^{p}\oplus 0
of
P^{\vee}
for some integers
p,q\geq 0
, then
G=0\oplus\mathbb{N}^{q}
. Thus if we express
\mathbb{Z}[P]
as
\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{p+q}]
, then

\mathbb{Z}[P]/\mathbb{Z}[P-G]=\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{p+q}]/(x_{1},\ldots,x_{p}).

Definition 7.1.1
.

Let
\Sigma
be a fan.
Suppose that
\Sigma^{\prime}
is a subfan of
\Sigma
, i.e., every cone of
\Sigma^{\prime}
is a cone of
\Sigma
.
We denote by

\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime})}

the fs log scheme whose underlying scheme is
\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma}}
and whose log structure is the compactifying log structure associated with the open immersion

(\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma}}-\bigcup_{\sigma\in\Sigma^{\prime}-\{0\}}V(\sigma))\rightarrow\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma}}.

For the convenience of the reader we will often draw figures illustrating the fans
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
.
In our drawings of
2
-dimensional fans,
thick (resp. thin) lines record the
1
-dimensional cones in
\Sigma^{\prime}
(resp. not in
\Sigma^{\prime}
),
and dark (resp. light) grey regions records the
2
-dimensional cones in
\Sigma^{\prime}
(resp. not in
\Sigma^{\prime}
).
For example,
the figure

(7.1.1)

depicts the pair of fans

\Sigma=\{{\rm Cone}((1,0),(0,1)),{\rm Cone}((0,1),(-1,0))\},\;\Sigma^{\prime}=\{{\rm Cone}((0,1),(-1,0))\}.

Here, the notation
\Sigma=\{\sigma,\ldots\}
means that
\Sigma
consists of all faces of the elements of the set
\{\sigma,\ldots\}
.
The algebraic variety encoded in the figure (
7.1.1
) can be described as follows.
We set
\sigma_{1}={\rm Cone}((1,0),(0,1))
,

\sigma_{2}=\Sigma^{\prime}={\rm Cone}((0,1),(-1,0))
,

M=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,
and
N={\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(M,\mathbb{Z})
.
The toric variety
\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma}}
is obtained by gluing the two affine schemes

\mathbb{A}_{\sigma_{1}}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[\sigma_{1}^{\vee}\cap N])=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x,y])

and

\mathbb{A}_{\sigma_{2}}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[\sigma_{2}^{\vee}\cap N])=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x^{-1},y])

along their common open subset

\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[\tau^{\vee}\cap N])=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x,x^{-1},y]),

where
\tau
is the 1-dimensional cone
(0,1)
.
The underlying scheme is

X=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}.

Here
x
is the coordinate on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and
y
is the coordinate on
\mathbb{A}^{1}
.
Consider the divisor
\partial X=\infty\times\mathbb{A}^{1}+\mathbb{P}^{1}\times 0
on
X
.
The corresponding compactifying log structure
(X,\partial X)
can be identified with the fs log scheme
\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime})}
.

In our drawings of
3
-dimensional fans,
thick points records
1
-dimensional cones in
\Sigma^{\prime}
, thick (resp. thin) lines record
2
-dimensional cones in
\Sigma^{\prime}
(resp. not in
\Sigma^{\prime}
),
and dark (resp. light) grey regions record
3
-dimensional cones in
\Sigma^{\prime}
(resp. not in
\Sigma^{\prime}
).

Before discussing the motives of general fs log schemes, we will first investigate some elementary examples of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
- and
\overline{\Box}
-bundles.

Let us define the geometric notion of vector bundles over fs log schemes.

Definition 7.1.2
.

Let
X
be an fs log scheme.
A
vector bundle

\xi:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow X
is a morphism of fs log schemes such that
\underline{\xi}:\underline{\mathcal{E}}\rightarrow\underline{X}
is a usual vector bundle and the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\xi}}

is cartesian, where the vertical arrows are the canonical morphisms removing the log structures. In other words, the log structure on
\mathcal{E}
is the pullback of the log structure on
X
along
\xi
.

Example 7.1.3
.

The projection
X\times_{S}\mathbb{A}_{S}^{n}\rightarrow X
is a vector bundle.
This is called the
trivial
vector bundle of rank
n
.

Definition 7.1.4
.

We say that “an fs log scheme
X
satisfies Zariski locally a property
P
” if for any point
x
of
X
and Zariski neighborhood
V
of
x
,
there is a Zariski neighborhood
U
of
x
inside
V
such that
U
satisfies
P
.

Definition 7.1.5
.

Let
X
be an fs log scheme.
A
\mathbb{P}^{n}
-
bundle

\xi:\mathcal{P}\rightarrow X
is a morphism of fs log schemes that Zariski locally on
X
is isomorphic to the projection
X\times\mathbb{P}^{n}\rightarrow X
.

Similarly, a
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
-
bundle

\xi:\mathcal{P}\rightarrow X
is a morphism of fs log schemes that Zariski locally on
X
is isomorphic to the projection

X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})\rightarrow X
.
Note that we have
(\mathbb{P}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{0})=\overline{\Box}
.

Recall that we view
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
as the hyperplane defined by
x_{n}=0
where
[x_{0}:\cdots:x_{n}]
is the coordinate on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
.

Lemma 7.1.6
.

The blow-up
X
of
\mathbb{P}^{n}
along the point
[0:\cdots:0:1]
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle over
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
.

Démonstration.

While this should be a well-known fact, since we need to fix some notation for later use, we give proof using the language of toric geometry.
Let
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
be the standard coordinates of
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.
The blow-up
X
in question is the toric variety associated with the fan with maximal cones

\sigma_{i}:={\rm Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{i-1},e_{i+1},\ldots,e_{n},e_{1}+\cdots+e_{n})\quad(1\leq i\leq n),

\sigma_{i}^{\prime}:={\rm Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{i-1},e_{i+1},\ldots,e_{n},-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{n})\quad(1\leq i\leq n).

For a fixed
i
, the toric variety
U_{i}
formed by
\sigma_{i}
and
\sigma_{i}^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Recall that
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
is the toric variety associated with the fan with maximal cones

\tau_{i}:={\rm Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{i-1},e_{i+1},\ldots,e_{n-1},-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{n-1})\quad(1\leq i\leq n-1),

\tau_{n}:={\rm Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n-1}).

For the homomorphism of lattices

\varphi:\mathbb{Z}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}

mapping
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
to
(x_{1}-x_{n},\ldots,x_{n-1}-x_{n})
,
we have that

\varphi(\sigma_{i}\cup\sigma_{i}^{\prime})=\tau_{i}.

Thus the morphism
X\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
induced by
\varphi
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle.
∎

Lemma 7.1.7
.

The blow-up of
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
along the point
[0:\cdots:0:1]
is a
\overline{\Box}
-bundle over
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
.

Démonstration.

The cones
\sigma_{i}
and
\sigma_{i}^{\prime}
appearing in the proof of Lemma
7.1.6
correspond espectively to

U_{i}:={\rm Spec}\left(\mathbb{Z}\left[\frac{x_{0}}{x_{i-1}},\ldots,\frac{x_{i-2}}{x_{i-1}},\frac{x_{i}}{x_{i-1}},\ldots,\frac{x_{n-1}}{x_{i-1}},\frac{x_{i-1}}{x_{n}}\right]\right)

and

U_{i}^{\prime}:={\rm Spec}\left(\mathbb{Z}\left[\frac{x_{0}}{x_{i-1}},\ldots,\frac{x_{i-2}}{x_{i-1}},\frac{x_{i}}{x_{i-1}},\ldots,\frac{x_{n-1}}{x_{i-1}},\frac{x_{n}}{x_{i-1}}\right]\right).

Their log structures induced by
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
are given by

\mathbb{N}\left(\frac{x_{n}}{x_{i-1}}\right)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\left[\frac{x_{0}}{x_{i-1}},\ldots,\frac{x_{i-2}}{x_{i-1}},\frac{x_{i}}{x_{i-1}},\ldots,\frac{x_{n-1}}{x_{i-1}},\frac{x_{n}}{x_{i-1}}\right].

In the proof of Lemma
7.1.6
, we noted that

U_{i}\cup U_{i}^{\prime}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}.

If we impose the above log structure, then this becomes
\mathbb{A}^{n-1}\times\overline{\Box}
, and the claim follows.
∎

Lemma 7.1.8
.

For
n\geq 2
we let
X
be the blow-up of
\mathbb{P}^{n}
at the point
[0:\cdots:0:1]
.
Let
H
be the divisor of
\mathbb{P}^{n}
defined by
(x_{0}=0)
,
let
E
be the exceptional divisor on
X
, and let
H^{\prime}
be the strict transform of
H
in
X
.
Then

(X,H^{\prime}+E)

is a
\overline{\Box}
-bundle over
(\mathbb{P}^{n-1},\mathbb{P}^{n-2})
.

Démonstration.

Recall the schemes
U_{i}
and
U_{i}^{\prime}
appearing in the proof of Lemma
7.1.7
.
We equip
U_{i}
with the log structures

\mathbb{N}\left(\frac{x_{0}}{x_{i-1}}\right)\oplus\mathbb{N}\left(\frac{x_{i-1}}{x_{n}}\right)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\left[\frac{x_{0}}{x_{i-1}},\ldots,\frac{x_{i-2}}{x_{i-1}},\frac{x_{i}}{x_{i-1}},\ldots,\frac{x_{n-1}}{x_{i-1}},\frac{x_{n}}{x_{i-1}}\right]\text{ if }i>1,

\mathbb{N}\left(\frac{x_{0}}{x_{n}}\right)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\left[\frac{x_{0}}{x_{i-1}},\ldots,\frac{x_{i-2}}{x_{i-1}},\frac{x_{i}}{x_{i-1}},\ldots,\frac{x_{n-1}}{x_{i-1}},\frac{x_{n}}{x_{i-1}}\right]\text{ if }i=1,

and
U_{i}^{\prime}
with the log structures

\mathbb{N}\left(\frac{x_{0}}{x_{i-1}}\right)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\left[\frac{x_{0}}{x_{i-1}},\ldots,\frac{x_{i-2}}{x_{i-1}},\frac{x_{i}}{x_{i-1}},\ldots,\frac{x_{n-1}}{x_{i-1}},\frac{x_{i-1}}{x_{n}}\right]\text{ if }i>1,

0\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\left[\frac{x_{0}}{x_{i-1}},\ldots,\frac{x_{i-2}}{x_{i-1}},\frac{x_{i}}{x_{i-1}},\ldots,\frac{x_{n-1}}{x_{i-1}},\frac{x_{i-1}}{x_{n}}\right]\text{ if }i=1.

Let
X_{i}
and
X_{i}^{\prime}
denote the associated fs log schemes, and let
Y_{i}
be the fs log scheme associated with

\mathbb{N}\left(\frac{x_{0}}{x_{i-1}}\right)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\left[\frac{x_{0}}{x_{i-1}},\ldots,\frac{x_{i-2}}{x_{i-1}},\frac{x_{i}}{x_{i-1}},\ldots,\frac{x_{n-1}}{x_{i-1}}\right].

With these definitions, we have

X_{i}\cup X_{i}^{\prime}\simeq Y_{i}\times\overline{\Box}.

This finishes the proof because the
Y_{i}
’s glue together to form
(\mathbb{P}^{n-1},\mathbb{P}^{n-2})
.
Here we consider
\mathbb{P}^{n-2}
as the divisor on
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
defined by
(x_{0}=0)
.
∎

Lemma 7.1.9
.

Let
X
be the blow-up of
\mathbb{P}^{n}
along the point
[0:\cdots:0:1]
, and let
E
be the corresponding exceptional divisor on
X
.
Then
(X,E)
is a
\overline{\Box}
-bundle over
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
.

Démonstration.

Recall the schemes
U_{i}
and
U_{i}^{\prime}
appearing in the proof of Lemma
7.1.7
.
Their log structures induced by
(X,E)
are given by

\mathbb{N}\left(\frac{x_{i-1}}{x_{n}}\right)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\left[\frac{x_{0}}{x_{i-1}},\ldots,\frac{x_{i-2}}{x_{i-1}},\frac{x_{i}}{x_{i-1}},\ldots,\frac{x_{n-1}}{x_{i-1}},\frac{x_{i-1}}{x_{n}}\right].

Our claim follows by noting there is an isomorphism

U_{i}\cup U_{i}^{\prime}\simeq\overline{\Box}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-1}.

∎

Proposition 7.1.10
.

Suppose
Y
is a
\overline{\Box}
-bundle over
X\in\mathscr{S}/S
.
If
\mathcal{T}
satisfies
(
\overline{\Box}
-inv)
and
(
sNis
-des)
, then the projection
Y\rightarrow X
induces an isomorphism

M(Y)\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(X).

Démonstration.

Use induction on the number of open subsets in a trivialization of
Y
; then from (
sNis
-des), we may assume
Y\simeq X\times\overline{\Box}
.
To conclude, we apply (
\overline{\Box}
-inv).
∎

7.2.
Invariance under admissible blow-ups along smooth centers

In this subsection,
assuming the properties (
Zar
-sep), (
\overline{\Box}
-inv), and (
sNis
-des) we will extend the invariance under log modifications (
div
-des) to admissible blow-ups along smooth centers,
whose definition is given below.

Definition 7.2.1
.

Suppose that
Z
is an effective Cartier divisor on
X\in Sm/S
, and let
i\colon Z\rightarrow X
be the corresponding closed immersion.
Then
Z
is a
strict normal crossing divisor
on
X
over
S
if Zariski locally on
X
, there is a cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\times\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]/(x_{1}\cdots x_{r}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\times\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}])}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

of schemes over
S
for some
r\leq n
,
where the horizontal morphisms are étale.

We say the divisors
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{r}
on
X\in Sm/S
form a
strict normal crossing divisor
if

Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{r}

is a strict normal crossing divisor on
X
over
S
.

Suppose that
Z
is a strict normal crossing divisor on
X
over
S
, and let
v:W\rightarrow X
be a closed immersion of schemes smooth over
S
.
We say that
W
has
strict normal crossing
with
Z
over
S
if Zariski locally on
X
, there is a commutative diagram of cartesian squares of schemes over
S

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\times\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]/(x_{1}\cdots x_{r}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\times\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\times\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]/(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{s}}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}

for some
r\leq n
and
1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{s}\leq n
,
where the right vertical morphisms are the evident closed immersions and the horizontal morphisms are étale. Note that, a priori, in this formulation
W
can be contained in a component of
Z
. If
r<i_{1}<\cdots i_{s}\leq n
, we get, in particular, that the restriction of
Z
to
W
is again a Cartier divisor, and that it forms a strict normal crossing divisor on
W
.

Definition 7.2.2
.

For divisors
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{r}
forming a strict normal crossing divisor on
X\in Sm/S
, for
1\leq s\leq r
, we use the convenient “associative” notation

\begin{split}((X,Z_{s+1}+\cdots+Z_{r}),Z_{1}^{\prime}+\cdots+Z_{s}^{\prime})&:=(X,Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{r})\end{split}

where
Z_{i}^{\prime}
is any fs log scheme such that
\underline{Z_{i}^{\prime}}\simeq Z_{i}
(we refer to Definition
1.1.1
for the notation
(X,Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{r})
).

To illustrate, suppose
X\in SmlSm/S
has compactifying log structure given by
Z_{s+1}+\cdots+Z_{r}
, written
(X,Z_{s+1}+\cdots+Z_{r})
.
And let us consider a strict normal crossing divisor
Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{s}
on
X
such that
Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{s}+Z_{s+1}+\cdots+Z_{r}
is again strict normal crossing.
This determines another object of
SmlSm/S
,
namely
(X,Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{r})
,
equipped with a canonical map

(X,Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{r})\to(X,Z_{s+1}+\cdots+Z_{r})

obtained by adding the divisor
Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{s}
to the log structure.

For instance, if
Y:=(X,Z_{s+1},\cdots,Z_{r})
, then we obtain

(Y,Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{s})=(X,Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{r}).

If
Y=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\overline{\Box}=(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\{\infty\})
and
Z_{1}^{\prime}=\{\infty\}\times\overline{\Box}
, then

(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\overline{\Box},\{\infty\}\times\overline{\Box})=\overline{\Box}^{2}.

Definition 7.2.3
.

An
admissible blow-up along a smooth center over
S
(or along a smooth center for short) is a proper birational morphism

f:X^{\prime}\rightarrow X

in
SmlSm/S
for which the following properties hold.

(1)

\underline{f}
is the blow-up along a center
Z
in
\underline{X}
such that
Z
is smooth over
S
.

(2)

\partial X^{\prime}
is the union of
f^{-1}(Z)
and the strict transform of
\partial X
with respect to
f
.

(3)

Z
is contained in
\partial X
and has strict normal crossing with
\partial X
over
S
in the sense of Definition
7.2.1
.

Note that by Lemma
A.5.10
, we may assume that
\partial X
is a strict normal crossing divisor on
X
,
so that condition (3) makes sense.
The open immersion
X-Z\rightarrow X
lifts to
X^{\prime}
.
Hence the log structure on
X^{\prime}
is precisely the compactifying log structure associated with the composite open immersion

X-\partial X\rightarrow X-Z\rightarrow X^{\prime}.

If
Z
has codimension
d
in
X
, then we say that
f
is an
admissible blow-up along a codimension
d
smooth center
.
Let
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}l_{Sm}/S
denote the smallest class of morphisms in
\mathscr{S}/S
containing all admissible blow-ups along a smooth center and closed under composition.

Let us give a fundamental example of admissible blow-ups along a smooth center.

Example 7.2.4
.

Let
X=\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{N}}^{p}\times\mathbb{A}^{q}
and let
e_{1},\ldots,e_{p+q}
denote the standard coordinates on
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.
We set

\sigma:={\rm Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{p+q}),\;\sigma^{\prime}:={\rm Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{p}),

and form the associated fans

\Sigma:=\{\sigma\},\;\Sigma^{\prime}:=\{\sigma^{\prime}\}.

Then
X
can be written as
\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime})}
.

Let
X^{\prime}
be the admissible blow-up of
X
along the origin.
Then

\underline{X^{\prime}}=\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Gamma}},

where
\Gamma
denotes the star subdivision
\Sigma^{*}(\sigma)
of
\Sigma
relative to
\sigma
.
Let
\Gamma^{\prime}
be the fan consisting of all cones
\sigma\in\Gamma
satisfying

\sigma\subset{\rm Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{p},e_{1}+\cdots+e_{p}).

Note that

\partial X=V(e_{1})\cup\cdots\cup V(e_{p}).

The strict transform of
V(e_{i})\subset X
for
1\leq i\leq p
with respect to
f
is again
V(e_{i})\subset X^{\prime}
.
The exceptional divisor on
X^{\prime}
is
V(e_{1}+\cdots+e_{p})
.
Using these descriptions of the divisors, we deduce that

X^{\prime}=\mathbb{A}_{(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})}.

For
p=q=1
,
we are considering
\mathbb{A}^{2}
with log structure given by the affine line
(x=0)=H\subset\mathbb{A}^{2}
, and
X^{\prime}
is
B_{(0,0)}(\mathbb{A}^{2})
with log structure
\tilde{H}+E
,
where
\tilde{H}
is the strict transform of
H
.
Note that
X^{\prime}\to X

is not
a log modification.
In fact, since the log structure on
\mathbb{A}^{2}
is given by
\mathbb{N}\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x,y])
,
n\mapsto x^{n}
, there are no non-trivial log modification.

We will first prove a particular case of Theorem
7.2.10
(invariance under admissible blow-ups),
which is the crucial point of the proof in the general case.
Roughly speaking,
it says that the cone of an admissible blow-up morphism is insensitive to the addition of a smooth component of the boundary divisor,
provided that it stays normal crossing with the center.
This is carried out,
however,
without constructing directly any map between the two cones.
A posteriori, this invariance property could be re-interpreted using the Gysin triangle.

Proposition 7.2.5
.

Assume that
\mathcal{T}
satisfies
(
\overline{\Box}
-inv)
and
(
sNis
-des)
.
Let
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
be the axes of
Y:=\mathbb{A}^{2}
, let
H_{1}^{\prime}
and
H_{2}^{\prime}
be the strict transforms in the blow-up
Y^{\prime}:=B_{\{(0,0)\}}\mathbb{A}^{2}
at
(0,0)
,
and let
E
be the exceptional divisor on
Y^{\prime}
.
For
X\in\mathscr{S}/S
, the naturally induced morphism

M(X\times(Y^{\prime},E+H_{2}^{\prime}))\rightarrow M(X\times(Y,H_{2}))

is an isomorphism if and only if the naturally induced morphism

M(X\times(Y^{\prime},E+H_{1}^{\prime}+H_{2}^{\prime}))\rightarrow M(X\times(Y,H_{1}+H_{2}))

is an isomorphism.

Démonstration.

It is enough to prove the claim for
X=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
.
We consider the following cones

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}:={\rm Cone}((1,0),(1,1)),\;\sigma_{2}:={\rm Cone}((1,1),(0,1)),\;\sigma_{3}:={\rm Cone}((0,1),(-1,0)),

\displaystyle\sigma_{4}:={\rm Cone}((-1,0),(0,-1)),\;\sigma_{5}:={\rm Cone}((0,-1),(1,0)).

Form the following fans

\displaystyle\Sigma_{1}:=\{\sigma_{1}\cup\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\sigma_{4},\sigma_{5}\},\;\Sigma_{2}:=\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\sigma_{4},\sigma_{5}\},\;\Sigma_{3}:=\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\cup\sigma_{3},\sigma_{4},\sigma_{5}\},

\displaystyle\Sigma_{4}:=\{\sigma_{1}\cup\sigma_{2}\},\;\Sigma_{5}:=\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\},\;\Sigma_{6}:=\{\sigma_{3},\sigma_{4},\sigma_{5}\},\;\Sigma_{7}:=\{\sigma_{2}\cap\sigma_{3},\sigma_{1}\cap\sigma_{5}\},

\displaystyle\Sigma_{8}:=\{\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}\},\;\Sigma_{9}:=\{\sigma_{2}\cup\sigma_{3}\},\;\Sigma_{10}:=\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{4},\sigma_{5}\},\;\Sigma_{11}:=\{\sigma_{1}\cap\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}\cap\sigma_{4}\},

\displaystyle\Sigma_{1}^{\prime}=\Sigma_{6}^{\prime}:=\{\sigma_{3},\sigma_{4}\},\;\Sigma_{2}^{\prime}:=\{\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\sigma_{4}\},\;\Sigma_{3}^{\prime}:=\{\sigma_{2}\cup\sigma_{3},\sigma_{4}\},

\displaystyle\Sigma_{4}^{\prime}=\Sigma_{7}^{\prime}:=\{\sigma_{2}\cap\sigma_{3}\},\;\Sigma_{5}^{\prime}:=\{\sigma_{2}\},\;\Sigma_{8}^{\prime}:=\{\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}\},

\displaystyle\Sigma_{9}^{\prime}:=\{\sigma_{2}\cup\sigma_{3}\},\;\Sigma_{10}^{\prime}:=\{\sigma_{1}\cap\sigma_{2},\sigma_{4}\},\;\Sigma_{11}^{\prime}:=\{\sigma_{1}\cap\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}\cap\sigma_{4}\}.

For the convenience of the reader, we include a figure illustrating the above fans.

(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{1}^{\prime})
(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{2}^{\prime})
(\Sigma_{3},\Sigma_{3}^{\prime})

(\Sigma_{4},\Sigma_{4}^{\prime})
(\Sigma_{5},\Sigma_{5}^{\prime})
(\Sigma_{6},\Sigma_{6}^{\prime})
(\Sigma_{7},\Sigma_{7}^{\prime})

(\Sigma_{8},\Sigma_{8}^{\prime})
(\Sigma_{9},\Sigma_{9}^{\prime})
(\Sigma_{10},\Sigma_{10}^{\prime})
(\Sigma_{11},\Sigma_{11}^{\prime})

Here we have
\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{1}^{\prime})}=\overline{\Box}\times(\mathbb{P}^{1},0+\infty)
,
and
\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{2}^{\prime})}=B_{(0,0)}(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})
with divisor the strict transform of the divisor on
\overline{\Box}\times(\mathbb{P}^{1},0+\infty)
together with the exceptional divisor.
Finally,

\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{3},\Sigma_{3}^{\prime})}
is (isomorphic to) the blow-up of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
at the point
[1:0:0]
,
with the coordinate lines
X_{0}=0
,
X_{1}=0
and
X_{2}=0
as divisors.
Note that there is also a blow-up morphism
B_{(0,0)}(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})\to\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{3},\Sigma_{3}^{\prime})}
.
We consider the following Zariski open coverings of these log schemes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{7},\Sigma_{7}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{6},\Sigma_{6}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{4},\Sigma_{4}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{1}^{\prime})},}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{7},\Sigma_{7}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{6},\Sigma_{6}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{5},\Sigma_{5}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{2}^{\prime})},}

(7.2.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{11},\Sigma_{11}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{10},\Sigma_{10}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{8},\Sigma_{8}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{2}^{\prime})},}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{11},\Sigma_{11}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{10},\Sigma_{10}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{9},\Sigma_{9}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{3},\Sigma_{3}^{\prime})}.}

(7.2.2)

Here,
the second diagram in (
) and the first diagram in (
) are two different open charts for the blow-up of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
at
(0,0)

(and the toric notation helps keeping track of the different log structures).
Note that
\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{6},\Sigma_{6}^{\prime})}
is a common Zariski open subset of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
and of the blow-up
B_{(0,0)}(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})
, namely it is the open subset

\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}-\{(0,0)\}
(which does not contain the centre of the blow-up).
Similarly,
note that
\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{10},\Sigma_{10^{\prime}})}
is a common Zariski open subset of
\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{3},\Sigma_{3}^{\prime})}
and of the blow-up of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
at
(0,0)

(this time containing the center of the blow-up).

Using (
sNis
-des) we have a zig-zag of isomorphisms

\displaystyle M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{5},\Sigma_{5}^{\prime})}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{2}^{\prime})})\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{4},\Sigma_{4}^{\prime})}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{1}^{\prime})}),

\displaystyle M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{8},\Sigma_{8}^{\prime})}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{2}^{\prime})})\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{9},\Sigma_{9}^{\prime})}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{3},\Sigma_{3}^{\prime})}).

The squares in (
) can be completed to a
3
-dimensional commutative diagram with back side

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{5},\Sigma_{5}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{2}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{4},\Sigma_{4}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{1}^{\prime})},}

where the morphisms
\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{2}^{\prime})}\to\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{1}^{\prime})}
and
\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{5},\Sigma_{5}^{\prime})}\to\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{4},\Sigma_{4}^{\prime})}
are induced by the blow-up morphisms

B_{(0,0)}(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})\to\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
B_{(0,0)}(\mathbb{A}^{2})\to\mathbb{A}^{2}
, respectively.
Taking into account the log structures,
the morphism
(Y^{\prime},E+H_{2}^{\prime})\rightarrow(Y,H_{2})
is equal to the morphism
\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{5},\Sigma_{5}^{\prime})}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{4},\Sigma_{4}^{\prime})}
.
Similarly, we can complete the squares in (
) to a 3-dimensional commutative diagram with back side

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{8},\Sigma_{8}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{2}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{9},\Sigma_{9}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{3},\Sigma_{3}^{\prime})},}

where the blow-ups induce the vertical morphisms.
More precisely, via the linear transformation

\mathbb{Z}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{2}

mapping
(a,b)
to
(a-b,a)
,
the morphism
(Y^{\prime},E+H_{1}^{\prime}+H_{2}^{\prime})\rightarrow(Y,H_{1}+H_{2})
can be identified with
\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{8},\Sigma_{8}^{\prime})}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{9},\Sigma_{9}^{\prime})}

(note that
\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma_{9}}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x^{-1},yx^{-1}])
).
Due to Proposition
C.1.7
there are induced isomorphisms

\displaystyle M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{5},\Sigma_{5}^{\prime})}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{4},\Sigma_{4}^{\prime})})\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{2}^{\prime})}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{1}^{\prime})}),

(7.2.3)

\displaystyle M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{8},\Sigma_{8}^{\prime})}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{9},\Sigma_{9}^{\prime})})\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{2}^{\prime})}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{3},\Sigma_{3}^{\prime})}).

(7.2.4)

Thus from (
7.2.3
) and (
7.2.4
), we reduce to showing the equivalence of

M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{2}^{\prime})}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{1}^{\prime})})=0

and

M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{2}^{\prime})}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{3},\Sigma_{3}^{\prime})})=0.

Both
\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{1}^{\prime})}
and
\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{3},\Sigma_{3}^{\prime})}
are
\overline{\Box}
-bundles over
\overline{\Box}
,
so that Proposition
7.1.10
gives isomorphisms

M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{1}^{\prime})})\rightarrow M(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})),\;M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{3},\Sigma_{3}^{\prime})})\rightarrow M(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})).

This finishes the proof.
∎

Recall now some terminology from
[
28
]
.

Definition 7.2.6

(
[
28
, Definition 4.5.3]
)
.

A
closed pair

(X,Z)

smooth
over
S
is a closed immersion
Z\rightarrow X
where both
X
and
Z
are object in
Sm/S
.
A
cartesian
(resp.
excisive
) morphism
(f,f^{\prime})
of closed pairs
(X,Z)\rightarrow(X^{\prime},Z^{\prime})
smooth over
S
is a commutative diagram of
S
-schemes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

which is cartesian (resp. such that
f^{\prime}:Z\rightarrow Z^{\prime}
is an isomorphism).

Definition 7.2.7

(
[
28
, Definitions 4.5.8]
)
.

Let
(X,Z)
be a closed pair smooth over
S
, and consider the smooth closed pair
(\mathbb{A}_{S}^{r+s},\mathbb{A}_{S}^{r})
given by

a_{0}\times{\rm id}:\mathbb{A}_{S}^{s}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{S}^{r+s},

where
a_{0}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{S}^{r}
is the
0
-section.
A
parametrization
of
(X,Z)
is a cartesian morphism
(f,f^{\prime}):(X,Z)\rightarrow(\mathbb{A}_{S}^{r+s},\mathbb{A}_{S}^{s})
such that
f
is étale.

Construction 7.2.8
.

We will use the following technique appearing in the proof of homotopy purity in
[
68
, Lemma 2.28, p. 117]
—
see also
[
28
, §4.5.2]
for another exposition,
and see
[
42
, §7]
for formulations in related settings.
Let
(X,Z)
be a closed pair smooth over
S
.
Then by
[
30
, IV.17.12.2]
, Zariski locally on
X
, there are étale morphisms
w
and
w^{\prime}
making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{S}^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{S}^{r+s}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}

cartesian. Setting
X_{1}:=\mathbb{A}_{Z}^{s}
the morphism
{\rm id}\times w^{\prime}:X_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{S}^{r+s}
is étale.
Let
\Delta
be the diagonal of
Z
over
\mathbb{A}_{S}^{r+s}
.
This is a closed subscheme of
Z\times_{\mathbb{A}_{S}^{r+s}}Z
,
and we define

X_{2}:=X\times_{\mathbb{A}_{S}^{r+s}}X_{1}-(X\times_{\mathbb{A}_{S}^{r+s}}Z-\Delta)\cup(Z\times_{\mathbb{A}_{S}^{r+s}}X_{1}-\Delta).

By
[
28
, Lemma 4.5.6]
,
\Delta
is a closed subscheme of
X_{2}
, and
X_{2}
is an open subscheme of
X\times_{\mathbb{A}_{S}^{r+s}}X_{1}
.
Let
i_{2}:Z\simeq\Delta\rightarrow X_{2}
be the closed immersion.
There are cartesian excisive morphisms of closed pairs smooth over
S

(X,Z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(u,{\rm id})}}{{\longleftarrow}}(X_{2},Z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(v,{\rm id})}}{{\longrightarrow}}(X_{1},Z)=(\mathbb{A}^{s}_{Z},Z),

(7.2.5)

where
u
(resp.
v
) denotes the étale morphism induced by the projection

X\times_{\mathbb{A}_{S}^{r+s}}X_{1}\rightarrow X(\text{resp.}\ X\times_{\mathbb{A}_{S}^{r+s}}X_{1}\rightarrow X_{1}).

By working Zariski locally on
X
,
the above morphisms of closed pairs smooth over
S
allow us to reduce claims about
X
and
Z
to claims about
X_{1}
and
Z
.

Example 7.2.9
.

If
Z=S
, then
r=0
and
X_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{S}^{r+s}=\mathbb{A}^{s}_{S}
is an isomorphism.
Thus
X_{2}\simeq X
, and the morphism
v:X_{2}\rightarrow X_{1}
is the parametrization map
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{S}^{s}
.

Theorem 7.2.10
.

Assume that
\mathcal{T}
satisfies
(
Zar
-sep)
,
(
\overline{\Box}
-inv)
,
(
sNis
-des)
, and
(
div
-des)
.
Let
Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Y
be a morphism in
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}l_{Sm}/S
.
Then

M(Y^{\prime})\rightarrow M(Y)

(7.2.6)

is an isomorphism.

Démonstration.

We may assume that
Y^{\prime}
is an admissible blow-up of
Y
along a connected codimension
d
smooth center
Z
for some integer
d\geq 2
.
We set
X:=\underline{Y}
and
X^{\prime}:=\underline{Y^{\prime}}
. Write
Z^{\prime}
for the exceptional divisor,
Z^{\prime}:=Z\times_{X}X^{\prime}
.
Let
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{r}
be divisors on
X
smooth over
S
such that
\partial Y=Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{r}
.
We write
Z_{i}^{\prime}
for the strict transform of
Z_{i}
in the blow-up
X^{\prime}
,
and set

Y:=(X,Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{r}),\;Y^{\prime}:=(X^{\prime},Z_{1}^{\prime}+\cdots+Z_{r}^{\prime}+Z^{\prime}).

Let
W_{i}:=\mathbb{A}^{i-1}\times\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})\times\mathbb{A}^{n-i}
denote the smooth divisor of
\mathbb{A}^{n}
given by
y_{i}=0
for
1\leq i\leq n
, where
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})
are coordinates on
\mathbb{A}^{n}
.
Write
C_{1},\ldots,C_{l}
for the irreducible components of the intersections
Z\cap(Z_{i_{1}}\ldots\cap Z_{i_{k}})
, where
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})
is a subset of
(1,\ldots,r)
of length
k
,
for
k=1,\ldots,r
.
Finally we set

X(s):={\rm max}\{c_{1},\ldots,c_{l}\},

(7.2.7)

where
c_{i}
is the codimension of
C_{i}
in
X
.

Next, observe the question is Zariski local on
X
,
owing to (
Zar
-sep),
so that we may assume there exists an étale morphism
u:X\rightarrow S\times\mathbb{A}^{n}
in
Sm/S
for which

(i)

u^{-1}(S\times W_{i})=Z_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq r
,

(ii)

u^{-1}(S\times W)=Z
, where

W:=\mathbb{A}^{r-p}\times(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}))^{p}\times(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}))^{q}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-r-q}

for some
1\leq p\leq r
and
0\leq q\leq n-r
.

Note that
W
is a smooth closed subscheme of codimension
p+q
in
\mathbb{A}^{n}
having strict normal crossing with
W_{1}+\ldots+W_{i}
for every
i=1,\ldots,n
.

Assume that
S=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
for simplicity of notation (the general case is deduced from this one by base change
S\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
).
For
r+1\leq i\leq r+q
we set

Z_{i}:=u^{-1}(W_{i}),\quad T:=u^{-1}((\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}))^{r+q}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-r-q}).

Note that
T\subseteq Z
and that
d=p+q
is the codimension of
Z
in
X
.
Moreover, Zariski-locally, the codimension of
T
in
X
agrees with
X(s)
defined in (
).
We also observe that (
7.2.6
) is trivially an isomorphism if
\dim X=q=r=0
.
Similarly, if
X(s)=1
, then
Z=T
is a divisor in
X
and coincides with one of the components of
\partial X
.
Thus the blow-up is trivially an isomorphism.
We can now proceed by induction on
X(s)>1
.

Step 1

Reduction to the case when
X=\mathbb{A}^{p+q}
and
r=p
.

Starting with a smooth pair
(X,T)
and
(X,T)\rightarrow(\mathbb{A}^{n},\mathbb{A}^{n-r-q})
,
we may use the technique of Construction
7.2.8
with respect to the parametrization
u\colon(X,T)\to(\mathbb{A}^{n},\mathbb{A}^{n-r-q})
to form the schemes
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
.
In this case
X_{1}=\mathbb{A}^{r+q}_{T}
,

X_{2}
is open in
X\times_{\mathbb{A}^{n}}X_{1}
,
and there exist étale morphisms
X_{2}\to X
,
X_{2}\to X_{1}
.

Let
X^{\prime}_{2}
be the pullback
X^{\prime}\times_{X}X_{2}
.
We equip
X_{2}
(resp.
X_{2}^{\prime}
) with the log structure induced by
Y
(resp.
Y^{\prime}
) and set

Y_{2}:=Y\times_{X}X_{2},\;\;Y_{2}^{\prime}:=Y^{\prime}\times_{X}X_{2}.

Next, we consider the open complement of
T
in
X

U:=(X-T,(Z_{1}-T)+\cdots+(Z_{r}-T)),

and the pullbacks

U^{\prime}:=U\times_{Y}Y^{\prime},\;\;U_{2}:=U\times_{X}X_{2},\;\;U_{2}^{\prime}:=U^{\prime}\times_{X}X_{2}.

We obtain strict Nisnevich distinguished squares in
\mathscr{S}/S

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{2}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{2}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}.}

By (
sNis
-des) there are isomorphisms

M(U_{2}\rightarrow U)\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(Y_{2}\rightarrow Y),\;M(U_{2}^{\prime}\rightarrow U^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(Y_{2}^{\prime}\rightarrow Y^{\prime}).

(7.2.8)

Observe that by removing
T
, we obtain strict inequalities

U(s)<X(s),\quad U_{2}(s)<X(s).

Thus, by induction, there are isomorphisms

M(U_{2}^{\prime}\rightarrow U_{2})\simeq M(U^{\prime}\rightarrow U)\simeq 0,

which by Proposition
C.1.7
imply the isomorphism

M(U_{2}^{\prime}\rightarrow U^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(U_{2}\rightarrow U).

(7.2.9)

Combining (
7.2.8
) and (
7.2.9
), we reduce to showing

M(Y_{2}^{\prime}\rightarrow Y_{2})\simeq 0.

Here we can replace
X
with
X_{2}
.
Similarly, we can repeat the argument using the right excisive square in (
) and replace
X_{2}
by
X_{1}
.
We note that
X_{1}=\mathbb{A}^{r+q}\times T
and that the subschemes
Z_{i}
and
Z
of
X
correspond to the subschemes

\mathbb{A}^{i-1}\times\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})\times\mathbb{A}^{r+q-i}\times T\text{ and }\mathbb{A}^{r-p}\times(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}))^{p}\times(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}))^{q}\times T.

Thus we have reduced to the case

X=\mathbb{A}^{r+q}\times T,\;\;Z_{i}=\mathbb{A}^{i-1}\times\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})\times\mathbb{A}^{r+q-i}\times T,

(7.2.10)

Z=\mathbb{A}^{r-p}\times(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}))^{p}\times(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}))^{q}\times T.

Note that under (
), we have further an identification

Y\simeq\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{N}}^{r-p}\times\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{N}}^{p}\times\mathbb{A}^{q}\times T.

Using the monoidal structure in
\mathcal{T}
, we may further assume that
T=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
and
r=p
,
i.e.,

X=\mathbb{A}^{p+q},\;\;Z_{i}=\mathbb{A}^{i-1}\times\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})\times\mathbb{A}^{p+q-i},\;\;Z=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}),

and

Y\simeq\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{N}}^{p}\times\mathbb{A}^{q}.

Step 2

Cases when
d=2
.
If
p=2
and
q=0
, then the morphism

(X^{\prime},Z_{1}^{\prime}+\cdots+Z_{r}^{\prime}+Z^{\prime})\rightarrow(X,Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{r})

in
\mathcal{T}
is a log modification since this is
\mathbb{A}_{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{N}}^{p}
,
where
M
is the star subdivision of
\mathbb{N}^{p}
relative to itself as in Definition
A.3.14
.
Thus we are done by (
div
-des).
The case when
p=q=1
is already done in Proposition
7.2.5
.
Together with Step 1, we see that

M(Y^{\prime})\rightarrow M(Y)

is an isomorphism for general
Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Y
if
d=2
.

Step 3

General case.
Recall that we may assume

X=\mathbb{A}^{p+q},\;\;Z_{i}=\mathbb{A}^{i-1}\times\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})\times\mathbb{A}^{p+q-i},\;\;Z=(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}))^{p}\times(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}))^{q}.

We will construct
X^{\prime}
from
X
by suitable blow-ups and blow-downs along codimension
2
smooth centers.

Let
\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{p+q}\}
denote the standard basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{p+q}
.
Starting with
\Sigma_{0}:=\mathbb{N}^{p+q}
, for
i=1,\ldots,p+q-1
we inductively let
\Sigma_{i}
be the star subdivision
\Sigma_{i-1}^{*}(\eta_{i+1})
,
where

\eta_{i}:={\rm Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{i}).

Let
\Sigma_{i}^{\prime}
be the fan consists of cones
\sigma\in\Sigma_{i}
such that

\sigma\subset{\rm Cone}(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{p},e_{1},e_{1}+e_{2},\ldots,e_{1}+\cdots+e_{i}).

Let
\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}
be shorthand for
\Sigma_{0}^{*}(\eta_{p+q})
.
For
i=1,\ldots,p+q-2
, we inductively define
\Gamma_{i}^{\prime}
to be the star subdivision
\Gamma_{i}^{\prime*}(\eta_{i+1})
.
We let
\Gamma_{i}^{\prime}
be the fan consisting of cones
\sigma\in\Gamma_{i}^{\prime}
such that

\sigma\subset{\rm Cone}(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{p},e_{1},e_{1}+e_{2},\ldots,e_{1}+\cdots+e_{i},e_{1}+\cdots+e_{p+q}).

Note that
(\Sigma_{p+q-1},\Sigma_{p+q-1}^{\prime})=(\Gamma_{p+q-2},\Gamma_{p+q-2}^{\prime})
.
We have naturally induced maps

(\Sigma_{p+q-1},\Sigma_{p+q-1}^{\prime})\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow(\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{0}^{\prime}),\;(\Gamma_{p+q-2},\Gamma_{p+q-2}^{\prime})\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow(\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}).

The following figure illustrates the fans in the case when
p=2
and
q=1
.

(\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{0}^{\prime})
(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{1}^{\prime})
(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{2}^{\prime})=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{1}^{\prime})
(\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{0}^{\prime})
e_{1}
e_{2}
e_{3}
\longleftarrow
\longleftarrow
\longrightarrow

From the description of admissible blow-ups in Example
7.2.4
, we see that all the induced morphisms

\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{p+q-1},\Sigma_{p+q-1}^{\prime})}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{0}^{\prime})},\;\mathbb{A}_{(\Gamma_{p+q-2},\Gamma_{p+q-2}^{\prime})}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{(\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{0}^{\prime})}

are admissible blow-ups along a codimension two smooth center.
Step 2 shows that the induced morphisms

\displaystyle M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{p+q-1},\Sigma_{p+q-1}^{\prime})})\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{0}^{\prime})}),

\displaystyle M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Gamma_{p+q-2},\Gamma_{p+q-2}^{\prime})})\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{0}^{\prime})})

are isomorphisms.
This implies there is a naturally induced isomorphism

M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{0}^{\prime})})\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{0}^{\prime})}),

which can be identified with

M(Y^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(Y).

∎

Definition 7.2.11
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}l_{div}/S
denote the smallest class of proper birational morphisms in
lSm/S

that is closed under composition and contains
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}l_{Sm}/S
together with all the dividing coverings.

Proposition 7.2.12
.

Assume that
\mathcal{T}
satisfies
(
Zar
-sep)
,
(
\overline{\Box}
-inv)
,
(
sNis
-des)
, and
(
div
-des)
.
Let
f:Y\rightarrow X
be a morphism in
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}l_{div}/S
.
Then there is a naturally induced isomorphism

M(Y)\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(X).

Démonstration.

If
f
is a dividing cover, then apply (
div
-des).
If
f
is in
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}l_{Sm}/S
, then apply Theorem
7.2.10
.
Since any morphism in
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}l_{div}/S
is a composition of dividing covers and morphisms in
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}l_{Sm}/S
, we are done.
∎

Proposition 7.2.13
.

For
X\in SmlSm/S
and
Y\rightarrow X\in\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}l_{div}/S
,
there exists a log modification
Z\rightarrow Y
such that the composite morphism
Z\rightarrow X
is in
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}l_{Sm}/S
.

Démonstration.

Without loss of generality, we may assume
f
is a dividing cover.
Proposition
A.11.9
shows that
f
is a log modification.
Owing to Theorem
A.14.12
the morphism
f
is dominated by a sequence of log modifications
X_{n}\to\ldots\to X_{1}\to X
along a smooth center, see Definition
A.14.10
.
This suffices to conclude the proof.
∎

7.3.
Blow-up triangles and
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
-invariance

Throughout this section we shall assume that the triangulated category
\mathcal{T}
satisfies the properties (
Zar
-sep), (
\overline{\Box}
-inv), (
sNis
-des), and (
div
-des).
Our aim is to conclude that (
(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})
-inv) holds.
As in the formulation of (
(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})
-inv),
recall that we write

[x_{0}:\cdots:x_{n}]

for the coordinates on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
and view
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}=H_{n}
as the hyperplane defined by
x_{n}=0
.
The following result is a direct consequence of Theorem
7.2.10
.

Proposition 7.3.1
.

For every
X\in\mathscr{S}/S
the projection
X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})\rightarrow X
induces an isomorphism

M(X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1}))\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(X).

Démonstration.

Owing to the isomorphism

M(X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1}))\simeq M(X)\otimes M(\mathbb{P}^{n}\times S,\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\times S),

it suffices to consider the case
X=S
.
We proceed by induction on
n
.
The case
n=0
is trivial, and the case
n=1
is precisely the (
\overline{\Box}
-inv) property, so we may assume
n>1
.
For the standard coordinates
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
on
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
,
recall that
\mathbb{P}^{n}
is the toric variety associated to a fan
\Sigma_{1}
with the maximal cones

\operatorname{Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}),

\operatorname{Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{i-1},e_{i+1},\ldots,e_{n},-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{n})\quad(1\leq i\leq n-1),

\tau:=\operatorname{Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n-1},-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{n}).

We shall compare
\mathbb{P}^{n}
with
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
Both of these schemes are dominated by a common blow-up, which we describe next.
Consider the star subdivision

\Sigma_{3}:=(\Sigma_{1}^{*}(\tau^{\prime}))^{*}(\tau),

where

\tau^{\prime}:=\operatorname{Cone}(e_{n},-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{n}).

The maximal cones of
\Sigma_{3}
are given by

\sigma_{1}:=\operatorname{Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}),

\sigma_{i2}:=\operatorname{Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{i-1},e_{i+1},\ldots,e_{n},-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{n-1})\quad(1\leq i\leq n-1),

\sigma_{i3}:=\operatorname{Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{i-1},e_{i+1},\ldots,e_{n-1},-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{n},-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{n-1})\quad(1\leq i\leq n-1),

\sigma_{i4}:=\operatorname{Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{i-1},e_{i+1},\ldots,e_{n},-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{n},-e_{n})\quad(1\leq i\leq n-1),

\sigma_{5}:=\operatorname{Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n-1},-e_{n}).

Moreover,

\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
is the toric variety corresponding to a fan
\Sigma_{2}
with the maximal cones

\sigma_{1},\;\sigma_{5},

\sigma_{i2}\quad(1\leq i\leq n-1),

\sigma_{i6}:=\operatorname{Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{i-1},e_{i+1},\ldots,e_{n-1},-e_{n},-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{n-1})\quad(1\leq i\leq n-1).

Since the maximal cones of the star subdivision
(\sigma_{i6})^{*}(\tau^{\prime\prime})
are
\sigma_{i3}
and
\sigma_{i4}
,
where
\tau^{\prime\prime}:=\operatorname{Cone}(-e_{n},-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{n-1})
,
we observe that
\Sigma_{3}=\Sigma_{2}^{*}(\tau^{\prime\prime})
.

Form the following fans

\displaystyle\Sigma_{1}^{\prime}:=\{{\rm Cone}(-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{n})\},\;\Sigma_{2}^{\prime}:=\{\tau^{\prime\prime}\},

\displaystyle\Sigma_{3}^{\prime}:=\{{\rm Cone}(-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{n},-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{n-1}),{\rm Cone}(-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{n},-e_{n})\}.

When
n=2
, the following figure describes the above fans.

(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{1}^{\prime})
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
(\Sigma_{3},\Sigma_{3}^{\prime})
(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{2}^{\prime})

The scheme associated with the left-hand drawing is
(\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathbb{P}^{1})
, where
\mathbb{P}^{1}
is the line at infinity
x_{2}=0
.
The middle drawing is the blow-up of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
at
(\infty,\infty)
with log structure given by the exceptional divisor,
indicated by the diagonal line,
together with the strict transforms of
\infty\times\mathbb{P}^{1}+\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\infty
(cf. the proof of Proposition
7.2.5
).
Finally, the right-hand drawing is
\overline{\Box}\times\overline{\Box}
.
Note that the blow-up dominates both
\overline{\Box}\times\overline{\Box}
and
(\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathbb{P}^{1})
.
From the description of admissible blow-ups along smooth centers discussed in Example
7.2.4
,
we observe that the morphisms

\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{1}^{\prime})}\leftarrow\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{3},\Sigma_{3}^{\prime})}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{2}^{\prime})}

are admissible blow-ups along smooth centers.
More precisely, the morphism

\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{3},\Sigma_{3}^{\prime})}\to\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{2}^{\prime})}

is a dividing cover, while the morphism

\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{3},\Sigma_{3}^{\prime})}\to\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{1}^{\prime})}

is an admissible blow-up, albeit not a dividing cover.

Theorem
7.2.10
shows there are naturally induced isomorphisms

M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{1}^{\prime})}\times S)\xleftarrow{\simeq}M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{3},\Sigma_{3}^{\prime})}\times S)\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{2}^{\prime})}\times S).

Since we have

\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{1}^{\prime})}\simeq(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})\text{ and }\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{2}^{\prime})}\simeq(\mathbb{P}^{n-1},\mathbb{P}^{n-2})\times\overline{\Box},

there is a naturally induced isomorphism

M((\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})\times S)\simeq M((\mathbb{P}^{n-1},\mathbb{P}^{n-2})\times S)\otimes M(\overline{\Box}\times S)

Using induction the above implies there is an isomorphism

M(\mathbb{P}^{n}\times S,\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\times S)\simeq M(\overline{\Box}\times S)^{\otimes n}.

This finishes the proof by appealing to the property (
\overline{\Box}
-inv).
∎

Proposition 7.3.2
.

Suppose
Y
is a
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
-bundle over
X\in\mathscr{S}/S
.
Then the projection
Y\rightarrow X
induces an isomorphism

M(Y)\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(X).

Démonstration.

As in the proof of Proposition
7.1.10
by using induction on the number of open subsets in a trivialization of
Y
, we may assume
Y\simeq X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
.
We conclude by reference to Proposition
7.3.1
.
∎

In the following theorem,
we compare the relative motive
M(Z\to X)
for a smooth closed pair
(X,Z)
with the relative motive of the blow-up of
X
along
Z
.
The result would be automatic in the presence of the localization property, which does not hold in our log setting (cf. the example in Section
7.5
).

Theorem 7.3.3
.

Let
(X,Z)
be a closed pair smooth over
S
, and let
X^{\prime}
be the blow-up of
X
along a smooth center
Z
.
For
Z^{\prime}:=Z\times_{X}X^{\prime}
there is a naturally induced isomorphism

M(Z^{\prime}\rightarrow X^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(Z\rightarrow X).

Démonstration.

Note that both
X
and
Z
have a trivial log structure.
By Proposition
C.1.7
, it suffices to show there is a naturally induced isomorphism

M(Z^{\prime}\rightarrow Z)\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(X^{\prime}\rightarrow X).

This question is Zariski local on
X
by (
Zar
-sep),
so that we may assume there exists an étale morphism
u:X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r+s}\times S
of schemes for which
u^{-1}(W\times S)=Z
,
where
W
denotes the subscheme
(\operatorname{Spec}(Z))^{r}\times\mathbb{A}^{s}
of
\mathbb{A}^{r+s}
.
Using
u
and
Z\hookrightarrow X
,
we obtain schemes
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
over
k
as in Construction
7.2.8
, together with the corresponding excisive squares.

In
\mathscr{S}/S
, there are strict Nisnevich distinguished squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}-Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X-Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}^{\prime}-Z^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}-Z^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}

where
X_{2}^{\prime}:=X_{2}\times_{X}X^{\prime}
(recall that
Z\simeq\Delta\hookrightarrow X_{2}
).
Property (
sNis
-des) implies there are isomorphisms

M((X_{2}-Z)\rightarrow(X-Z))\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(X_{2}\rightarrow X),

and

M((X_{2}^{\prime}-Z^{\prime})\rightarrow(X^{\prime}-Z^{\prime}))\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(X_{2}^{\prime}\rightarrow X^{\prime}).

On account of the identifications
X_{2}-Z\simeq X_{2}^{\prime}-Z^{\prime}
and
X-Z\simeq X^{\prime}-Z^{\prime}
, there is an isomorphism

M(X_{2}^{\prime}\rightarrow X^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(X_{2}\rightarrow X).

By Proposition
C.1.7
we find the isomorphism

M(X_{2}^{\prime}\rightarrow X_{2})\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(X^{\prime}\rightarrow X).

Similarly,
letting
X_{1}^{\prime}
denote the blow-up of
X_{1}
along
Z
,
there is a naturally induced isomorphism

M(X_{2}^{\prime}\rightarrow X_{2})\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(X_{1}^{\prime}\rightarrow X_{1}).

By the above, we may replace
X
with
X_{1}
and assume
X=\mathbb{A}^{r}\times Z
.
Using the monoidal structure on
\mathcal{T}
, we can further reduce to the case
X=\mathbb{A}^{r}
and
Z=\{0\}
. Then
X^{\prime}
is
B_{0}(\mathbb{A}^{r})
and
Z^{\prime}=E
is the exceptional divisor.
Let
Y
be shorthand for
(\mathbb{P}^{n}\times S,H_{n}\times S)
,
where
H_{n}=\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
is the hyperplane at infinity.
Let
Y^{\prime}
be the object of
\mathscr{S}/S
with log structure induced by
Y
and whose underlying scheme is the blow-up of
\mathbb{P}^{n}
at the origin.

Using the open immersion
X\hookrightarrow Y
, we obtain strict Nisnevich distinguished squares in
\mathscr{S}/S

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X-Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y-Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}-Z^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}-Z^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}.}

(7.3.1)

By (
sNis
-des), there are isomorphisms

\displaystyle M((X-Z)\rightarrow(Y-Z))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(X\rightarrow Y),

\displaystyle M((X^{\prime}-Z^{\prime})\rightarrow(Y^{\prime}-Z^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(X^{\prime}\rightarrow Y^{\prime}).

Since
X^{\prime}-Z^{\prime}\simeq X-Z
and
Y^{\prime}-Z^{\prime}\simeq Y-Z
, we obtain an isomorphism

M(X^{\prime}\rightarrow Y^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(X\rightarrow Y).

Using Proposition
C.1.7
, and the relevant cube obtained by combining the two squares in (
), we get the isomorphism

M(X^{\prime}\rightarrow X)\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Y).

Hence it suffices to show there is a naturally induced isomorphism

M(Z^{\prime}\rightarrow Z)\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Y).

Due to Proposition
7.3.1
there is an isomorphism

M(Z)\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(Y),

given by the inverse of the projection
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})\to S
.
Hence we are reduced to consider
M(Z^{\prime})\rightarrow M(X^{\prime})
.
Lemma
7.1.7
shows that
X^{\prime}
is a
\overline{\Box}
-bundle over
\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n-1}
.
Thus by Proposition
7.3.2
, the projection
X^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n-1}
induces an isomorphism

M(X^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n-1}).

This concludes the proof since the composition
Z^{\prime}\rightarrow X^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n-1}
is an isomorphism of schemes.
∎

7.4.
Thom motives

Throughout this section we shall assume that
\mathcal{T}
satisfies the properties (
Zar
-sep), (
\overline{\Box}
-inv), (
sNis
-des), and (
div
-des).
Let
E
be a vector bundle over
X\in Sm/k
with
0
-section
Z
.
The Thom motive associated with
E
in
{\bf DM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
is defined as

M((E-Z)\rightarrow E).

In the log setting,
taking the open complement
E-Z
of the
0
-section in
E
does not give the “correct” homotopy type :
we can see this by taking the Betti realization of the log scheme, as discussed in
[
76
, V.1]
.
See also Proposition
7.4.4
below.
In the following, we will discuss an appropriate definition and then prove some properties of our Thom motives.

Definition 7.4.1
.

Suppose
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{r}
form a strict normal crossing divisor on
X\in Sm/S
, and that a smooth closed subscheme
Z
has strict normal crossing with
Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{r}
over
S
.
For
Y:=(X,Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{r})
the blow-up of
Y
along
Z
is defined as

B_{Z}Y:=(B_{Z}X,W_{1}+\cdots+W_{r}),

where
B_{Z}X
is the blow-up of
X
along
Z
, and
W_{i}
is the strict transform of
Z_{i}
.
If
E
is the corresponding exceptional divisor on
X
, then there is a naturally induced morphism of schemes

B_{Z}X-(E\cup W_{1}\cup\cdots\cup W_{r})\rightarrow X-(Z_{1}\cup\cdots\cup Z_{r}).

Since the construction of compactifying log structure is functorial by
[
76
, §III.1.6]
, the above morphism induces a morphism of fs log schemes

(B_{Z}Y,E)\rightarrow Y.

See Definition
7.2.2
for the notation. By definition, we say that
(B_{Z}Y,E)
is the blow-up of
Y
along
Z
.
On the other hand, we do
not
have a naturally induced morphism of fs log schemes
B_{Z}Y\rightarrow Y
in general.

Regard
B_{Z}Y
as the strict transform of
Y\times\{0\}
in
B_{Z\times\{0\}}(Y\times\overline{\Box})
.
The deformation space of a pair
(Y,Z)
is defined as

D_{Z}Y:=B_{Z\times\{0\}}(Y\times\overline{\Box})-B_{Z}Y.

The strict transform of
Z\times\overline{\Box}
in
B_{Z\times\{0\}}(Y\times\overline{\Box})
does not intersect with
B_{Z}X
, so we may and will consider
Z\times\overline{\Box}
as a smooth divisor on
D_{Z}Y
.

Consider the normal bundle
p:N_{Z}X\rightarrow X
of
Z
in
X
.
The
normal bundle
of
Z
in
Y
is defined as

N_{Z}Y:=(N_{Z}X,p^{-1}(Z_{1})+\cdots+p^{-1}(Z_{r})).

Note that
N_{Z}Y\rightarrow Y
is a vector bundle.

Proposition 7.4.2
.

Suppose
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{r}
are divisors forming a strict normal crossing divisor on
X\in Sm/S
over
S
, and that
Z
has strict normal crossing with
Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{r}
over
S
.
Let
f:X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
be an étale morphism in
Sm/S
such that
Z^{\prime}:=f^{-1}(Z)\rightarrow Z
is an isomorphism,
and let
E
(resp.
E^{\prime}
) be the exceptional divisor on the blow-up
B_{Z}X
(resp.
B_{Z^{\prime}}X^{\prime}
).
Let

Y:=(X,Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{r}),\;Y^{\prime}:=(X^{\prime},Z_{1}^{\prime}+\cdots+Z_{r}^{\prime}),

where
Z_{i}^{\prime}
denotes the pullback of
Z_{i}
along
f
.
Then
f
induces an isomorphism

M((B_{Z^{\prime}}Y^{\prime},E^{\prime})\rightarrow Y^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\simeq}M((B_{Z}Y,E)\rightarrow Y).

Démonstration.

For notational convenience we set

U:=(X-Z,(Z_{1}-Z)+\cdots+(Z_{r}-Z)),\;U^{\prime}:=U\times_{Y}Y^{\prime}.

Since
X^{\prime}\amalg X-Z\rightarrow X
and
B_{Z^{\prime}}X^{\prime}\amalg X-Z\rightarrow B_{Z}X
are Nisnevich covers by assumption,
there are induced strict Nisnevich distinguished squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B_{Z^{\prime}}Y^{\prime},E^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B_{Z}Y,E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y.}

By passing to motives,
property (
sNis
-des) shows there are naturally induced isomorphisms

M(U^{\prime}\rightarrow U)\xrightarrow{\simeq}M((B_{Z^{\prime}}Y^{\prime},E^{\prime})\rightarrow(B_{Z}Y,E)),

M(U^{\prime}\rightarrow U)\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Y).

This finishes the proof by appealing to Proposition
C.1.7
and the isomorphism

M((B_{Z^{\prime}}Y^{\prime},E^{\prime})\rightarrow(B_{Z}Y,E))\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Y).

∎

Associated to vector bundles over fs log schemes, we have the fundamental notion of a Thom motive ;
this plays a crucial role in the theory of log motives.

Definition 7.4.3
.

Suppose
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{r}
form a strict normal crossing divisor on
X\in Sm/S
.
For
Y:=(X,Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{r})
and a vector bundle
\xi:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow Y
of fixed rank, let
Z
be the
0
-section of
\mathcal{E}
.
Then

\xi^{-1}(Z_{1}),\ldots,\xi^{-1}(Z_{r})

form a strict normal crossing divisor on
\underline{\mathcal{E}}
, and
Z
has strict normal crossing with
\xi^{-1}(Z_{1})+\cdots+\xi^{-1}(Z_{r})
over
S
.
Let
E
be the exceptional divisor on the blow-up
B_{Z}(\mathcal{E})
.
The
Thom motive
of
\mathcal{E}
over
X
is defined as

MTh_{X}(\mathcal{E}):=M((B_{Z}(\mathcal{E}),E)\rightarrow\mathcal{E}).

We often omit the subscript
X
in the notation when no confusion seems likely to arise.

According to the following proposition,
the Betti realization of the motivic Thom space is homotopy equivalent to the quotient of the unit disk bundle by the unit sphere bundle,
which is a formulation of Thom spaces in algebraic topology.

Proposition 7.4.4
.

Suppose
X
is a separated scheme over
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{C})
, and let
\xi:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow X
be a vector bundle.
Then the Betti realization of
\mathcal{E}
(resp.
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}:=(B_{Z}(\mathcal{E}),E)
) is homotopy equivalent to a unit disk bundle (resp. unit sphere bundle).

Démonstration.

The question is Zariski local on
X
, so we may assume that
\xi
is a trivial rank
n
vector bundle.
Consider the fs log scheme
Y:=(B_{\{0\}}\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{C}}^{n},D)
where
D
is the exceptional divisor.
Then by Proposition
A.9.2
, the Betti realization of
Y
is homotopy equivalent to

(Y-\partial Y)_{log}\simeq(\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{C}}^{r}-\{0\})_{an}\simeq S^{2r-1},

where
S^{i}
denotes the
i
-dimensional unit sphere.
Recall the Betti realization of
\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{C}}^{r}
is homotopy equivalent to the
2r
-dimensional unit disk
D^{2r}
.
Since
\mathcal{E}\simeq X\times_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}\simeq X\times_{\mathbb{C}}Y
, we are done.
∎

We prove an analog of
[
68
, Proposition 2.17(3), p. 112]
as follows.
Note that our proof,
which involves the blow-up along the zero section of the vector bundle,
is different from the proof given by Morel-Voevodsky
[
68
]
.

Proposition 7.4.5
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a rank
n
vector bundle over
X
in
SmlSm/S
,
and view
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})
as the closed subscheme of
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O})
at infinity.
Then there is a canonical isomorphism

MTh(\mathcal{E})\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})\rightarrow\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O})).

In particular, if
n=1
and
\mathcal{E}
is trivial, then

MTh(X\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\simeq M(X)(1)[2].

Démonstration.

Let
Y
(resp.
Y^{\prime}
) be the blow-up of
\mathcal{E}
(resp.
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O})
) along the zero section
Z_{0}
, and let
E
(resp.
E^{\prime}
) be the exceptional divisor on
Y
(resp.
Y^{\prime}
).
View
\mathcal{E}
as the open subscheme
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O})-\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})
of
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})
.
Owing to (
sNis
-des) and Proposition
C.1.7
, the cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y,E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y^{\prime},E^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O})}

induces an isomorphism

MTh(\mathcal{E})=M((Y,E)\rightarrow\mathcal{E})\xrightarrow{\simeq}M((Y^{\prime},E^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O})).

(7.4.1)

Since
Z_{0}\cap\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})=\emptyset
, we can view
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})
as a divisor
Z
of
Y^{\prime}
not intersecting with
E^{\prime}
.

We claim the closed immersion
Z\rightarrow(Y^{\prime},E^{\prime})
induces an isomorphism

M(Z)\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(Y^{\prime},E^{\prime}).

Due to (
Zar
-sep), we reduce to the case when
\mathcal{E}
is trivial.
In this case, owing to Lemma
7.1.10
,
(Y^{\prime},E^{\prime})
is a
\overline{\Box}
-bundle over
\mathbb{P}_{X}^{n-1}
.
The composite morphism
Z\rightarrow(Y^{\prime},E^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathbb{P}_{X}^{n-1}
is an isomorphism.
Hence the composite morphism

M(Z)\rightarrow M(Y^{\prime},E^{\prime})\rightarrow M(\mathbb{P}_{X}^{n-1})

is also an isomorphism. The second morphism is an isomorphism by Proposition
7.3.2
.
Thus the first morphism is an isomorphism, which proves the claim.

From the above and Proposition
C.1.6
, we deduce the isomorphism

M((Y^{\prime},E^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O}))\simeq M(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})\rightarrow\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O})).

(7.4.2)

To conclude the proof we combine (
7.4.1
) and (
7.4.2
).
∎

Definition 7.4.6
.

For a smooth fan
\Sigma
with a cone
\sigma
, we have used the notation
\Sigma^{*}(\sigma)
, which is the star subdivison (see Definition
A.3.14
)
relative to
\sigma
.
If
x
is the center of
\sigma
, i.e.,
x=(a_{1}+\cdots+a_{n})
whenever
\sigma={\rm Cone}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
, we set

\Sigma^{*}\langle x\rangle:=\Sigma^{*}(\sigma).

In
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory,
the construction of the Thom space of a vector bundle
E
on
X
is compatible with the monoidal structure.
That is, if
E_{i}
is a vector bundle on
X_{i}
for
i=0,1
, there is a canonical isomorphism

Th_{X_{1}}(E_{1})\otimes Th_{X_{2}}(E_{2})\simeq Th_{X_{1}\times X_{2}}(E_{1}\times E_{2}).

This follows immediately from the construction, see
[
68
, Proposition 2.17 (1)]
.
Alas, in our setting, this property is less evident.
In the following, we address this problem for double and triple products of vector bundles.
This will be applied later in the proof of Proposition
7.4.17
,
see also Remark
7.4.18
.

Remark 7.4.7
.

Assume that
k
admits resolution of singularities as in Definition
7.6.3
.
In this case, the compatibility between Thom motives and products can be deduced a posteriori from the one in
{\bf DM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
,
provided the underlying scheme
\underline{X}
is proper.
This is a consequence of Proposition
8.2.10
.

Lemma 7.4.8
.

Let
X
be an irreducible separated scheme with two nowhere dense closed subschemes
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
.
We set
W_{1}:=Z_{1}\times_{X}B_{Z_{2}}X
and
W_{2}:=B_{Z_{2}}X\times_{X}Z_{1}
.
If
Y
is the closure of the generic point of
B_{Z_{1}}X\times_{X}B_{Z_{2}}X
whose image in
X
is the generic point, then there are isomorphisms

Y\simeq B_{W_{2}}(B_{Z_{1}}X)\simeq B_{W_{1}}(B_{Z_{2}}X).

Démonstration.

Due to
[
37
, Section B.6.9]
,
B_{W_{2}}(B_{Z_{1}}X)
is a closed subscheme of
B_{Z_{1}}X\times_{X}B_{Z_{2}}X
.
Since
B_{W_{2}}(B_{Z_{1}}X)
is proper and birational over
X
, we have
Y\simeq B_{W_{2}}(B_{Z_{1}}X)
.
We can similarly show
Y\simeq B_{W_{1}}(B_{Z_{2}}X)
.
∎

Lemma 7.4.9
.

Let
\Sigma
be a fan in
N
with two partial subdivisions
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
.
We set
\Sigma_{12}:=\Sigma_{1}\times_{\Sigma}\Sigma_{2}
, i.e.,

\Sigma_{12}=\{\sigma_{1}\cap\sigma_{2}:\sigma_{1}\in\Sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\Sigma_{2}\}.

If
Y
is the closure of the generic point
\xi
of
\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma_{1}}}\times_{\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma}}}\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma_{2}}}
whose image in
\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma}}
is the generic point, then there is an isomorphism

Y\simeq\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma_{12}}}.

Démonstration.

We only need to show this for cones of
\Sigma
,
\Sigma_{1}
, and
\Sigma_{2}
.
Hence we may assume

\Sigma=\operatorname{Spec}(P),\;\Sigma_{1}=\operatorname{Spec}(P_{1}),\;\Sigma_{2}=\operatorname{Spec}(P_{2}),

where
P
,
P_{1}
, and
P_{2}
are fs submonoids of the dual lattice
M
of
N
such that

M\simeq P^{\mathrm{gp}}\simeq P_{1}^{\mathrm{gp}}\simeq P_{2}^{\mathrm{gp}}.

There is an isomorphism

\underline{\mathbb{A}_{P_{1}}}\times_{\underline{\mathbb{A}_{P}}}\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{P}_{2}}}\simeq\underline{\mathbb{A}_{P_{1}\oplus_{P}^{\rm mon}P_{2}}},

where
P_{1}\oplus_{P}^{\rm mon}P_{2}
denotes the amalgamated sum in the category of monoids.
The amalgamated sum
P_{1}\oplus_{P}^{\rm int}P_{2}
in the category of integral monoids is the image of
P_{1}\oplus P_{2}
in
M
, i.e.,

P_{1}\oplus_{P}^{\rm int}P_{2}=P_{1}+P_{2}.

There is a naturally induced homomorphism

f\colon\mathbb{Z}[P_{1}\oplus_{P}^{\rm mon}P_{2}]\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}[M],

and since
Y
is the closure of
\xi
we observe that
Y=\operatorname{Spec}(\operatorname{Im}{f})
.
Thus

Y\simeq\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[P_{1}+P_{2}])

since
\operatorname{Im}{f}
agrees with
\mathbb{Z}[P_{1}+P_{2}]
.
The fan
\Sigma_{12}
has only one maximal cone
P_{1}^{\vee}\cap P_{2}^{\vee}
, which corresponds to
P_{1}+P_{2}
by duality.
This shows
Y\simeq\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma_{12}}}
.
∎

Construction 7.4.10
.

For
X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}\in Sm/S
and vector bundles
\mathcal{E}_{i}\rightarrow X_{i}
of fixed rank for
i=1,2,3
, we will for future usage construct 55 blow-ups of the triple product

T:=\mathcal{E}_{1}\times_{S}\mathcal{E}_{2}\times_{S}\mathcal{E}_{3}.

Let
Z_{i}
be the
0
-section of
\mathcal{E}_{i}
for
i=1,2,3
, and we set

\displaystyle W_{1}:=Z_{1}\times_{S}\mathcal{E}_{2}\times_{S}\mathcal{E}_{3},\;W_{2}:=\mathcal{E}_{1}\times_{S}Z_{2}\times_{S}\mathcal{E}_{3},\;W_{3}:=\mathcal{E}_{1}\times_{S}\mathcal{E}_{2}\times_{S}Z_{3},

\displaystyle W_{4}:=Z_{1}\times_{S}Z_{2}\times_{S}\mathcal{E}_{3},\;W_{5}:=\mathcal{E}_{1}\times_{S}Z_{2}\times_{S}Z_{3},\;W_{6}:=Z_{1}\times_{S}Z_{2}\times_{S}Z_{3}.

For every subset
I=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}\}\subset\{1,\ldots,6\}
with
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{n}
, consider the fiber product

B_{W_{i_{1}}}(T)\times_{T}\cdots\times_{T}B_{W_{i_{n}}}(T)

Collect all generic points whose image in
T
are also generic, and let
T_{I}
denote the union of the closures of these generic points.
Due to Lemma
7.4.8
if
j\notin I
, then
T_{I\amalg\{j\}}
is the blow-up of
T_{I}
along the preimage
W_{I,j}
of
W_{j}
in
T_{I}
.

We claim that
W_{I,j}
is smooth if

I\cap\{4,5,6\}=\{4,5\}.

This question is Zariski local on
X_{1}
,
X_{2}
, and
X_{3}
, so we may assume that
\mathcal{E}_{i}
is a trivial vector bundle of rank
p_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq 3
.
We set
n=p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3}
.
Let
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
be the standard coordinates in
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, and let
\Sigma
be the fan whose only maximal cone is
{\rm Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})
.
We define the following sets

\displaystyle S_{0}:=\emptyset,\;S_{1}:=\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{p_{1}}\},

\displaystyle S_{2}:=\{e_{p_{1}+1},\ldots,e_{p_{1}+p_{2}}\},\;S_{3}:=\{e_{p_{1}+p_{2}},\ldots,e_{n}\},

\displaystyle S_{4}:=S_{1}\cup S_{2},\;S_{5}:=S_{2}\cup S_{3},\;S_{6}:=S_{1}\cup S_{2}\cup S_{3}.

We set

\displaystyle f_{1}:=e_{1}+\cdots+e_{p_{1}},\;f_{2}:=e_{p_{1}+1}+\cdots+e_{p_{1}+p_{2}},\;f_{3}:=e_{p_{1}+p_{2}+1}+\cdots+e_{n},

\displaystyle f_{4}:=f_{1}+f_{2},\;f_{5}:=f_{2}+f_{3},\;f_{6}:=f_{1}+f_{2}+f_{3}.

For every subset
I=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}\}\subset\{1,\ldots,6\}
with
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{m}
we set

\Sigma_{I}:=\Sigma_{i_{1}}\times_{\Sigma}\cdots\times_{\Sigma}\Sigma_{i_{m}},

i.e.,

\Sigma_{I}=\{\sigma_{1}\cap\cdots\cap\sigma_{m}:\sigma_{1}\in\Sigma_{i_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{m}\in\Sigma_{m}\}.

By convention
\Sigma_{\emptyset}:=\Sigma
.
Due to Lemmas
7.4.8
and
7.4.9
there is an isomorphism

T_{I}\simeq\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma_{I}}}\times(X_{1}\times_{S}X_{2}\times_{S}X_{3}).

Hence it suffices to show that
\Sigma_{I\amalg\{j\}}
is a star subdivision of
\Sigma_{I}
relative to a smooth cone.
This will be done in Lemma
7.4.12
after proving several preliminary results.

For
u\in S_{1}
,
v\in S_{2}
,
w\in S_{3}
,
p\in\{0,1,4,6\}
,
q\in\{0,2,4,5,6\}
, and
r\in\{0,3,5,6\}
, we set

\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}:=\{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}:a_{u}\leq a_{i}\text{ if }i\in S_{p},\;a_{v}\leq a_{i}\text{ if }i\in S_{q},\;a_{w}\leq a_{i}\text{ if }i\in S_{r}\}.

Note that if
p=0
(resp.
q=0
, resp.
r=0
), then the condition
a_{u}\leq a_{i}
(resp.
a_{v}\leq a_{i}
resp.
a_{w}\leq a_{i}
) has no meaning.
For notational convenience, in this case we also write
u=*
(resp.
v=*
, resp.
w=*
).

If inequalities of the form
a_{i}\leq a_{j}
and
a_{j}\leq a_{i}
enter in the condition for some
i\neq j
when forming
\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}
,
then
\dim\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}<n
.
By using this criterion, we can list all possible values of
(p,q,r)
such that
\dim\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}=n
, see Table

.
