List of all concise
(p,q,r)
.

We say that
(p,q,r)
is
concise
if it has a value as listed in the table.
A concise triple
(p,q,r)
is called
standard
if
\{4,5\}\not\subset\{p,q,r\}
.
For
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq t\leq 6
to simplify notation we set

f_{0}^{i}:=e_{i},\;f_{t}^{i}:=f_{t}.

If
(p,q,r)
is standard, then we can check by hand that

\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}={\rm Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{u-1},e_{u+1},\ldots,e_{v-1},e_{v+1},\ldots,e_{w-1},e_{w+1},\ldots,e_{n},f_{p}^{u},f_{q}^{v},f_{r}^{w}).

We regard
\{0,\ldots,6\}
as a partially ordered set whose order
\prec
is generated by

(0\prec 1,2,3),\;(1\prec 4,5),\;(2\prec 4,5,6),\;(3\prec 5,6),\;(4\prec 6),\;(5\prec 6).

We use the following shorthand notation :

\displaystyle p^{s}:=\sup\{p,s\}\text{ (if }s\in\{1,4,6\}),\;q^{s}:=\sup\{q,s\}\text{ (if }s\in\{2,4,5,6\}),

\displaystyle r^{s}:=\sup\{r,s\}\text{ (if }s\in\{3,5,6\}),

where
\sup
is taken with respect to the order
\prec
.
Note that

p^{s}=p\text{ or }s,\;q^{s}=q,\;s,\text{ or }6,\;r^{s}=r\text{ or }s.

(7.4.3)

If
p\neq 0
and
s\in\{1,4,6\}
, then we have

\begin{split}&\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}\cap\sigma_{u**}^{s00}\\
=&\{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}:a_{u}\leq a_{i}\text{ if }i\in S_{p}\cup S_{s},\;a_{v}\leq a_{i}\text{ if }i\in S_{q},\;a_{w}\leq a_{i}\text{ if }i\in S_{r}\}\\
=&\sigma_{uvw}^{p^{s}qr}.\end{split}

(7.4.4)

We similar have

\begin{split}\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}\cap\sigma_{*v*}^{0s0}=\sigma_{uvw}^{pq^{s}r}\text{ (if }q\neq 0),&\;\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}\cap\sigma_{**w}^{00s}=\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr^{s}}\text{ (if }r\neq 0),\\
\sigma_{*vw}^{0qr}\cap\sigma_{u**}^{s00}=\sigma_{uvw}^{sqr},\;\sigma_{u*w}^{p0r}\cap\sigma_{*v*}^{0s0}&=\sigma_{uvw}^{psr},\;\sigma_{uv*}^{pq0}\cap\sigma_{**w}^{00s}=\sigma_{uvw}^{pqs}\end{split}

(7.4.5)

for all appropriate
s
.
Moreover, we have

\dim\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}\cap\sigma_{u^{\prime}**}^{s00}<n\text{ if }u\neq u^{\prime},\;p\neq 0,\;s\in\{1,4,6\}

(7.4.6)

since the inequalities
a_{u}\leq a_{u^{\prime}}
and
a_{u^{\prime}}\leq a_{u}
are appeared.
Similarly, we have

\begin{split}\dim\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}\cap\sigma_{*v^{\prime}*}^{0s0}<n&\text{ if }v\neq v^{\prime},\;q\neq 0,\;s\in\{2,4,5,6\},\\
\dim\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}\cap\sigma_{**w^{\prime}}^{00s}<n&\text{ if }w\neq w^{\prime},\;r\neq 0,\;s\in\{3,5,6\}.\end{split}

(7.4.7)

Lemma 7.4.11
.

With notations as above,
let
I
be a subset of
\{1,\ldots,6\}
such that

I\cap\{4,5,6\}\neq\{4,5\}.

Then any
n
-dimensional cone of
\Sigma_{I}
can be written as
\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}
for some standard triple
(p,q,r)
.

Démonstration.

Suppose
s\in\{1,\ldots,6\}-I
and
(I\amalg\{s\})\cap\{4,5,6\}\neq\{4,5\}
.
Any
n
-dimensional cone of
\Sigma_{I\amalg\{s\}}
is of the form
\sigma\cap\sigma^{\prime}
for some
\sigma\in\Sigma_{I}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{s}
.
Thus from (
7.4.3
), (
7.4.4
), (
7.4.5
), (
7.4.6
), and (
7.4.7
),
we observe that any
n
-dimensional cone of
\Sigma_{I\amalg\{s\}}
takes the form
\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}
with
p,q,r\in I\amalg\{0,s\}
if any maximal cone of
\Sigma_{I}

can be written as
\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}
with
p,q,r\in I\amalg\{0\}
.
Now use induction on the number of elements of
I
to deduce this holds for all
I
satisfying
I\cap\{4,5,6\}\neq\{4,5\}
.

Let
\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}
be an
n
-dimensional cone of
\Sigma_{I}
.
If
(p,q,r)
is not concise, then
\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}
is not an
n
-dimensional cone.
Thus
(p,q,r)
is concise.
If
(p,q,r)
is not standard, then
\{4,5\}\in\{p,q,r\}
.
We have shown in the above paragraph that this implies
\{4,5\}\in I
.
From the condition
I\cap\{4,5,6\}\neq\{4,5\}
we have
6\in I
.
Then there are six cases according to Table
1
:

\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}=\sigma_{uvw}^{450},\;\sigma_{uvw}^{453},\;\sigma_{uvw}^{405},\;\sigma_{uvw}^{425},\;\sigma_{uvw}^{045},\;\sigma_{uvw}^{145}.

In the first and second (resp. third and fourth, resp. fifth and sixth) cases,
a_{u},a_{v}\notin\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}
(resp.
a_{u},a_{w}\notin\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}
, resp.
a_{v},a_{w}\notin\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}
).
Thus in all cases,
\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}
is not contained in any of

\sigma_{u^{\prime}**}^{600},\;\sigma_{*v^{\prime}*}^{060},\;\sigma_{**w^{\prime}}^{060}.

This contradicts the fact that
6\in I
.
Thus
(p,q,r)
is standard.
∎

Lemma 7.4.12
.

With notations as above,
let
I
be a subset of
\{1,\ldots,6\}
, and let
s
be an element of
\{1,\ldots,6\}-I
.
If

\{4,5,6\}\cap I\neq\{4,5\}\text{ and }\{4,5,6\}\cap(I\amalg\{s\})\neq\{4,5\},

then

\Sigma_{I\amalg\{s\}}=\Sigma_{I}^{*}(\tau),

where
\tau
is the cone of
\Sigma_{I}
generated by
f_{s}
.

Démonstration.

Let
\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}
be a maximal cone of
\Sigma_{I}
.
Lemma
7.4.11
shows that
(p,q,r)
is standard.
Suppose we have

\{\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}\}\times_{\Sigma}\Sigma_{j}\neq\{\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}\}.

Due to (
7.4.4
), (
7.4.5
), (
7.4.6
), and (
7.4.7
), its
n
-dimensional cones are of the following forms :

\displaystyle\sigma_{uvw}^{sqr}\text{ (if }p\neq 0\text{ and }s\neq 2,3,5),\;\sigma_{uvw}^{psr}\text{ (if }q\neq 0\text{ and }s\neq 1,3),

\displaystyle\sigma_{uvw}^{pqs}\text{ (if }q\neq 0\text{ and }s\neq 1,2,4),\;\sigma_{1vw}^{sqr},\ldots,\sigma_{p_{1}vw}^{sqr}\text{ (if }p=0\text{ and }s\neq 2,3,5),

\displaystyle\sigma_{u(p_{1}+1)w}^{psr},\ldots,\sigma_{u(p_{1}+p_{2})w}^{psr}\text{ (if }q=0\text{ and }s\neq 1,3),

\displaystyle\sigma_{uv(p_{1}+p_{2}+1)}^{pqs}\ldots,\sigma_{uvn}^{pqs}\text{ (if }r=0\text{ and }s\neq 1,2,4).

Note that some of these cones can have dimension
<n
.

Suppose
\sigma_{uvw}^{sqr}=\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}\cap\sigma_{u**}^{s00}
is a cone in
\{\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}\}\times_{\Sigma}\Sigma_{j}
and has dimension
n
with
s\in\{1,4,6\}
and
p\prec s
.
Lemma
7.4.11
shows that
(s,q,r)
is standard.
Then

\displaystyle\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}={\rm Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{u-1},e_{u+1},\ldots,e_{v-1},e_{v+1},\ldots,e_{w-1},e_{w+1},\ldots,e_{n},f_{p}^{u},f_{q}^{v},f_{r}^{w}),

\displaystyle\sigma_{uvw}^{sqr}={\rm Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{u-1},e_{u+1},\ldots,e_{v-1},e_{v+1},\ldots,e_{w-1},e_{w+1},\ldots,e_{n},f_{s},f_{q}^{v},f_{r}^{w}).

As a particular case, we have

\sigma_{*vw}^{0qr}={\rm Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{u-1},e_{u+1},\ldots,e_{v-1},e_{v+1},\ldots,e_{w-1},e_{w+1},\ldots,e_{n},e_{u},f_{q}^{v},f_{r}^{w}).

Since
\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}
contains
\sigma_{uvw}^{sqr}
, we have
f_{s}\in\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}
.
Let
\tau
be the subcone of
\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}
generated by
f_{s}
, and
let
\{d_{1},\ldots,d_{l}\}
be the generator of
\tau
.
Since
f_{s}\in\tau
, it follows that
\{f_{s},d_{1},\ldots,d_{l}\}
is linearly independent.
It follows that if
f_{p}^{u}\notin\tau
, then
\dim\sigma_{uvw}^{sqr}<n
.
This contradicts the fact that
(s,q,r)
is standard.
In conclusion, we have :

(Rule 1)

To obtain
\sigma_{uvw}^{sqr}
from
\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}
replace
f_{p}^{u}
by
f_{s}
.
To obtain
\sigma_{uvw}^{sqr}
from
\sigma_{*vw}^{0qr}
replace
e_{u}
by
f_{s}
.

(Rule 2)

f_{p}^{u}
should be in
\tau
.

We have similar rules for
\sigma_{uvw}^{psr}
and
\sigma_{uvw}^{pqs}
also under similar assumptions.

If
{\rm Cone}(\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{t})

is an arbitrary cone with the star subdivision
\Gamma
relative to a subcone
{\rm Cone}(\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{t^{\prime}})
, then we have :

(Rule 1’)

To obtain a maximal cone of
\Gamma
replace
\varepsilon_{i}
by
\varepsilon_{1}+\cdots+\varepsilon_{t^{\prime}}
.

(Rule 2’)

\varepsilon_{i}
should be in
{\rm Cone}(\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{t^{\prime}})
.

Compare (Rule 1) and (Rule 2) with (Rule 1’) and (Rule 2’) to deduce that

\{\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}\}\times_{\Sigma}\Sigma_{s}=\{\sigma_{uvw}^{pqr}\}^{*}(\tau),

(7.4.8)

which finishes the proof.
∎

Example 7.4.13
.

Let us illustrate (
7.4.8
) with an example.
If
(p,q,r,s)=(4,2,0,6)
, then

\displaystyle\sigma_{uv*}^{420}={\rm Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{u-1},e_{u+1},\ldots,e_{v-1},e_{v+1},\ldots,e_{w-1},e_{w+1},\ldots,e_{n},f_{4},f_{2},e_{w}),

\displaystyle\sigma_{uv*}^{620}={\rm Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{u-1},e_{u+1},\ldots,e_{v-1},e_{v+1},\ldots,e_{w-1},e_{w+1},\ldots,e_{n},f_{6},f_{2},e_{w}),

\displaystyle\sigma_{uv*}^{460}={\rm Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{u-1},e_{u+1},\ldots,e_{v-1},e_{v+1},\ldots,e_{w-1},e_{w+1},\ldots,e_{n},f_{4},f_{6},e_{w}),

\displaystyle\sigma_{uvw}^{426}={\rm Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{u-1},e_{u+1},\ldots,e_{v-1},e_{v+1},\ldots,e_{w-1},e_{w+1},\ldots,e_{n},f_{4},f_{2},f_{6}).

The cone
\sigma_{uv*}^{460}
has dimension
<n
, and

\sigma_{uv*}^{620},\sigma_{uv(p_{1}+p_{2}+1)}^{426},\ldots,\sigma_{uvn}^{426}

are precisely all the maximal cones of
\{\sigma_{uv*}^{420}\}\times_{\Sigma}\Sigma_{6}
.
In addition,

\tau={\rm Cone}(f_{4},e_{p_{1}+p_{2}+1},\ldots,e_{n})

is the subcone of
\sigma_{uv*}^{420}
generated by
f_{6}
, and

\{\sigma_{uvw}^{420}\}\times_{\Sigma}\Sigma_{6}=\{\sigma_{uvw}^{420}\}^{*}(\tau).

Construction 7.4.14
.

For
X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}\in SmlSm/S
and vector bundles
\mathcal{E}_{i}\rightarrow X_{i}
of fixed rank for
i=1,2,3
,
we may apply Construction
7.4.10
to the vector bundles
\underline{\mathcal{E}_{1}}\rightarrow\underline{X_{1}}
,
\underline{\mathcal{E}_{2}}\rightarrow\underline{X_{2}}
, and
\underline{\mathcal{E}_{3}}\rightarrow\underline{X_{3}}
.
In this way, we obtain the blow-up
\underline{T_{I}}
of

\underline{T}:=\underline{\mathcal{E}_{1}}\times_{S}\underline{\mathcal{E}_{2}}\times_{S}\underline{\mathcal{E}_{3}}

for every subset
I
of
\{1,\ldots,6\}
such that

I\cap\{4,5,6\}\neq\{4,5\}.

For
i=1,2,3
let
Z_{i}
be the
0
-section of
\mathcal{E}_{i}
, and set

\displaystyle U:=(\mathcal{E}_{1}-\partial\mathcal{E}_{1})\times_{S}(\mathcal{E}_{2}-\partial\mathcal{E}_{2})\times_{S}(\mathcal{E}_{3}-\partial\mathcal{E}_{3}),\;U_{1}:=U-Z_{1}\times_{S}\mathcal{E}_{2}\times_{S}\mathcal{E}_{3},

\displaystyle U_{2}:=U-\mathcal{E}_{1}\times_{S}Z_{2}\times_{S}\mathcal{E}_{3},\;U_{3}:=U-\mathcal{E}_{1}\times_{S}\mathcal{E}_{2}\times_{S}Z_{3},

\displaystyle U_{4}:=U-Z_{1}\times_{S}Z_{2}\times_{S}\mathcal{E}_{3},\;U_{5}:=U-\mathcal{E}_{1}\times_{S}Z_{2}\times_{S}Z_{3},\;U_{6}:=U-Z_{1}\times_{S}Z_{2}\times_{S}Z_{3}.

If
I=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}\}
, then the open immersion

j_{I}\colon U_{I}:=U_{i_{1}}\cap\cdots\cap U_{i_{n}}\rightarrow\underline{T}

can be lifted to
\underline{T_{I}}
since the images of the exceptional divisors on
\underline{T_{I}}
in
\underline{T}
lie in the complement of
j_{I}
.
Let
T_{I}
denote the fs log scheme whose underlying scheme is
\underline{T_{I}}
and whose log structure is the compactifying log structure associated with
j_{I}\colon U_{I}\rightarrow\underline{T}
.
If
I=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}\}
, for simplicity of notation, we set

T_{i_{1}\ldots i_{n}}:=T_{I}

In particular, we set
T:=T_{\emptyset}
.

We can divide all the
T_{I}
’s into the following groups such that if
T_{I}
and
T_{J}
belong to the same group, then
U_{I}=U_{J}
.

\begin{gathered}\{T\},\;\{T_{1},T_{14},T_{16},T_{146}\},\;\{T_{2},T_{24},T_{25},T_{26},T_{246},T_{256},T_{2456}\},\\
\{T_{3},T_{35},T_{36},T_{356}\},\;\{T_{4},T_{46}\},\;\{T_{5},T_{56}\},\;\{T_{6}\},\\
\{T_{12},T_{124},T_{125},T_{126},T_{1246},T_{1256},T_{12456}\},\\
\{T_{13},T_{134},T_{135},T_{136},T_{1346},T_{1356},T_{13456}\},\\
\{T_{23},T_{234},T_{235},T_{236},T_{2346},T_{2356},T_{23456}\},\\
\{T_{15},T_{156},T_{1456}\},\;\{T_{34},T_{346},T_{3456}\},\\
\{T_{123},T_{1234},T_{1235},T_{1236},T_{12346},T_{12356},T_{123456}\},\;\{T_{456}\}.\end{gathered}

(7.4.9)

If
U_{I}=U_{J}
and
\lvert J\rvert=\lvert I\rvert+1
, then
T_{J}
is an admissible blow-up of
T_{I}
along a smooth center since we have shown that
W_{I,j}
in Construction
7.4.10
is smooth.

We can now turn to prove compatibility between Thom motives and products.
As can be expected, since blow-ups are involved in our formulation, this requires some extra arguments than in the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-setting.

Proposition 7.4.15
.

For
X_{1},X_{2}\in SmlSm/S
and vector bundles
\mathcal{E}_{1}\rightarrow X_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}\rightarrow X_{2}
, there exists a canonical isomorphism

MTh_{X_{1}}(\mathcal{E}_{1})\otimes MTh_{X_{2}}(\mathcal{E}_{2})\xrightarrow{\simeq}MTh_{X_{1}\times_{S}X_{2}}(\mathcal{E}_{1}\times_{S}\mathcal{E}_{2}).

(7.4.10)

Démonstration.

Step 1
Apply Construction
7.4.14
to the vector bundles
\mathcal{E}_{1}\rightarrow X_{1}
,
\mathcal{E}_{2}\rightarrow X_{2}
, and
S\rightarrow S

to obtain an fs log scheme
T_{I}\in SmlSm/S
for every subset
I
of
\{1,2,4\}
.
According to Construction
7.4.14
, the morphisms

T_{124}\rightarrow T_{12},\;T_{14}\rightarrow T_{1},\;T_{24}\rightarrow T_{2}

are admissible blow-ups along smooth centers.
Theorem
7.2.10
shows the naturally induced morphisms

M(T_{124})\rightarrow M(T_{12}),\;M(T_{14})\rightarrow M(T_{1}),\;M(T_{24})\rightarrow M(T_{2})

(7.4.11)

are isomorphisms.

with

,

By (Sq), there exist canonical distinguished triangles

\displaystyle M(D_{2}^{\prime})\rightarrow M(D_{1}^{\prime})\rightarrow M(Q^{\prime})\rightarrow M(D_{2}^{\prime})[1],

(7.4.12)

\displaystyle M(T_{24}\rightarrow T_{24})\rightarrow M(T\rightarrow T)\rightarrow M(D_{1}^{\prime})\rightarrow M(T_{24}\rightarrow T_{24})[1].

(7.4.13)

Due to Proposition
C.1.5
and (

) we have
M(D_{1}^{\prime})=0
.
We will show in Step 3 that
M(D_{2}^{\prime})=0
.
Assuming this, from (

), we have

M(Q^{\prime})=0.

There exists a morphism of distinguished triangles due to (Sq)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(C_{2}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(C_{1}^{\prime})}

(7.4.14)

The first and fourth vertical morphisms are the identity, and the second vertical morphism is an isomorphism since we also have a morphism of distinguished triangles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(T_{24}\rightarrow T_{24})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(T_{4}\rightarrow T)}

Thus the third vertical morphism in (

) is an isomorphism.

Using (Sq) we deduce that the naturally induced morphism

M(C_{2})\rightarrow M(C_{1})

(7.4.15)

is an isomorphism.

Let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{12}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{2}}

Step 3
It remains to verify that
M(D_{1}^{\prime})=0
, which is equivalent to showing the naturally induced morphism

M(T_{124}\rightarrow T_{24})\rightarrow M(T_{14}\rightarrow T_{4})

is an isomorphism due to (Sq).
By (
Zar
-sep), this question is Zariski local on
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
.
Hence we may assume that
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}
are trivial, say of rank
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
.
In this case, we can further reduce to the case when
X_{1}=X_{2}=S
by applying (MSq).

For simplicity of notation we suppose that
S=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
.
Recall the notations
f_{1}
,
f_{2}
, and
\Sigma_{I}
for
I\subset\{1,2,4\}
in Construction
7.4.10
.
We set

\displaystyle\Sigma_{4}^{\prime}:=\{{\rm Cone}(f_{1}+f_{2})\},\;\Sigma_{14}^{\prime}:=\{{\rm Cone}(f_{1},f_{1}+f_{2})\},\;\Sigma_{24}^{\prime}:=\{{\rm Cone}(f_{2},f_{1}+f_{2})\},

\displaystyle\Sigma_{124}^{\prime}:=\{{\rm Cone}(f_{1},f_{1}+f_{2}),{\rm Cone}(f_{2},f_{1}+f_{2})\}.

With these definitions we have

T_{124}=\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{124},\Sigma_{124}^{\prime})},\;T_{14}=\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{14},\Sigma_{14}^{\prime})},\;T_{24}=\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{24},\Sigma_{24}^{\prime})},\;T_{4}=\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{4},\Sigma_{3}^{\prime})}.

When
p=2
and
q=1
, we can describe the fans as follows.

e_{1}
e_{2}
e_{3}
(\Sigma_{124},\Sigma_{124}^{\prime})
(\Sigma_{14},\Sigma_{14}^{\prime})
(\Sigma_{24},\Sigma_{24}^{\prime})
(\Sigma_{4},\Sigma_{4}^{\prime})

For any cone
\sigma
, we write
\{\sigma\}
for the fan whose only maximal cone is
\sigma
.
Due to (
Zar
-sep) it suffices to check that the naturally induced morphism

M(T_{124}\times_{\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma_{4}}}}\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\{\sigma\}}}\rightarrow T_{24}\times_{\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma_{4}}}}\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\{\sigma\}}})\rightarrow M(T_{14}\times_{\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma_{4}}}}\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\{\sigma\}}}\rightarrow T_{4}\times_{\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma_{4}}}}\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\{\sigma\}}})

(7.4.18)

is an isomorphism for every cone
\sigma
of
\Sigma_{4}
.
Equivalently, owing to Proposition
C.1.7
, it suffices to check that the naturally induced morphism

M(T_{124}\times_{\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma_{4}}}}\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\{\sigma\}}}\rightarrow T_{14}\times_{\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma_{4}}}}\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\{\sigma\}}})\rightarrow M(T_{24}\times_{\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma_{4}}}}\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\{\sigma\}}}\rightarrow T_{4}\times_{\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma_{4}}}}\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\{\sigma\}}})

(7.4.19)

is an isomorphism.
If

\sigma\subset{\rm Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{i-1},e_{i+1},\ldots,e_{p_{1}+p_{2}},f_{1}+f_{2})

for some
1\leq i\leq p_{1}
, then the naturally induced morphisms

T_{124}\times_{\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma_{4}}}}\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\{\sigma\}}}\rightarrow T_{24}\times_{\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma_{4}}}}\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\{\sigma\}}},\;T_{14}\times_{\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma_{4}}}}\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\{\sigma\}}}\rightarrow T_{4}\times_{\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma_{4}}}}\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\{\sigma\}}}

are isomorphisms.
Thus the morphism (
7.4.18
) is also an isomorphism.
On the other hand, if

\sigma\subset{\rm Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{p_{1}+j-1},e_{p_{1}+j+1},\ldots,e_{p_{1}+p_{2}},f_{1}+f_{2})

for some
1\leq j\leq p_{2}
, then the naturally induced morphisms

T_{124}\times_{\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma_{4}}}}\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\{\sigma\}}}\rightarrow T_{14}\times_{\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma_{4}}}}\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\{\sigma\}}},\;T_{24}\times_{\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma_{4}}}}\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\{\sigma\}}}\rightarrow T_{4}\times_{\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma_{4}}}}\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\{\sigma\}}}

are isomorphisms.
Thus the morphism (
7.4.19
) is also an isomorphism.
∎

Proposition 7.4.16
.

For
X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}\in SmlSm/S
and vector bundles
\mathcal{E}_{i}\rightarrow X_{i}
for
i=1,2,3
,
there are naturally induced isomorphisms rendering the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces MTh_{X_{1}}(\mathcal{E}_{1})\otimes MTh_{X_{2}}(\mathcal{E}_{1})\otimes MTh_{X_{3}}(\mathcal{E}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces MTh_{X_{1}}(\mathcal{E}_{1})\otimes MTh_{X_{2}\times_{S}X_{3}}(\mathcal{E}_{2}\times_{S}\mathcal{E}_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces MTh_{X_{1}\times_{S}X_{2}}(\mathcal{E}_{1}\times_{S}\mathcal{E}_{2})\otimes MTh_{X_{3}}(\mathcal{E}_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces MTh_{X_{1}\times_{S}X_{2}\times_{S}X_{3}}(\mathcal{E}_{1}\times_{S}\mathcal{E}_{2}\times_{S}\mathcal{E}_{3})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

(7.4.20)

commutative.

Démonstration.

We note the isomorphisms are obtained from Proposition
7.4.15
.
Applying Construction
7.4.14
to the vector bundles
\mathcal{E}_{1}\rightarrow X_{1}
,
\mathcal{E}_{2}\rightarrow X_{2}
, and
\mathcal{E}_{3}\rightarrow X_{3}
we obtain an fs log scheme

T_{I}\in SmlSm/S
for every subset
I
of
\{1,\ldots,6\}
such that

I\cap\{4,5,6\}\neq\{4,5\}.

For
i=1,2,3
,
let

be the
0
-section of
\mathcal{E}_{i}
, and let

be the exceptional divisor on the blow-up

.
All of the blow-ups
\underline{T_{I}}
contains

(\mathcal{E}_{1}-\partial\mathcal{E}_{1})\times_{S}(\mathcal{E}_{2}-\partial\mathcal{E}_{2})\times_{S}(\mathcal{E}_{3}-\partial\mathcal{E}_{3})

as an open subscheme.
Let

denote the fs log scheme whose underlying scheme is
\underline{T_{I}}
and whose log structure is the compactifying log structure associated with the above open immersion.

-squares

By (Sq) there are distinguished triangles

\displaystyle M(C^{\prime\prime})\rightarrow M(C_{3})\rightarrow M(Q^{\prime})\rightarrow M(C^{\prime\prime})[1],\;M(C^{\prime\prime})\rightarrow M(C_{3}^{\prime})\rightarrow M(Q^{\prime})\rightarrow M(C^{\prime\prime})[1],

\displaystyle M(T_{6}\rightarrow T_{6})\rightarrow M(T_{6}\rightarrow T_{6})\rightarrow M(C^{\prime\prime})\rightarrow M(T_{6}\rightarrow T_{6})[1].

Since
M(T_{6}\rightarrow T_{6})=0
by Proposition
C.1.5
, it follows that
M(C^{\prime\prime})=0
.
Thus the morphisms labeled
(f)
are isomorphisms.
The diagram remains commutative after inverting all morphisms with labels
(a)
,
(c)
,
(e)
, and
(f)
.
This means that also the diagram (
7.4.20
) commutes.
∎

Proposition 7.4.17
.

For every
X\in SmlSm/S
and integer
n\geq 1
, there is a canonical isomorphism

MTh_{X}(X\times\mathbb{A}^{n})\simeq M(X)(n)[2n].

Démonstration.

Owing to Proposition
7.4.5
there are isomorphisms

MTh_{X}(X\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\simeq M(X)(1)[2],\;MTh_{S}(S\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\simeq M(S)(1)[2].

Then apply Proposition
7.4.15
to obtain an isomorphism

MTh_{X}(X\times\mathbb{A}^{r})\otimes MTh_{S}(S\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\simeq MTh_{X}(X\times\mathbb{A}^{r+1})

for every integer
r\geq 1
.
Induction on
n
concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 7.4.18
.

We can also produce an isomorphism

MTh_{X}(X\times\mathbb{A}^{n})\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(X)(n)[2n]

by applying Proposition
7.4.15
in a different order, e.g., as in

\displaystyle MTh(X\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\otimes(MTh_{S}(S\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\otimes MTh_{S}(S\times\mathbb{A}^{1}))

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle MTh(X\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\otimes MTh_{S}(S\times\mathbb{A}^{2})\rightarrow MTh_{X}(X\times\mathbb{A}^{3}).

Proposition
7.4.16
ensures that the isomorphism
MTh_{X}(X\times\mathbb{A}^{n})\simeq M(X)(n)[2n]
is indeed independent of choosing such an order.

Corollary 7.4.19
.

For every
X\in SmlSm/S
and integer
n\geq 1
, there is a canonical isomorphism

M(\mathbb{P}_{X}^{n-1}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}_{X}^{n})\simeq M(X)(n)[2n].

Démonstration.

By (MSq) there is a canonical isomorphism

M(X)\otimes M(\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n-1}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n})\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(\mathbb{P}_{X}^{n-1}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}_{X}^{n}).

Thus we may reduce to the case when
X=S
, and conclude by applying Propositions
7.4.5
and
7.4.17
.
∎

Remark 7.4.20
.

This result is analogous to the isomorphism

\mathbb{P}^{n}/\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\simeq T^{n}

in
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory of schemes
[
68
, Corollary 2.18, p. 112]
.

7.5.
Gysin triangles

Throughout this section we shall assume
\mathcal{T}
satisfies the properties (
Zar
-sep), (
\overline{\Box}
-inv), (
sNis
-des), and (
div
-des).
In
{\bf DM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
,
recall from
[
64
, Theorem 15.15]
that for any smooth scheme
X
with a smooth closed subscheme
Z
with codimension
c
,
there is the Gysin isomorphism

M((X-Z)\rightarrow X)\simeq M(Z)(c)[2c].

This does not hold in
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
.
For example, if
X=\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
Z=\{\infty\}
,
then the said Gysin isomorphism would imply

M(\mathbb{A}^{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1})\simeq\Lambda(1)[2].

It follows that
M(\mathbb{A}^{1})\simeq\Lambda
, so that
\mathbb{A}^{1}
is invertible in
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
.
This is evidently a contradiction since the object
\mathbb{G}_{a}\otimes\Lambda
is in
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
,
but

\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)}(M(\mathbb{A}^{1}),\mathbb{G}_{a}\otimes\Lambda)\not\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)}(M(k),\mathbb{G}_{a}\otimes\Lambda),

see Theorem
9.7.1
.

Our goal in this section is to formulate a Gysin isomorphism in
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
.
As for the construction of Thom motives performed in the previous section,
we need to “compactify”
X-Z
without changing the “homotopy type” of
X-Z
.
This is done by taking the blow-up
B_{Z}X
of
X
along
Z
and adding the log structure given by the exceptional divisor
E
on
B_{Z}X
.
In this case, our formulation of the Gysin isomorphism should take the form

\mathfrak{g}_{X,Z}:M((B_{Z}X,E)\rightarrow X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\rightarrow}}MTh(N_{Z}Y).

Here
MTh(N_{Z}Y)
is the Thom motive associated with the normal bundle
N_{Z}Y
.
We shall formulate the Gysin isomorphism more generally in Construction
7.5.3
allowing nontrivial log structures on
X
.

Recall that the technique of deformation to the normal cone is used in the proof of purity for
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory of schemes
[
68
, Theorem 2.23, p. 115]
.
We will develop a log version of the said technique based on our unit interval
\overline{\Box}
.

Definition 7.5.1
.

Let
X\in Sm/S
, and let
Z
be a smooth closed subscheme of
X
.
We have the commutative diagram of deformation to the normal cone

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z\times\overline{\Box}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{Z}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{Z}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{1\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\Box}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0\}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}}

(7.5.1)

where

(i)

each square is cartesian,

(ii)

i_{0}
is the
0
-section, and
i_{1}
is the
1
-section,

(iii)

Z\rightarrow N_{Z}X
is the
0
-section,

(iv)

D_{Z}X\rightarrow\overline{\Box}
is the composition
D_{Z}X\rightarrow B_{Z}(X\times\overline{\Box})\rightarrow X\times\overline{\Box}\rightarrow\overline{\Box}
where the third arrow is the projection.

Suppose
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{r}
form a strict normal crossing divisor on
X
, and that
Z
has strict normal crossing with
Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{r}
over
S
.
For
Y:=(X,Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{r})
we obtain the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{Z}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{Z\times\overline{\Box}}(D_{Z}Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{Z}(N_{Z}Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{Z}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{Z}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{1\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\Box}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0\}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}}

(7.5.2)

Remark 7.5.2
.

One difference between the technique of deformation to the normal cone used in the proof of
[
68
, Theorem 2.23, p. 115]
and ours is that we use
X\times\overline{\Box}
instead of
X\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
.

Construction 7.5.3
.

We are ready to formulate and prove our purity theorem for log motives.
With the above definitions, let
E
(resp.
E^{D}
,
E^{N}
) be the exceptional divisor on
B_{Z}Y
(resp.
B_{Z\times\overline{\Box}}(D_{Z}Y)
, resp.
B_{Z}(N_{Z}Y)
).
In the case
Z
has codimension
1
in
Y
, we have
E=Z
(resp.
E^{D}=Z\times\overline{\Box}
, resp.
E^{N}=Z
) by convention.
From the diagram (
7.5.2
), we have the naturally induced morphisms

M((B_{Z}Y,E)\rightarrow Y)\rightarrow M((B_{Z\times\overline{\Box}}(D_{Z}Y),E^{D})\rightarrow D_{Z}Y),

M((B_{Z}(N_{Z}Y),E^{N})\rightarrow N_{Z}Y)\rightarrow M((B_{Z\times\overline{\Box}}(D_{Z}Y),E^{D})\rightarrow D_{Z}Y).

If these morphisms are isomorphisms, then via a zig-zag, we obtain the isomorphism

\mathfrak{g}_{Y,Z}:M((B_{Z}Y,E)\rightarrow Y)\rightarrow MTh(N_{Z}Y)

that we refer to as the Gysin isomorphism for the closed pair
(X,Z)
.
This will be the log analogue to
[
68
, Theorem 2.23]
.

Theorem 7.5.4
.

Suppose that
Z_{1},Z_{2},\ldots,Z_{r}
are smooth divisors forming a strict normal crossing divisor on
X\in Sm/S

and that
Z
is a smooth closed subscheme of
X
having strict normal crossing with
Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{r}
over
S
such that
Z
is not contained in any component of
Z_{1}\cup\cdots\cup Z_{r}
.
Let
E
(resp.
E^{D}
, resp.
E^{N}
) be the exceptional divisor on
B_{Z}X
(resp.
B_{Z\times\overline{\Box}}(D_{Z}X)
, resp.
B_{Z}(N_{Z}X)
).
For
Y:=(X,Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{r})
the morphisms

M((B_{Z}Y,E)\rightarrow Y)\rightarrow M((B_{Z\times\overline{\Box}}(D_{Z}Y),E^{D})\rightarrow D_{Z}Y),

M((B_{Z}(N_{Z}Y),E^{N})\rightarrow N_{Z}Y)\rightarrow M((B_{Z\times\overline{\Box}}(D_{Z}Y),E^{D})\rightarrow D_{Z}Y).

induced by
(
7.5.2
)
are isomorphisms and functorial in the pair
(Y,Z)
.

Démonstration.

Step 1
We proceed as in the proof of Theorem
7.2.10
, using a local parametrization of
(X,Z)
.
For
i=1,\ldots,r
, consider the divisor

W_{i}:=V(t_{i})

on
\mathbb{A}^{r+s}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{r+s}])
,
and let
W
denote the subscheme
V(t_{r+1},\ldots,t_{r+p})
.
The question is Zariski local on
X
by (
Zar
-sep),
and
Z
is not contained in any component of
Z_{1}\cup\cdots\cup Z_{r}
,
so we may assume there exists an étale morphism
u:X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{S}^{r+s}
of schemes over
S
for which
u^{-1}(S\times W_{i})=Z_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq r
and
u^{-1}(S\times W)=Z
.

Step 2

Using
u
and
Z\rightarrow X
,
Construction
7.2.8
produces schemes
X_{j}
over
S
for
j=1,2
.
Form
Y_{j}
from
X_{j}
in the same way as we obtained
Y
from
X
, and
let
E_{j}
(resp.
E
) be the exceptional divisor on
B_{Z}Y_{j}
(resp.
B_{Z}Y
).

Step 3

Since blow-ups commute with flat base change, we have the cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{Z}X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}\times\overline{\Box}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{Z}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times\overline{\Box}}

of fs log schemes over
S
.
It follows that
D_{Z}X_{2}\rightarrow D_{Z}X
is étale.
Let
E_{2}^{D}
(resp.
E_{2}^{N}
) be the exceptional divisor on
B_{Z\times\overline{\Box}}(D_{Z}Y_{2})
(resp.
B_{Z}(N_{Z}Y_{2})
).
Since
X_{2}\rightarrow X
and
N_{Z}X_{2}\rightarrow N_{Z}X
are also étale,
Proposition
7.4.2
implies there are isomorphisms

M((B_{Z}Y_{2},E_{2})\rightarrow Y_{2})\xrightarrow{\simeq}M((B_{Z}Y,E)\rightarrow Y),

M((B_{Z\times\overline{\Box}}(D_{Z}Y_{2}),E_{2}^{D})\rightarrow D_{Z}Y_{2})\xrightarrow{\simeq}M((B_{Z\times\overline{\Box}}(D_{Z}Y),E^{D})\rightarrow D_{Z}Y),

and

M((B_{Z}(N_{Z}Y_{2}),E_{2}^{N})\rightarrow N_{Z}Y_{2})\xrightarrow{\simeq}M((B_{Z}(N_{Z}Y),E^{N})\rightarrow N_{Z}Y).

Hence we can replace
X
with
X_{2}
.
Similarly, we can replace
X_{2}
with
X_{1}
.
This reduces to the situation when
X=T\times\mathbb{A}^{r+s},

Z_{i}=T\times W_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq r
, and
Z=T\times W
for some
T\in Sm/S
.

Step 4

Let
F
(resp.
F^{D}
, resp.
F^{N}
) be the exceptional divisor on
B_{\{0\}}(\mathbb{A}^{p})
(resp.
B_{\{0\}\times\overline{\Box}}(D_{\{0\}}(\mathbb{A}_{S}^{p}))
, resp.
B_{\{0\}\times\overline{\Box}}(N_{\{0\}}(\mathbb{A}_{S}^{p}))
).
Setting
V:=(Z,Z\cap Z_{1}+\cdots+Z\cap Z_{r})
we observe that

V=(T\times W,T\times(W_{1}\cap W)+\cdots+T\times(W_{r}\cap W))\simeq T\times\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}))^{p}\times\mathbb{A}^{s-p},

(B_{Z}Y,E)=(T\times B_{W}(\mathbb{A}^{r+s}),T\times W_{1}+\cdots+T\times W_{r})\simeq V\times(B_{\{0\}}(\mathbb{A}^{p}),F).

and

Y=(T\times\mathbb{A}^{r+s},T\times W_{1}+\cdots+T\times W_{r})\simeq V\times\mathbb{A}^{p}.

By (MSq),
it remains to show that (
7.5.1
) induces isomorphisms

\begin{split}&M(V)\otimes M((B_{\{0\}}(\mathbb{A}_{S}^{p}),F)\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{S}^{p})\\
\xrightarrow{\simeq}&M(V)\otimes M((B_{\{0\}\times\overline{\Box}}(D_{\{0\}}(\mathbb{A}_{S}^{p}),F^{D})\rightarrow D_{\{0\}}(\mathbb{A}_{S}^{p})),\end{split}

\begin{split}&M(V)\otimes M((B_{\{0\}}(N_{\{0\}}(\mathbb{A}_{S}^{p})),F^{N})\rightarrow N_{\{0\}}(\mathbb{A}_{S}^{p}))\\
\xrightarrow{\simeq}&M(V)\otimes M((B_{\{0\}}(D_{\{0\}}(\mathbb{A}_{S}^{p})),F^{D})\rightarrow D_{\{0\}}(\mathbb{A}_{S}^{p})).\end{split}

Step 5

Due to Step 4 we may assume that
X=\mathbb{A}_{S}^{p}
,
r=0
, and
Z=\{0\}\times S
.
We set
S=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
for simplicity.
Let
e_{1},\ldots,e_{p}
denote the standard coordinates in
\mathbb{Z}^{p}
.
There are corresponding cones

\displaystyle\tau:={\rm Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{p-1}),\;\sigma_{1}:={\rm Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{p-1},e_{1}+\cdots+e_{p}),

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}:={\rm Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{p-1},-e_{p}),\;\sigma_{3}:={\rm Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{p-1},-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{p}),

and their fans

\Sigma_{1}:=\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\},\;\Sigma_{2}:=\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{3}\},\;\Sigma_{1}^{\prime}:=\{{\rm Cone}(-e_{p})\},\;\Sigma_{2}^{\prime}:=\{{\rm Cone}(-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{p})\}.

Moreover, we shall consider the star subdivisions

\Sigma_{3}:=\Sigma_{2}^{*}(\sigma_{3}),\;\Sigma_{4}:=\Sigma_{1}^{*}(\tau),\;\Sigma_{5}:=\Sigma_{2}^{*}(\tau),\;\Sigma_{6}:=\Sigma_{3}^{*}(\tau),

and the subfans

\displaystyle\Sigma_{3}^{\prime}:=\{{\rm Cone}(-e_{p},-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{p})\},\;\Sigma_{4}^{\prime}:=\{{\rm Cone}(e_{1}+\cdots+e_{p-1},-e_{p})\},

\displaystyle\Sigma_{5}^{\prime}:=\{{\rm Cone}(e_{1}+\cdots+e_{p-1},-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{p})\},

\displaystyle\Sigma_{6}^{\prime}:=\{{\rm Cone}(e_{1}+\cdots+e_{p-1},-e_{p}),{\rm Cone}(-e_{p},-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{p})\}.

For
1\leq i\leq p-1
, we set

\eta_{i}:={\rm Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{i-1},e_{i+1},\ldots,e_{p-1},-e_{p},-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{p}),

and form the fans

\Sigma_{7}:=\{\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{p-1}\},\;\Sigma_{7}^{\prime}:=\Sigma_{3}^{\prime}.

When
p=3
, the above fans take the following forms.

e_{1}
e_{2}
e_{1}+e_{2}+e_{3}
-e_{3}
(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{1}^{\prime})
-e_{1}-e_{2}-e_{3}
(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{2}^{\prime})
(\Sigma_{3},\Sigma_{3}^{\prime})

(\Sigma_{4},\Sigma_{4}^{\prime})
(\Sigma_{5},\Sigma_{5}^{\prime})
(\Sigma_{6},\Sigma_{6}^{\prime})
(\Sigma_{7},\Sigma_{7}^{\prime})

Observe that

D_{Z}Y=\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{1}^{\prime})},\;(B_{Z\times\overline{\Box}}(D_{Z}Y),E^{D})=\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{4},\Sigma_{4}^{\prime})}.

The fan
\{\sigma_{1}\}
corresponds to the trivial line bundle
Y\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
, and the
0
-section (resp.
1
-section) of the bundle is
Y
(resp.
N_{Z}Y
).
Hence there are naturally induced commutative diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{1}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{3},\Sigma_{3}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{Z}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{2}^{\prime})},}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{4},\Sigma_{4}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B_{Z}Y,E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{6},\Sigma_{6}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B_{Z}(N_{Z}Y),E^{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{5},\Sigma_{5}^{\prime})}.}

Due to these diagrams, we are reduced to showing the naturally induced morphisms

\displaystyle M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{4},\Sigma_{4}^{\prime})}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{1}^{\prime})})\rightarrow M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{6},\Sigma_{6}^{\prime})}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{3},\Sigma_{3}^{\prime})}),

(7.5.3)

\displaystyle M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{6},\Sigma_{6}^{\prime})}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{3},\Sigma_{3}^{\prime})})\rightarrow M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{5},\Sigma_{5}^{\prime})}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{2}^{\prime})}),

(7.5.4)

\displaystyle M((B_{Y}Z,E)\rightarrow Y)\rightarrow M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{5},\Sigma_{5}^{\prime})}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{2}^{\prime})}),

(7.5.5)

\displaystyle M((B_{Z}(N_{Z}Y),E^{N})\rightarrow N_{Z}Y)\rightarrow M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{5},\Sigma_{5}^{\prime})}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{2}^{\prime})})

(7.5.6)

are isomorphisms.

The naturally induced morphisms

\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{6},\Sigma_{6}^{\prime})}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{5},\Sigma_{5}^{\prime})},\;\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{3},\Sigma_{3}^{\prime})}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{2}^{\prime})}

are admissible blow-ups along

V({\rm Cone}(e_{1}+\cdots+e_{p-1},-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{p})),\;V(\sigma_{3}).

Theorem
7.2.10
shows that

M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{3},\Sigma_{3}^{\prime})}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{2}^{\prime})})=0,\;M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{6},\Sigma_{6}^{\prime})}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{5},\Sigma_{5}^{\prime})})=0.

Proposition
C.1.7
allows us to deduce that (
7.5.4
) is an isomorphism.

We can identify
\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{2}^{\prime})}
and
\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{5},\Sigma_{5}^{\prime})}
with

Y\times\overline{\Box},\;(B_{Z}Y,E)\times\overline{\Box}.

The fibers at
0\in\overline{\Box}
(resp.
1\in\overline{\Box}
) correspond to
N_{Z}Y
and
(B_{Z}(N_{Z}Y),E^{N})
(resp.
Y
and
(B_{Z}Y,E)
).
Thus (
\overline{\Box}
-inv) shows that (
7.5.5
) and (
7.5.6
) are isomorphisms.

There are strict Zariski distinguished squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{1}^{\prime})}\cap\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{7},\Sigma_{7}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{7},\Sigma_{7}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{1}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{3},\Sigma_{3}^{\prime})},}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{4},\Sigma_{4}^{\prime})}\cap\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{7},\Sigma_{7}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{7},\Sigma_{7}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{4},\Sigma_{4}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{6},\Sigma_{6}^{\prime})}.}

Since

\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{1}^{\prime})}\cap\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{7},\Sigma_{7}^{\prime})}=\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{4},\Sigma_{4}^{\prime})}\cap\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{7},\Sigma_{7}^{\prime})},

we can use (
sNis
-des) to obtain the isomorphism

M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{4},\Sigma_{4}^{\prime})}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{6},\Sigma_{6}^{\prime})})\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{1}^{\prime})}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{(\Sigma_{3},\Sigma_{3}^{\prime})}).

Combined with Proposition
C.1.7
we deduce that (
7.5.3
) is an isomorphism, which finishes the proof.
Moreover,
functoriality follows from the construction.
∎

Definition 7.5.5
.

With reference to Theorem
7.5.4
, the
Gysin isomorphism
is the isomorphism

\mathfrak{g}_{Y,Z}:M((B_{Z}Y,E)\rightarrow Y)\rightarrow MTh(N_{Z}Y),

7.6.
Invariance under admissible blow-ups

In this section,
we assume that
\mathcal{T}
satisfies the properties (
Zar
-sep), (
\overline{\Box}
-inv), (
sNis
-des), and (
div
-des),
S=\operatorname{Spec}(k)
, and
k
admits resolution of singularities.
Under these hypotheses, we prove that motives are invariant under admissible blow-ups.
This invariance will be used for comparing
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
with
{\bf DM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
. We start by introducing the following (weaker) version of the notion of admissible blow-up in Definition
7.2.1
.

Definition 7.6.1
.

An
admissible blow-up

Y\rightarrow X
in
\mathscr{S}/S
is a proper birational morphism of fs log schemes such that the induced morphism

Y-\partial Y\rightarrow X-\partial X

of schemes is an isomorphism.
We denote by
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}l/S
the class of admissible blow-ups.

Note that
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}l_{Sm}/S
and
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}l_{div}/S
are subclasses of
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}l/S
.

Remark 7.6.2
.

The class
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}l/S
is equivalent to the class
\mathcal{S}_{b}
in
[
9
]
if
S=\operatorname{Spec}(k)
and
\mathscr{S}/S=SmlSm/k
.

Definition 7.6.3
.

The field
k
admits
resolution of singularities
if the following conditions hold.

(1)

For any integral scheme
X
of finite type over
k
, there is a proper birational morphism
Y\rightarrow X
of schemes over
k
such that
Y
is smooth over
k
.

(2)

Let
f:Y\rightarrow X
be a proper birational morphism of integral schemes over
k
such that
X
is smooth over
k
and let
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{r}
be smooth divisors forming a strict normal crossing divisor on
X
.
Assume that

f^{-1}(X-Z_{1}\cup\cdots\cup Z_{r})\rightarrow X-Z_{1}\cup\cdots\cup Z_{r}

is an isomorphism.
Then there is a sequence of blow-ups

X_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{n-1}}}{{\rightarrow}}X_{n-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{n-2}}}{{\rightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{0}}}{{\rightarrow}}X_{0}=X

along smooth centers
W_{i}\subset X_{i}
such that

(i)

the composition
X_{n}\rightarrow X
factors through
f
,

(ii)

W_{i}
is contained in the preimage of
Z_{1}\cup\cdots\cup Z_{r}
in
X_{i}
,

(iii)

W_{i}
has strict normal crossing with the sum of the reduced strict transforms of

Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{r},f_{0}^{-1}(W_{0}),\ldots,f_{i-1}^{-1}(W_{i-1})

in
X_{i}
.

Note that
k
must be perfect in order to satisfy (1). If
k
has characteristic
0
, then (1) (resp. (2)) is satisfied by
[
43
, Main theorem I]
(resp.
[
43
, Main theorem II]
).

Proposition 7.6.4
.

Assume that
k
admits resolution of singularities.
Then the class
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}l_{div}/k
admits a calculus of right fractions.

Démonstration.

By definition,
the class in question contains isomorphisms and is closed under composition.
If
f,g:X\rightarrow Y
and
s:Y\rightarrow Z
are morphisms of fs log schemes log smooth over
k
such that
s\circ f=s\circ g
and
s\in\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}l_{div}/k
,
then
f
and
g
agree on the dense open subscheme
X-\partial X
of
X
since the morphism
Y-\partial Y\rightarrow Z-\partial Z
induced by
s
is an isomorphism.
Thus
f=g
by Lemma
2.3.1
and the right cancellabilty condition holds.

It remains to check the right Ore condition.
Suppose
f:Y\rightarrow X
and
g:X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
are morphisms where
f\in\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}l_{div}/k
.
By Proposition
A.10.2
, we can choose a dividing cover
X_{1}^{\prime}\rightarrow X^{\prime}
such that
\partial X_{1}^{\prime}
is a strict normal crossing divisor.
Since
f
is an admissible blow-up,
Y\times_{X}X_{1}^{\prime}
contains
X_{1}^{\prime}-\partial X_{1}^{\prime}
as an open subscheme.
Let
Y_{1}^{\prime}
be the closure of
X_{1}^{\prime}-\partial X_{1}^{\prime}
, which is proper and birational over
X_{1}^{\prime}
.
By resolution of singularities, there exists a morphism
Y^{\prime}\to X_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}l_{Sm}/k
that factors through
Y_{1}^{\prime}
.
Thus we have the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Here the left vertical arrow is in
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}l_{div}/k
, so the right Ore condition is satisfied.
∎

Proposition 7.6.5
.

Assume that
k
admits resolution of singularities.
For any morphism
f:Y\rightarrow X
in
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}l/k
, there is a morphism
Y^{\prime}\rightarrow X
in
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}l_{div}/k
that factors through
f
.

Démonstration.

There exists a dividing cover
g:X_{1}\rightarrow X
such that
\underline{X_{1}}
is smooth over
k
and
\partial X_{1}
is a strict normal crossing divisor,
see Proposition
A.10.2
.
Applying part (2) of Definition
7.6.3
to the projection
p:X_{1}\times_{X}Y\rightarrow X_{1}
,
we obtain a morphism
h:Y^{\prime}\rightarrow X_{1}
in
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}l_{Sm}/k
that factors through
p
.
Then the composition
gh
is in
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}l_{div}/k
and factors through
f
.
∎

Proposition 7.6.6
.

Assume that
k
admits resolution of singularities.
Then
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}l/k
admits a calculus of right fractions.

Démonstration.

The right Ore condition follows from Propositions
7.6.4
and
7.6.5
.
The proof of the right cancellability condition is similar to that in Proposition
7.6.4
.
∎

Theorem 7.6.7
.

Assume that
k
admits resolution of singularities.
Then any morphism
f:Y\rightarrow X
in
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}l/k
induces an isomorphism

M(Y)\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(X).

Démonstration.

Propositions
7.6.4
and
7.6.6
show that
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}l/k
and
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}l_{div}/k
satisfy a calculus of right fractions.
All the conditions in Lemma
C.2.1
are met by Propositions
7.2.12
and
7.6.5
, so we are done.
∎

Remark 7.6.8
.

By Theorem
7.6.7
, we can apply the results in
6
to

\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}Bl/k;\,I=\overline{\Box};\,T=lSm/k,Cor/k.

For example,
for any
(I,\mathcal{A})
-weak equivalence
f:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
of bounded above complexes of presheaves (resp. presheaves with transfers) on
lSm/k
,
the image of
f
in
{\bf logDA}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
(resp.
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
) is an isomorphism.

7.7.
Invariance under log modifications

In this section we assume that
\mathscr{S}=SmlSm/S
and
\mathcal{T}
satisfies the properties
(
Zar
-sep), (
\overline{\Box}
-inv), (
sNis
-des), and (
(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})
-inv).
Under these hypothesis we show that the property (
div
-des) holds.

Lemma 7.7.1
.

Let
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
be the closed subschemes
\{0\}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
and
\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\{0\}
of
\mathbb{A}^{2}
,
let
H_{1}^{\prime}
and
H_{2}^{\prime}
be the strict transforms of
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
in
{\rm Bl}_{\{(0,0)\}}(\mathbb{A}^{2})
,
and let
E
be the exceptional divisor on
{\rm Bl}_{\{(0,0)\}}(\mathbb{A}^{2})
.
Then for any
X\in\mathscr{S}/S
, there is a naturally induced isomorphism

M(X\times({\rm Bl}_{\{(0,0)\}}\mathbb{A}^{2},H_{1}^{\prime}+H_{2}^{\prime}+E))\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(X\times(\mathbb{A}^{2},H_{1}+H_{2})).

Démonstration.

Assume that
S=X=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
for simplicity of notation as usual.
Due to Proposition
7.2.5
, it suffices to show there is a naturally induced isomorphism

M({\rm Bl}_{\{(0,0)\}}\mathbb{A}^{2},H_{1}^{\prime}+E)\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(\mathbb{A}^{2},H_{1}).

Let
\overline{H_{1}}
and
\overline{H_{2}}
be the closure of
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
in
\mathbb{P}^{2}
,
and let
\overline{H_{1}^{\prime}}
and
\overline{H_{2}^{\prime}}
be the closures of
H_{1}^{\prime}
and
H_{2}^{\prime}
in
{\rm Bl}_{\{O\}}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
,
where
O:=[0:0:1]
.
There are Zariski distinguished squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{A}^{2},H_{1})-\{O\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{A}^{2},H_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{P}^{2},\overline{H_{1}})-\{O\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{P}^{2},\overline{H_{1}})}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{A}^{2},H_{1})-\{O\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\rm Bl}_{\{(0,0)\}}\mathbb{A}^{2},H_{1}^{\prime}+E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{P}^{2},\overline{H_{1}})-\{O\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\rm Bl}_{\{O\}}\mathbb{P}^{2},\overline{H_{1}^{\prime}}+E).}

Applying (
sNis
-des) to these squares yields an isomorphism

M(({\rm Bl}_{\{(0,0)\}}\mathbb{A}^{2},H_{1}^{\prime}+E)\rightarrow({\rm Bl}_{\{O\}}\mathbb{P}^{2},\overline{H_{1}^{\prime}}+E))\xrightarrow{\simeq}M((\mathbb{A}^{2},H_{1})\rightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},\overline{H_{1}}))

By Proposition
C.1.7
we find the isomorphism

M(({\rm Bl}_{\{(0,0)\}}\mathbb{A}^{2},H_{1}^{\prime}+E)\rightarrow(\mathbb{A}^{2},H_{1}))\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(({\rm Bl}_{\{O\}}\mathbb{P}^{2},\overline{H_{1}^{\prime}}+E)\rightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},\overline{H_{1}})).

Hence we only need to show that the morphism

M({\rm Bl}_{\{O\}}\mathbb{P}^{2},\overline{H_{1}^{\prime}}+E)\rightarrow M(\mathbb{P}^{2},\overline{H_{1}})

is an isomorphism.
Since
({\rm Bl}_{\{O\}}\mathbb{P}^{2},\overline{H_{1}^{\prime}}+E))
is a
\overline{\Box}
-bundle over
\overline{\Box}
by Lemma
7.1.8
,
(
(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})
-inv) gives

M({\rm Bl}_{\{O\}}\mathbb{P}^{2},\overline{H_{1}^{\prime}}+E)\simeq M(\mathbb{Z})\simeq M(\mathbb{P}^{2},\overline{H_{1}}).

∎

Definition 7.7.2
.

For a number
r\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, let
{\rm Fan}_{r}
denote the category where objects are
r
-dimensional fans and morphisms are subdivisions.
For an
r
-dimensional smooth fan
\Sigma
,
let
{\rm Fan}_{r}^{sm}/\Sigma
denote the full category of
{\rm Fan}_{r}/\Sigma
consisting of smooth
r
-dimensions fans that are subdivisions of
\Sigma
.
Let
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{D}/\Sigma
be the class of morphisms in
{\rm Fan}_{r}/\Sigma
,
and let
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{D}_{2}/\Sigma
denote the class of morphisms in
{\rm Fan}_{r}^{sm}/\Sigma
that are obtained by finite successions of star subdivisions relative to two-dimensional cones.

Lemma 7.7.3
.

Let
\Sigma
be a fan.
The class
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{D}/\Sigma
admits a calculus of right fractions.
If in addition
\Sigma
is smooth, then
the class
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{D}_{2}/\Sigma
also admits a calculus of right fractions.

Démonstration.

Let
r
be the dimension of
\Sigma
.
By definition,
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{D}_{2}/\Sigma
and
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{D}_{2}/\Sigma
are closed under compositions.
For any objects
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
of
{\rm Fan}_{r}/\Sigma
or
{\rm Fan}_{r}^{sm}/\Sigma
, there is at most one morphism from
\Sigma_{2}
to
\Sigma_{1}
.
Thus the right cancellabilty condition holds for
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{D}/\Sigma
and
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{D}_{2}/\Sigma
.
It remains to check the right Ore condition.
Suppose that
f:\Sigma_{2}\rightarrow\Sigma_{1}
and
g:\Sigma_{1}^{\prime}\rightarrow\Sigma_{1}
are in
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{D}/\Sigma
.
Let
\Sigma_{2}^{\prime}
be the fan generated by
\Sigma_{2}
and
\Sigma_{1}^{\prime}
.
We have the naturally induced commutative diagram of fans

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma_{2}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma_{1}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma_{1}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

This shows the Ore condition for
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{D}/\Sigma
.

Suppose that
\Sigma
,
\Sigma_{1}
,
\Sigma_{1}^{\prime}
, and
\Sigma_{2}
are smooth.
The fan
\Sigma_{2}^{\prime}
is not necessarily smooth, but
\Sigma_{2}^{\prime}
has a subdivision
\Sigma_{2}^{\prime\prime}
that is smooth by toric resolution of singularities,
see
[
25
, Theorem 11.1.9]
.
By Lemma
A.3.15
, there exists a morphism
f^{\prime}:\Sigma_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}\rightarrow\Sigma_{1}
in
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{D}_{2}/\Sigma
that factors through

\Sigma_{2}^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\Sigma_{2}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f^{\prime}}}{{\rightarrow}}\Sigma_{1}^{\prime}.

This shows the right Ore condition for
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{D}_{2}/\Sigma
.
∎

Theorem 7.7.4
.

Every log modification
f:Y\rightarrow X
in
SmlSm/S
induces an isomorphism

M(f):M(Y)\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(X).

Démonstration.

By (
Zar
-sep), the question is Zariski local on
X
.
Hence by definition, we may assume that there exists a morphism
g:Z\rightarrow Y
such that both
fg
and
g
are log blow-ups.
It suffices to show that
M(fg):M(Z)\rightarrow M(X)
and
M(g):M(Z)\rightarrow M(Y)
are isomorphisms, so
we reduce to the case when
f
is a log blow-up.

The question is Zariski local on
X
, so we may assume that
X
has an fs chart
P
with
P\simeq\mathbb{N}^{r}
for some
r
such that
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{P}
is smooth by Lemma
A.5.9
.
By
[
76
, Lemma III.2.6.4]
, we may further assume that
f
is the projection
X\times_{\mathbb{A}_{P}}\mathbb{A}_{M}\rightarrow X
for some subdivision of fans
M\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}(P)
.
Consider the dual monoid
P^{\vee}
as a fan, and consider
M
as a fan that is a subdivision of
P^{\vee}
.

We have the functors
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{D}/P^{\vee}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{D}_{2}/P^{\vee}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}
given by

(N\in{\rm Fan}_{r}/P^{\vee})\mapsto M(X\times_{\mathbb{A}_{P}}\mathbb{A}_{N}).

By Lemma
7.7.3
, both
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{D}/P^{\vee}
and
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{D}_{2}/P^{\vee}
admit a calculus of right fractions.
Owing to Lemma
C.2.1
, to have the isomorphism

M(f):M(X\times_{\mathbb{A}_{P}}\mathbb{A}_{M})\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(X).

Hence we are reduced to showing that

M(g):M(X\times_{\mathbb{A}_{P}}\mathbb{A}_{N^{\prime}})\rightarrow M(X\times_{\mathbb{A}_{P}}\mathbb{A}_{N})

is an isomorphism for any morphism
N^{\prime}\rightarrow N
in
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{D}_{2}/P^{\vee}
.
It suffices to consider the case when
N^{\prime}
is the star subdivision of
N
relative to a
2
-dimensional cone.
Using Zariski locality, we reduce to the case when
N
is a
2
-dimensional cone, and
N^{\prime}
is its star subdivision.
Since
X
is smooth over
\mathbb{A}_{P}
, we see that
X\times_{\mathbb{A}_{P}}\mathbb{A}_{N}
is smooth over
\mathbb{A}_{N}
.
Hence we can replace
X
by
X\times_{\mathbb{A}_{P}}\mathbb{A}_{N}
and
N^{\prime}
by
M
so that it suffices to show

M(f):M(X\times_{\mathbb{A}_{P}}\mathbb{A}_{M})\rightarrow M(X)

is an isomorphism when
P=\mathbb{N}^{2}
and
M
is the star subdivision of
P^{\vee}
.

Next, we set

Z:=\underline{X}\times_{\underline{\mathbb{A}_{P}}}\underline{{\rm pt}_{P}}.

Since
X
is smooth over its chart
\mathbb{A}_{P}
, Zariski locally on
X
,
there is a strict étale morphism
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}\times\mathbb{A}_{P}
such that the composition with the projection
\mathbb{A}^{n}\times\mathbb{A}_{P}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{P}
is equal to
X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{P}
.
In this case,

(\underline{X},Z)
has a parametrization
(\mathbb{A}^{n+2},\mathbb{A}^{n})
and Construction
7.2.8
produces schemes
\underline{X_{j}}
for
j=1,2
.
Give
\underline{X_{2}}
the log structure induced by that of
X
, and we obtain an fs log scheme
X_{2}
.
Set
X_{1}:=Z\times\mathbb{A}_{P}
, which gives the log structure on
\underline{X_{1}}
.
Now define
Y_{1}:=X_{1}\times_{\mathbb{A}_{P}}\mathbb{A}_{M}
and
Y_{2}:=X_{2}\times_{\mathbb{A}_{P}}\mathbb{A}_{M}
.
Let
E
,
E_{1}
, and
E_{2}
be the exceptional divisors on
Y
,
Y_{1}
, and
Y_{2}
.
Note that
E_{2}\rightarrow E
and
E_{2}\rightarrow E_{1}
are isomorphisms since
Z
is unchanged in
X_{2}\rightarrow X
and
X_{2}\rightarrow X_{1}
.

Owing to Lemma
7.7.1
,
M(Y_{1})\rightarrow M(X_{1})
is an isomorphism, i.e., we have

M(Y_{1}\rightarrow X_{1})\simeq 0.

We obtain strict Nisnevich distinguished squares in
SmlSm/S

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}-Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X-Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{2}-E_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y-E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y.}

By (
sNis
-des), there are isomorphisms

M((Y_{2}-E_{2})\rightarrow(Y-E))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}M(Y_{2}\rightarrow Y),\;M((X_{2}-Z)\rightarrow(X-Z))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}M(X_{2}\rightarrow X).

Since
Y_{2}-E_{2}^{\prime}\simeq X_{2}-Z
and
Y-E\simeq X-Z
, there is an isomorphism

M(Y_{2}\rightarrow Y)\simeq M(X_{2}\rightarrow X_{2})

Using Proposition
C.1.7
, we obtain an isomorphism

M(Y_{2}\rightarrow X_{2})\simeq M(Y\rightarrow X).

Similarly, there is an isomorphism

M(Y_{2}\rightarrow X_{2})\simeq M(Y_{1}\rightarrow X_{1}).

Thus
M(Y\rightarrow X)\simeq 0
since
M(Y_{1}\rightarrow X_{1})\simeq 0
,
so
M(Y)\rightarrow M(X)
is an isomorphism.
∎

7.8.
Strictly
(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})
-invariant complexes

In this section we introduce the notions of strictly
(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})
-invariant and strictly dividing invariant complexes of sheaves on
SmlSm/k
.
We show that these two notions are equivalent.
Consequently, we provide another way of constructing objects of
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
and
{\bf logDM}_{d\acute{e}t}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
.

Definition 7.8.1
.

We denote by
(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})
the class of projections

(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})\times X\rightarrow X

for all
X\in SmlSm/k
and positive integers
n>0
.

Definition 7.8.2
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a complex of
t
-sheaves on
SmlSm/k
, where
t
is one of
sNis
,
s\acute{e}t
, or
k\acute{e}t
.
We say that
\mathcal{F}
is
strictly dividing invariant
if for every log modification
Y\rightarrow X
in
SmlSm/k
and integer
i\in\mathbb{Z}
there is an isomorphism

\mathbf{H}_{t}^{i}(X,\mathcal{F})\simeq\mathbf{H}_{t}^{i}(Y,\mathcal{F}).

We say that
\mathcal{F}
is
strictly
(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})
-invariant
if for every
X\in SmlSm/k
,
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, and integer
n>0
there is an isomorphism

\mathbf{H}_{t}^{i}(X,\mathcal{F})\simeq\mathbf{H}_{t}^{i}(X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1}),\mathcal{F}).

Theorem 7.8.3
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a complex of
t
-sheaves on
SmlSm/k
, where
t
is one of
sNis
,
s\acute{e}t
, or
k\acute{e}t
.
Then
\mathcal{F}
is strictly dividing invariant if and only if
\mathcal{F}
is strictly
(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})
-invariant.

Démonstration.

We only consider the strict Nisnevich topology since the proofs are similar.
Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\mathcal{F}
is strict Nisnevich local.

On
\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{Shv}_{sNis}(SmlSm/k,\Lambda))
we have the
div
-local descent model structure and the
(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})
-local descent model structure.
Let
\mathbf{Ho}_{div}
and
\mathbf{Ho}_{(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})}
denote the corresponding homotopy categories.
Proposition
7.0.2
shows the properties (
Zar
-sep), (
\overline{\Box}
-inv), (
sNis
-des), and (
div
-des) hold in
{\bf logDA}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
.
We can similarly prove that (
Zar
-sep), (
\overline{\Box}
-inv), (
sNis
-des), and (
div
-des) hold in
\mathbf{Ho}_{div}
,
while (
Zar
-sep), (
\overline{\Box}
-inv), (
sNis
-des), and (
(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})
-inv) hold in
\mathbf{Ho}_{(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})}
.
Owing to Proposition
7.3.1
and Theorem
7.7.4
,
we see that (
Zar
-sep), (
\overline{\Box}
-inv), (
sNis
-des), (
div
-des), and (
(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})
-inv) hold in both
\mathbf{Ho}_{div}

and
\mathbf{Ho}_{(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})}
.

Suppose now that
\mathcal{F}
is strictly dividing invariant.
Then
\mathcal{F}
is a fibrant object with respect to the
div
-local descent model structure on
\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{Shv}_{sNis}(SmlSm/k,\Lambda))
.
Thus for every
X\in SmlSm/k
and integer
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is an isomorphism

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{Ho}_{div}}(\Lambda(X),\mathcal{F}[i])\simeq\mathbf{H}_{sNis}^{i}(X,\mathcal{F}).

Since (
(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})
-inv) holds in
\mathbf{Ho}_{div}
this shows
\mathcal{F}
is strictly
(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})
-invariant.

Conversely, if
\mathcal{F}
is strictly
(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})
-invariant,
then
\mathcal{F}
is a fibrant object with respect to the
(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})
-local descent model structure on
\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{Shv}_{sNis}(SmlSm/k,\Lambda))
.
Thus for every
X\in SmlSm/k
and integer
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is an isomorphism

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{Ho}_{(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})}}(\Lambda(X),\mathcal{F}[i])\simeq\mathbf{H}_{sNis}^{i}(X,\mathcal{F}).

Since (
div
-des) holds in
\mathbf{Ho}_{(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})}
, this shows
\mathcal{F}
is strictly dividing invariant.
∎

Construction 7.8.4
.

Let us begin with a strictly
(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})
-invariant bounded below complex
\mathcal{F}
of strict Nisnevich sheaves with log transfers on
SmlSm/k
.
Owing to Theorem
7.8.3
we see that
\mathcal{F}
is strictly dividing invariant.
Applying Theorem
5.1.8
, this means that for every
X\in SmlSm/k
and integer
i\in\mathbb{Z}
there are isomorphisms

H_{dNis}^{i}(X,a_{dNis}^{*}\mathcal{F})\simeq\mathop{\rm colim}_{Y\in X_{div}^{Sm}}H_{sNis}^{i}(Y,\mathcal{F})\simeq H_{sNis}^{i}(X,\mathcal{F}).

(7.8.1)

This shows that
a_{dNis}^{*}\mathcal{F}
is strictly
\overline{\Box}
-invariant.
Then using Construction
5.4.3
, we see that
\iota_{\sharp}a_{dNis}^{*}\mathcal{F}
is strictly
\overline{\Box}
-invariant too.
In this way to obtain an object
\iota_{\sharp}a_{dNis}^{*}\mathcal{F}
of
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
from
\mathcal{F}
.
Combining (
5.4.2
) and (
7.8.1
) we have that

\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)}(M(X),\iota_{\sharp}a_{dNis}^{*}\mathcal{F})\simeq H_{dNis}^{i}(X,a_{dNis}^{*}\mathcal{F})\simeq H_{sNis}^{i}(X,\mathcal{F}).

Similarly, if
\mathcal{F}
is a strictly
(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})
-invariant bounded below complex of strict étale (resp. Kummer étale) sheaves with log transfers, then there is an isomorphism

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf logDM}_{d\acute{e}t}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)}(M(X),\iota_{\sharp}a_{d\acute{e}t}^{*}\mathcal{F})\simeq H_{s\acute{e}t}^{i}(X,\mathcal{F}),

(7.8.2)

\displaystyle\text{(resp.\ }\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf logDM}_{l\acute{e}t}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)}(M(X),\iota_{\sharp}a_{l\acute{e}t}^{*}\mathcal{F})\simeq H_{k\acute{e}t}^{i}(X,\mathcal{F}).\text{)}

(7.8.3)

8.
Comparison with Voevodsky’s motives

Our main results in this section are the projective bundle theorem and a comparison result showing that Voevodsky’s category of derived motives
{\bf DM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)

identifies with the full subcategory of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-local objects in our derived category of log motives
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
.

8.1.
Presheaves with transfers and direct image functors

Lemma 8.1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant Nisnevich sheaf with transfer on
Sm/k
.
Let
X
be a smooth scheme over
k
and let
j\colon U\rightarrow X
be an open immersion whose complement is a strict normal crossing divisor on
X
.
If
k
is perfect, then for any
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is an isomorphism

H_{Nis}^{i}(X,j_{*}j^{*}\mathcal{F})\simeq H_{Nis}^{i}(U,\mathcal{F}).

Démonstration.

Let
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{r}
be the divisors forming the complement of
j
, and set

U_{i}:=X-(Z_{i+1}\cup\cdots\cup Z_{r}).

Now let
j_{i}\colon U_{i}\rightarrow U_{i+1}
denote the induced open immersion.
By the Leray spectral sequence

E_{2}^{pq}=H^{p}(U_{i+1},R^{q}j_{i*}j_{i}^{*}\mathcal{F})\Rightarrow H^{p+q}(U_{i},\mathcal{F}),

it suffices to show that

R^{q}j_{i*}j_{i}^{*}\mathcal{F}=0

for any
q>0
.
Thus we reduce to the case when
X-U
is a smooth divisor on
X
.
Furthermore, we reduce to the case when
X
is the Hensel local scheme of a smooth scheme at a point.
Let
c\colon X-U\rightarrow X
denote the complement of
j\colon U\rightarrow X
.

For every
q\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
H_{Nis}^{q}(\mathcal{F})
denote the presheaf given by

X\mapsto H_{Nis}^{q}(X,\mathcal{F}).

Owing to
[
64
, Theorem 24.1]
the presheaf
H_{Nis}^{q}(\mathcal{F})
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant.
By
[
64
, Proposition 23.5, Example 23.8, Theorem 23.12]
we obtain the vanishing

R^{q}j_{*}j^{*}\mathcal{F}\simeq c_{*}H_{Nis}^{q}(\mathcal{F})_{(X\times\mathbb{A}^{1},Z\times\mathbb{A}^{1})}\simeq c_{*}(H_{Nis}^{q}(\mathcal{F})_{-1})_{Nis}=0

for
q>0
as in the proof of
[
64
, Proposition 24.3]
.
This allows us to proceed as in the proof of
[
64
, Proposition 24.3]
.
∎

Definition 8.1.2
.

Suppose
\mathcal{F}
is a presheaf on a category
\mathcal{C}
equipped with a topology
t
.
We denote the
t
-sheafification of
\mathcal{F}
by

\mathcal{F}_{t}:=a_{t}\mathcal{F}.

Definition 8.1.3

(cf.
[
88
, §4.2]
)
.

Let
X
be a smooth scheme over
k
with a strict normal crossing divisor
W
.
Let
Z
be an irreducible component of
W
with corresponding closed immersion
i\colon Z\rightarrow X
.
For a presheaf
\mathcal{F}
on
X
, we set

\mathcal{F}_{(X,W,Z)}:=i^{*}(\operatorname{Coker}(u_{*}u^{*}\mathcal{F}\rightarrow v_{*}v^{*}\mathcal{F}))_{Zar}.

Here
u\colon X-\overline{(W-Z)}\rightarrow X
and
v\colon X-W\rightarrow X
are the naturally induced open immersions.

There is an exact sequence

(u_{*}u^{*}\mathcal{F})_{Zar}\rightarrow(v_{*}v^{*}\mathcal{F})_{Zar}\rightarrow i_{*}\mathcal{F}_{(X,W,Z)}\rightarrow 0

(8.1.1)

of Zariski sheaves on
X
as in
[
88
, Remark 4.10]
.

Lemma 8.1.4
.

With the above notations, let
f\colon X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
be an étale morphism of smooth schemes over
k
such that
Z^{\prime}:=f^{-1}(Z)\rightarrow Z
is an isomorphism.
For
W^{\prime}:=f^{-1}(W)
there is a naturally induced isomorphism

\mathcal{F}_{(X,W,Z)}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathcal{F}_{(X^{\prime},W^{\prime},Z^{\prime})}

of sheaves on
Z
if
\mathcal{F}
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant presheaf with transfers.

Démonstration.

First note that
W^{\prime}
is a strict normal crossing divisor on
X^{\prime}
and
Z^{\prime}
is an irreducible compoenent of
Z^{\prime}
since
f
is étale, so we can use the notation
\mathcal{F}_{(X^{\prime},W^{\prime},Z^{\prime})}
.
Let

u^{\prime}\colon X^{\prime}-\overline{(W^{\prime}-Z^{\prime})}\rightarrow X^{\prime}

and

v^{\prime}\colon X^{\prime}-W^{\prime}\rightarrow X^{\prime}

be the naturally induced open immersions, and let
U
be an open subscheme of
X
.
For
U^{\prime}:=U\times_{X}X^{\prime}
we have the commutative diagram with exact rows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(U\times_{X}(X-\overline{(W-Z)}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(U\times_{X}(X-W))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Coker}(u_{*}u^{*}\mathcal{F}\rightarrow v_{*}v^{*}\mathcal{F})(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(U^{\prime}\times_{X^{\prime}}(X^{\prime}-\overline{(W^{\prime}-Z^{\prime})}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(U^{\prime}\times_{X^{\prime}}(X^{\prime}-W^{\prime}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Coker}(u_{*}^{\prime}u^{\prime*}\mathcal{F}\rightarrow v_{*}^{\prime}v^{\prime*}\mathcal{F})(U^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

Let
a
be an element of
\operatorname{Coker}(u_{*}u^{*}\mathcal{F}\rightarrow v_{*}v^{*}\mathcal{F})(U)
.
If
\varphi(a)=0
, then Zariski locally on
U
, we have
a=0
by
[
88
, Proposition 4.12(1)]
.
On the other hand, suppose
b
is an element of
\operatorname{Coker}(u_{*}^{\prime}u^{\prime*}\mathcal{F}\rightarrow v_{*}^{\prime}v^{\prime*}\mathcal{F})(U^{\prime})
.
Then, Zariski locally on
U
,
b
is in the image of
\varphi
by
[
88
, Proposition 4.12(2)]
.
∎

Lemma 8.1.5
.

Let
Z
be a smooth scheme over
k
with a strict normal crossing divisor
Y
, and let
j\colon Z-Y\rightarrow Z
be the open immersion.
For any
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant presheaf
\mathcal{F}
with transfers, there is a canonical isomorphism

(j_{*}j^{*}\mathcal{F}_{-1})_{Zar}\simeq\mathcal{F}_{(Z\times\mathbb{A}^{1},(Z\times\{0\})\cup(Y\times\mathbb{A}^{1}),Z\times\{0\})}

of Zariski sheaves on
Z
.

Démonstration.

We shall argue as in the proof of
[
88
, Proposition 4.11]
(see also the proof of
[
64
, Proposition 23.10]
).
Fix a point
z
of
Z
.
Let
U
be an affine open neighborhood of
z
in
Z
, and let
V
be an affine open neighborhood of
U\times\{0\}
in
U\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
.
Following the proof of
[
64
, Proposition 23.10]
,
there exists a neighborhood
U^{\prime}
of
z
in
U
and an affine open subscheme
T
of
\mathbb{P}_{k}^{1}
such that
U^{\prime}\times T
contains both
\mathbb{P}_{U^{\prime}}^{1}-V^{\prime}
and

U^{\prime}\times\{0\}
where
V^{\prime}:=(U^{\prime}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\times_{U\times\mathbb{A}^{1}}V
.
We form the fiber product
Y_{U^{\prime}}:=Y\times_{Z}U^{\prime}
.

Since a Cartier divisor gives
Y
, we may further assume that

U^{\prime}-Y_{U^{\prime}}
is affine,
and hence
(U^{\prime}-Y_{U^{\prime}})\times T
is affine.
Setting
V^{\prime\prime}:=V^{\prime}\times_{U^{\prime}}(U^{\prime}-Y_{U^{\prime}})
we deduce the standard triple (in the sense of
[
64
, Definition 11.5]
)

T_{U^{\prime},V^{\prime}}:=(\mathbb{P}_{U^{\prime}-Y_{U^{\prime}}}^{1},\mathbb{P}_{U^{\prime}-Y_{U^{\prime}}}^{1}-V^{\prime\prime},(U^{\prime}-Y_{U^{\prime}})\times\{0\}),

see the second paragraph in the proof of
[
64
, Proposition 23.10]
.
Next, we form

T_{U^{\prime}}:=(\mathbb{P}_{U^{\prime}-Y_{U^{\prime}}}^{1},(U^{\prime}-Y_{U^{\prime}})\times\{\infty\},(U^{\prime}-Y_{U^{\prime}})\times\{0\})\text{ and }V_{0}^{\prime\prime}:=V^{\prime\prime}-(U^{\prime}\times\{0\}).

Following the proof of
[
64
, Proposition 23.10]
we see the identity on
\mathbb{P}_{U^{\prime}}^{1}
is a finite morphism of standard triples
T_{U^{\prime},V^{\prime}}\rightarrow T_{U^{\prime}}

in the sense of
[
64
, Definition 21.1]
.
Using
[
64
, Theorem 21.6]
, we obtain a split exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mathcal{F}((U^{\prime}-Y_{U^{\prime}})\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}((U^{\prime}-Y_{U^{\prime}})\times(\mathbb{A}^{1}-\{0\}))\oplus\mathcal{F}(V^{\prime\prime})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}(V_{0}^{\prime\prime})\rightarrow 0.

Since
\mathcal{F}
is homotopy invariant, the morphism

\mathcal{F}((U^{\prime}-Y_{U^{\prime}})\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}((U^{\prime}-Y_{U^{\prime}})\times(\mathbb{A}^{1}-\{0\}))

is injective.
This implies that the morphism
\mathcal{F}(V^{\prime\prime})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}(V_{0}^{\prime\prime})
is injective and

(j_{*}j^{*}\mathcal{F}_{-1})(U^{\prime})\simeq\mathcal{F}(V_{0}^{\prime\prime})/\mathcal{F}(V^{\prime\prime}).

The right side is
\operatorname{Coker}(u_{*}u^{*}\mathcal{F}\rightarrow v_{*}v^{*}\mathcal{F})(V^{\prime})
,
where

u\colon(Z\times\mathbb{A}^{1})-(Y\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\rightarrow Z\times\mathbb{A}^{1},\;v\colon Z\times\mathbb{A}^{1}-((Z\times\{0\}\cup(Y\times\mathbb{A}^{1}))\rightarrow Z\times\mathbb{A}^{1}

are the naturally induced open immersions.
We are done by passing to the limit over all
U^{\prime}
and
V^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma 8.1.6
.

Let
X
be a scheme smooth over
k
with a strict normal crossing divisor
W
,
let
Z
be an irreducible component of
W
, and let

j\colon(X-\overline{(W-Z)})\times_{X}Z\rightarrow Z

be the naturally induced open immersion.
For any
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant presheaf
\mathcal{F}
with transfers,
Zariski locally on
X
,
there is an isomorphism

(j_{*}j^{*}\mathcal{F}_{-1})_{Zar}\simeq\mathcal{F}_{(X,W,Z)}

of Zariski shaves on
X
.

Démonstration.

Since the question is Zariski local on
X
,
we may assume there exists an étale morphism
f\colon X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{k}^{n}
of schemes over
k
such that
W
is the preimage of the union of axes in
\mathbb{A}_{k}^{n}
and

Z=f^{-1}(\{0\}\times\mathbb{A}_{k}^{n-1})
,
see Definition
7.2.1
.

Consider the morphism of closed pairs

(Z,X)\leftarrow(Z,X_{2})\rightarrow(Z,X_{1})

in Construction
7.2.8
.
Note that
X_{1}=Z\times\mathbb{A}^{m}
where
m
is the codimension of
Z
in
X
.
Using Lemma
8.1.4
twice, we can replace
(Z,X)
by
(Z,X_{1})
.
In this case,

W
becomes the strict normal crossing divisor

(Z\times\{0\})\cup((Z\cap(\overline{(W-Z)})\times\mathbb{A}^{1})

on
X_{1}=X\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
.
We are done by Lemma
8.1.5
.
∎

Remark 8.1.7
.

If
W=Z
, then Lemma
8.1.6
is a special case of
[
88
, Theorem 4.14]
.

Lemma 8.1.8
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant presheaf with transfer on
Sm/k
.
Let
X
be a smooth scheme over
k
and let
j\colon U\rightarrow X
be an open immersion whose complement is a strict normal crossing divisor
Z
on
X
.
If
k
is perfect, then the sheafification morphism
\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{Nis}
induces an isomorphism

(j_{*}j^{*}\mathcal{F})_{Nis}\xrightarrow{\simeq}(j_{*}j^{*}\mathcal{F}_{Nis})_{Nis}\simeq j_{*}j^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{Nis}).

(8.1.2)

Démonstration.

Assume the sheafification morphism
\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{Zar}
induces an isomorphism

(j_{*}j^{*}\mathcal{F})_{Zar}\xrightarrow{\simeq}(j_{*}j^{*}\mathcal{F}_{Zar})_{Zar}\simeq j_{*}j^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{Zar}).

(8.1.3)

The functors
j^{*}
and
j_{*}
map Nisnevich sheaves to Nisnevich sheaves, and
\mathcal{F}_{Zar}\simeq\mathcal{F}_{Nis}
by
[
88
, Proposition 5.5]
.
Thus
j_{*}j^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{Zar})
is a Nisnevich sheaf on
Sm/X
, so
(j_{*}j^{*}\mathcal{F})_{Zar}
is a Nisnevich sheaf on
Sm/X
due to (
8.1.3
).
It follows that (
8.1.2
) is an isomorphism.
Therefore it suffices to show that (
8.1.3
) is an isomorphism.

We proceed by induction on the number
r
of connected components of
Z
.
If
r=0
, then
j
is an isomorphism, and there is nothing to prove.
Suppose
r\geq 1
, and let
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{r}
be the irreducible components of
Z
.
For each
i
, set
S_{i}:=Z_{1}\cup\cdots\cup Z_{i}
, and let

u_{i}\colon X-S_{i}\rightarrow X,\;j_{i}\colon X-S_{i+1}\rightarrow X-S_{i},\;z_{i}\colon Z_{i}\rightarrow X

be the naturally induced immersions.

For any open immersion
v\colon V^{\prime}\rightarrow V
of schemes,
the functor
v^{*}
commutes with Zariski sheafification.
Furthermore, we have

v^{*}w_{*}\simeq w_{*}^{\prime}v^{\prime*}
where
v^{\prime}\colon V^{\prime}\times_{V}W\rightarrow W
and
w\colon V^{\prime}\times_{V}W\rightarrow W
are the projections.
Hence the problem is Zariski local on
X
.
Moreover,
by
[
88
, Corollary 4.19, Proposition 4.34]
, Lemma
8.1.6
and induction,
we may assume there are isomorphisms

\mathcal{F}_{(X,S_{i+1},Z_{i+1})}\simeq(j_{i*}j_{i}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{-1})_{Zar}\simeq j_{i*}j_{i}^{*}((\mathcal{F}_{Zar})_{-1})

and an exact sequence

0\rightarrow u_{i*}u_{i}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{Zar}\rightarrow u_{i+1,*}u_{i+1}^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{Zar})\rightarrow{z_{i}}_{*}(j_{i*}j_{i}^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{Zar})_{-1})\rightarrow 0

of Zariski sheaves on
X
.
Using (
8.1.1
), there is an induced commutative diagram with exact rows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(u_{i*}u_{i}^{*}\mathcal{F})_{Zar}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(u_{i+1,*}u_{i+1}^{*}\mathcal{F})_{Zar}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{i*}((j_{i*}j_{i}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{-1})_{Zar})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{i*}u_{i}^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{Zar})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{i+1,*}u_{i+1}^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{Zar})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{i*}(j_{i*}j_{i}^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{Zar})_{-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}

where the Zariski sheafification functor induces the vertical morphisms.
Here the left vertical morphism is an isomorphism by induction,
and the right vertical morphism is an isomorphism since
(j_{i*}j_{i}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{-1})_{Zar}\simeq j_{i*}j_{i}^{*}((F_{-1})_{Zar})
by induction on
r
and
(\mathcal{F}_{-1})_{Zar}\simeq(\mathcal{F}_{Zar})_{-1}
.
The snake lemma implies that also the middle vertical morphism is an isomorphism.

The above paragraph shows that
(u_{i*}u_{i}^{*}\mathcal{F})_{Zar}\rightarrow u_{i*}u_{i}^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{Zar})
is an isomorphism by induction on
i
.
When
i=0
, this is trivial since
u_{0}
is the identity.
Specializing to the case
i=r
finishes the proof because
u_{r}=j
.
∎

We refer to (
) the definition of the functor

\omega^{*}\colon\mathbf{Shv}_{Nis}^{\mathrm{tr}}(k,\Lambda)\rightarrow\mathbf{Shv}^{\mathrm{ltr}}_{dNis}(k,\Lambda).

Lemma 8.1.9
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a strictly
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant complex of Nisnevich sheaves with transfer on
Sm/k
.
Let
X
be a smooth scheme over
k
and let
j\colon U\rightarrow X
be an open immersion whose complement is a strict normal crossing divisor on
X
.
If
k
is perfect, then for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is an isomorphism

\mathbf{H}_{Nis}^{i}(X,j_{*}j^{*}\mathcal{F})\simeq\mathbf{H}_{Nis}^{i}(U,\mathcal{F}).

In particular, for every
Y\in SmlSm/k
and
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is an isomorphism

\mathbf{H}_{sNis}^{i}(Y,\omega^{*}\mathcal{F})\simeq\mathbf{H}_{Nis}^{i}(Y-\partial Y,\mathcal{F}).

Démonstration.

For any complex
\mathcal{G}
of presheaves and
i\in Z
, we let
H^{i}(\mathcal{G})
denote the associated cohomology presheaf.
The Nisnevich cohomological dimensions of
X
and
U
are finite,
see e.g.,
[
64
, Example 11.2]
.
Hence by
[
95
, 5.7.9]
, there are two strongly convergent hypercohomology spectral sequences
E
and
E^{\prime}
given by

E_{2}^{pq}=H_{Nis}^{p}(X,(H^{q}(j_{*}j^{*}\mathcal{F}))_{Nis})\Rightarrow\mathbf{H}^{p+q}(X,j_{*}j^{*}\mathcal{F}),

and

E_{2}^{\prime pq}=H_{Nis}^{p}(U,(H^{q}(\mathcal{F}))_{Nis})\Rightarrow\mathbf{H}^{p+q}(U,\mathcal{F}).

There is a naturally induced morphism of spectral sequences
E\rightarrow E^{\prime}
.

Since
j_{*}
and
j^{*}
are both exact functors between presheaf categories, there is an isomorphism

H^{q}(j_{*}j^{*}\mathcal{F})\simeq j_{*}j^{*}(H^{q}(\mathcal{F})).

Thus
(H^{q}(j_{*}j^{*}\mathcal{F}))_{Nis}\simeq j_{*}j^{*}(H^{q}(\mathcal{F})_{Nis})
by Lemma
8.1.8
.
We deduce the maps on
E_{2}
terms are isomorphisms by Lemma
8.1.1
since
(H^{q}(\mathcal{F}))_{Nis}
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant Nisnevich sheaf by
[
64
, Proposition 14.8]
.
Owing to the strong convergence of both the spectral sequences above, the induced map between the filtered target groups is also an isomorphism.
∎

Lemma 8.1.10
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a strictly
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant complex of Nisnevich sheaves with transfers on
Sm/k
.
For every
X\in lSm/k
and integer
i\in\mathbb{Z}
there is a naturally induced isomorphism

\mathbf{H}_{dNis}^{i}(X,\omega^{*}\mathcal{F})\simeq\mathbf{H}_{Nis}^{i}(X-\partial X,\mathcal{F}).

(8.1.4)

Démonstration.

If
Y\rightarrow X
is a log modification, there is a naturally induced isomorphism

Y-\partial Y\xrightarrow{\simeq}X-\partial X.

Due to Lemma
4.5.6
, we deduce the isomorphism

a_{dNis}^{*}\Lambda_{\mathrm{ltr}}(Y)\xrightarrow{\simeq}a_{dNis}^{*}\Lambda_{\mathrm{ltr}}(X).

Moreover, we conclude there are isomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{dNis}^{i}(X,\omega^{*}\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{Shv}^{\mathrm{ltr}}_{dNis}(k,\Lambda))}(a_{dNis}^{*}\Lambda_{\mathrm{ltr}}(X),\omega^{*}\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{Shv}^{\mathrm{ltr}}_{dNis}(k,\Lambda))}(a_{dNis}^{*}\Lambda_{\mathrm{ltr}}(Y),\omega^{*}\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbf{H}_{dNis}^{i}(Y,\omega^{*}\mathcal{F}).

This shows both sides of (
8.1.4
) are invariant under log modifications.
By Proposition
A.10.2
, we may assume that
\underline{X}
is smooth, and
\partial X
is a strict normal crossing divisor.

Choose a fibrant resolution
f\colon\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
in the model category
\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{Shv}_{Nis}^{\mathrm{tr}}(k,\Lambda))
with respect to the descent model structure.
This implies that
f
is a quasi-isomorphism and

\mathbf{H}_{Nis}^{i}(X-\partial X,\mathcal{F})\simeq H^{i}(\mathcal{G}(X-\partial X)).

(8.1.5)

Let us show that
\omega^{*}\mathcal{G}
is a fibrant object in the model category
\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{Shv}^{\mathrm{ltr}}_{dNis}(k,\Lambda))
for the descent model structure.
By definition of
\omega^{*}
, for every integer
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is an isomorphism

H^{i}(\omega^{*}\mathcal{G}(X))\simeq H^{i}(\mathcal{G}(X-\partial X)).

(8.1.6)

We need to show that for every dividing Nisnevich hypercover
\mathscr{X}\rightarrow X
we have an isomorphism

H^{i}(\omega^{*}\mathcal{G}(X))\rightarrow H^{i}({\rm Tot}^{\pi}(\omega^{*}\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{X}))),

see (
B.1.1
).

Since
\mathscr{X}-\partial\mathscr{X}\rightarrow X-\partial X
is a Nisnevich hypercover,
the assumption that
\mathcal{G}
is fibrant in
\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{Shv}_{Nis}^{\mathrm{tr}}(k,\Lambda))
implies

H^{i}(\mathcal{G}(X-\partial X))\simeq H^{i}({\rm Tot}^{\pi}\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{X}-\partial\mathscr{X})).

(8.1.7)

Using (
8.1.6
) we have that

H^{i}({\rm Tot}^{\pi}(\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{X}-\partial\mathscr{X})))\simeq H^{i}({\rm Tot}^{\pi}(\omega^{*}\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{X}))).

(8.1.8)

By combining (
8.1.6
), (
8.1.7
), and (
8.1.8
) we deduce that
\omega^{*}\mathcal{G}
is fibrant.
Hence there is an isomorphism

\mathbf{H}_{dNis}^{i}(X,\omega^{*}\mathcal{G})\simeq H^{i}(\omega^{*}\mathcal{G}(X)).

(8.1.9)

Due to Lemma
8.1.9
there are isomorphisms

\mathbf{H}_{sNis}^{i}(X,\omega^{*}\mathcal{F})\simeq\mathbf{H}_{Nis}^{i}(X-\partial X,\mathcal{F})

and

\mathbf{H}_{sNis}^{i}(X,\omega^{*}\mathcal{G})\simeq\mathbf{H}_{Nis}^{i}(X-\partial X,\mathcal{G}).

Since
f
is a quasi-isomorphism, we deduce that
\omega^{*}f
is a quasi-isomorphism for the strict Nisnevich topology.
Thus
\omega^{*}f
is a quasi-isomorphism for the dividing Nisnevich topology.
It follows that

\mathbf{H}_{dNis}^{i}(X,\omega^{*}\mathcal{F})\simeq\mathbf{H}_{dNis}^{i}(X,\omega^{*}\mathcal{G}).

(8.1.10)

To conclude the proof we combine (
8.1.5
), (
8.1.6
), (
8.1.9
), and (
8.1.10
).
∎

Lemma 8.1.11
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a Nisnevich (resp. an étale) sheaf on
Sm/k
.
Then
\omega^{*}\mathcal{F}
is a dividing Nisnevich (resp. log étale) sheaf on
lSm/k
.

Démonstration.

Let
p\colon U\rightarrow X
be a dividing Nisnevich (resp. log étale) cover.
We need to show that the naturally induced sequence

\omega^{*}\mathcal{F}(X)\rightarrow\omega^{*}\mathcal{F}(U)\rightrightarrows\omega^{*}\mathcal{F}(U\times_{X}U)

is exact.
This sequence is isomorphic to

\mathcal{F}(X-\partial X)\rightarrow\mathcal{F}(U-\partial U)\rightrightarrows\mathcal{F}(U-\partial U\times_{X-\partial X}U-\partial U),

which is exact since
U-\partial U\rightarrow X-\partial X
is a Nisnevich (resp. an étale) cover and
\mathcal{F}
is a Nisnevich (resp. an étale) sheaf.
∎

Proposition 8.1.12
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a strictly
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant complex of Nisnevich sheaves with transfers on
Sm/k
.
Then
\omega^{*}\mathcal{F}
is a both strictly
(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})
-invariant and strictly
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant complex of dividing Nisnevich sheaves with log transfers.

Démonstration.

Lemma
8.1.11
shows that
\omega^{*}\mathcal{F}
is a complex of dividing Nisnevich sheaves with transfers.
For
X\in lSm/k
, owing to Lemma
8.1.10
there are isomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{dNis}^{i}(X,\omega^{*}\mathcal{F})\simeq\mathbf{H}_{Nis}^{i}(X-\partial X,\mathcal{F}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{dNis}^{i}(X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1}),\omega^{*}\mathcal{F})\simeq\mathbf{H}_{Nis}^{i}((X-\partial X)\times\mathbb{A}^{n},\mathcal{F}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{dNis}^{i}(X\times\mathbb{A}^{1},\omega^{*}\mathcal{F})\simeq\mathbf{H}_{Nis}^{i}((X-\partial X)\times\mathbb{A}^{1},\mathcal{F}).

To conclude the proof, we use the assumption that
\mathcal{F}
is strict
