f\colon\underline{X}\to\underline{Y}
, the functor
f^{!}
can be defined by appropriately choosing a factorization of
f
as an open embedding followed by a proper map (but unless it agrees with
f^{\times}
, it is not the right adjoint to
Rf_{*}
). However, it can be computed explicitly in some cases of interest. If
\underline{X}
is smooth of dimension
d
over
k
, then there is a canonical isomorphism

\pi_{\underline{X}}^{!}k\simeq\Omega^{d}_{\underline{X}}[d].

Moreover,
for every
j\geq 0
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
there is an isomorphism (canonical up to the choice of a sign)

\Omega_{\underline{X}}^{j}[n]\xrightarrow{\simeq}D_{\underline{X}}(\Omega^{d-j}_{\underline{X}})[n-d].

The latter isomorphism arises from exterior products of forms

\Omega^{j}_{\underline{X}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathscr{H}\!\textit{om}_{\underline{X}}(\Omega^{d-j}_{\underline{X}},\Omega^{d}_{\underline{X}}),\quad\omega\mapsto(\eta\mapsto\omega\wedge\eta).

Our next goal is to extend the above results to the log setting.
For the analytic setting,
see Esnualt-Viehweg
[
33
]
,
which in turn was inspired by analogous results in the theory of
\mathcal{D}
-modules (see e.g., Bernstein
[
8
]
).
We begin with the following definition.

Definition 9.3.1
.

Let
X
be an fs log scheme in
SmlSm/k
.
By Lemma
A.5.10
, we have that
X=(\underline{X},\partial X)
,
where
\underline{X}
is a smooth
k
-scheme and
\partial X=\partial X_{1}+\ldots\partial X_{r}
is a strict normal crossing divisor on
\underline{X}
.
We let
I_{\partial X}
be the invertible sheaf of ideals defining
\partial X
.

Lemma 9.3.2
.

Let
X
be an integral fs log scheme in
SmlSm/k
of dimension
d_{X}
.
Then, for any
j\geq 0
and every
n
, there is an isomorphism

\theta_{X}\colon\Omega^{j}_{X}[n]=\Omega^{j}_{\underline{X}}(\log\partial X)[n]\xrightarrow{\simeq}D_{\underline{X}}(\Omega^{d_{X}-j}_{X}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\underline{X}}}I_{\partial X})[n-d_{X}]

(9.3.3)

in
{\bf D}^{b}(\underline{X})
.

Démonstration.

To prove (
), we note there is an isomorphism of sheaves

\Omega^{j}_{\underline{X}}(\log\partial X)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathscr{H}\!\textit{om}_{\underline{X}}(\Omega^{d_{X}-j}_{\underline{X}}(\log\partial X)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\underline{X}}}I_{\partial X},\Omega^{d_{X}}_{\underline{X}})

(9.3.4)

induced by the exterior product of forms

\omega\mapsto(\beta\otimes u\mapsto\omega\wedge(\beta\otimes u)),

where
u
is a local section of
I_{\partial X}
.
Here we are implicitly making a choice of sign since the assignment
\beta\otimes u\mapsto\beta\wedge(\omega\otimes u)
is equally valid.
We note the inclusion

\Omega^{d_{X}-j}_{\underline{X}}(\log\partial X)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\underline{X}}}I_{\partial X}\subset\Omega^{d_{X}-j}_{\underline{X}}

(every section of the sheaf is a regular differential).

To show (
) is an isomorphism, we resort to computing in local coordinates.
We have

\Omega^{d_{X}}_{\underline{X}}(\log\partial X)\otimes I_{\partial X}\simeq\Omega^{d_{X}}_{\underline{X}},

since

(d\log x_{1}\wedge\ldots d\log x_{r}\wedge dx_{r+1}\wedge\ldots dx_{d_{X}})\,x_{1}\cdots x_{r}=dx_{1}\wedge\ldots dx_{d_{X}},

where
(x_{1}\cdots x_{r})=I_{\partial X}
locally around any point of
\partial X
.
Next, note that for every integer
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}\!\textit{om}_{\underline{X}}(\Omega^{d_{X}-j}_{\underline{X}}(\log\partial X)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\underline{X}}}I_{\partial X},\Omega^{d_{X}}_{\underline{X}})[n]

(9.3.5)

\displaystyle=\mathscr{H}\!\textit{om}^{\bullet}_{\underline{X}}(\Omega^{d_{X}-j}_{\underline{X}}(\log\partial X)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\underline{X}}}I_{\partial X},\Omega^{d_{X}}_{\underline{X}}[d_{X}])[n-d_{X}]

(sheaf
{\rm Hom}
complex)

\displaystyle=R{\mathscr{H}\!\textit{om}}_{\underline{X}}(\Omega^{d_{X}-j}_{\underline{X}}(\log\partial X)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\underline{X}}}I_{\partial X},\pi_{X}^{!}(k))[n-d_{X}]

(
\Omega^{d_{X}-j}_{\underline{X}}(\log\partial X)
is locally free)

Here,
we have used the notation
\mathscr{H}\!\textit{om}_{\underline{X}}^{\bullet}
for the inner Hom of complexes of sheaves on
\underline{X}
, and
R\mathscr{H}\!\textit{om}
for the derived functor of
\mathscr{H}\!\textit{om}_{\underline{X}}
.
By combining (
) with (
9.3.5
) we get the desired isomorphism.
∎

Remark 9.3.3
.

(1)

One can use the twisted dualizing functor

D_{X}=D_{\underline{X}}(-\otimes I_{\partial X})

to prove that (
) holds more generally for the logarithmic de Rham complex
DR_{\partial X}(\mathcal{V})
associated to a coherent

\mathcal{O}_{\underline{X}}
-module
\mathcal{V}
equipped with a meromorphic connection with regular singularities along
\partial X
.
For details see
[
33
, (A.2)]
.

(2)

Let
j\colon U=\underline{X}-\partial X\hookrightarrow\underline{X}
be the open immersion of the complement of the boundary divisor on
\underline{X}
.
Then, for every
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\Omega^{j}_{U}=(\Omega^{j}_{X})_{|U}\simeq D_{\underline{X}}(\Omega^{d_{X}-j}_{X}\otimes I_{\partial X})_{|U}[-d_{X}]=D_{U}(\Omega^{d_{X}-j}_{U})[-d_{X}].

9.4.
Cohomology with support and cycle classes

Following
[
17
]
, in order to define the action of finite log correspondences on logarithmic differentials,
we will need to construct a cycle class in Hodge cohomology associated with a closed subscheme.
A priori, this is potentially a subtle point since we might need to deal with closed immersions of schemes that are not strict as morphisms of log schemes
(this is the case for an arbitrary finite log correspondence).
As discussed later in this section, this can be avoided,
and the following discussion of cohomology with support in the log setting suffices for our purposes.

Construction 9.4.1
.

Let
X
be an integral fs log scheme of finite type over
k
,
and let
v\colon Z\hookrightarrow X
be a strictly closed immersion of log schemes.
For
\mathcal{F}
a Zariski sheaf of abelian groups on
X_{Zar}
we set

\Gamma_{Z}(X,\mathcal{F}):=\operatorname{Ker}(\mathcal{F}(X)\to\mathcal{F}(X-Z)).

It is elementary to see that the functor
\Gamma_{Z}(X,-)
is left exact, and there exists an induced derived functor

R\Gamma_{Z}(X,-):\mathbf{D}^{b}(X_{Zar})\rightarrow\mathbf{D}^{b}({\bf Ab}).

We define cohomology with support by

H_{Z}^{i}(X,\mathcal{F}):=R\Gamma_{Z}^{i}(X,\mathcal{F}).

Note that we have
R\Gamma_{X}(X,-)=R\Gamma(X,-)
.
For
Z\subset X
strict as above, we define the
Hodge cohomology of
X
with support in
Z
(cf.
[
17
, §2]
) as the sum

H_{\rm Hodge}^{*}(X,Z)=\bigoplus_{i,j\geq 0}H^{i}_{Z}(X,\Omega^{j}_{X}).

If
Z
is empty, we write
H_{\rm Hodge}^{*}(X)
for
H_{\rm Hodge}^{*}(X,\emptyset)
.

Construction 9.4.2
.

For
f\colon X\to Y
a morphism and
Z
a strictly closed subscheme of
Y
,
we write
f^{-1}(Z)
for
X\times_{Y}Z
seen as a strictly closed subscheme of
X
.
There is an isomorphism of functors

R\Gamma_{f^{-1}(Z)}\xrightarrow{\simeq}R\Gamma_{Z}Rf_{*}.

We say that a strict closed subscheme
W
of
X
contains
f^{-1}(Z)
if the strict closed immersion
f^{-1}(Z)\hookrightarrow X
admits a factorization
f^{-1}(Z)\subset W\hookrightarrow X
.
In this situation the latter isomorphism furnishes a natural transformation

f^{*}\colon R\Gamma_{Z}\to R\Gamma_{Z}Rf_{*}Lf^{*}\xrightarrow{\simeq}R\Gamma_{f^{-1}(Z)}Lf^{*}\to R\Gamma_{W}Lf^{*}.

(9.4.1)

See
[
17
, §2.1.5]
for more details.
The displayed natural transformations are morphisms in the category of functors from
\mathbf{D}_{qc}({Y}_{Zar})
to
\mathbf{D}({\bf Ab})
, and restricts to a morphism in the category of functors from
\mathbf{D}^{b}({Y}_{Zar})
to
\mathbf{D}^{b}({\bf Ab})
when
f
has finite Tor dimension.

Note that here the log structures on
X
and
Y
are irrelevant since (
) only involves standard operations on sheaves.
The pullback
f^{*}
in (
) is compatible with composition, see
[
17
, (2.1.8)]
.

Remark 9.4.3
.

(1)

Every object and every morphism involved in the natural transformation (
) belong to the bounded derived category of coherent sheaves on
\underline{X}
or
\underline{Y}
.
In particular,
the sheaf

\Omega^{j}_{X}=\Omega^{j}_{\underline{X}}(\log\partial X)

is considered as a coherent sheaf on
\underline{X}
(and likewise for
\Omega^{j}_{Y}
and
\underline{Y}
).
In other words,
the log structures on
X
and
Y
do not play any role in defining the derived categories that we consider.
Moreover, the sheaves of log differentials completely capture the log geometric information.

Since
\Omega^{j}_{Y}
is locally free when
Y\in SmlSm/k
,
we have
Lf^{*}\Omega^{j}_{Y}=f^{*}\Omega^{j}_{Y}
and composing with the morphism
f^{*}\Omega^{j}_{Y}\to\Omega^{j}_{X}
from
[
76
, Proposition IV.1.2.15]
we obtain

f^{*}\colon R\Gamma_{Z}\Omega^{j}_{Y}\to R\Gamma_{W}\Omega^{j}_{X}.

In particular, this yields the pullback morphism in Hodge cohomology

f^{*}\colon H^{*}_{\rm Hodge}(Y,Z)\to H^{*}_{\rm Hodge}(X,W).

In the above
Y\in SmlSm/k
for simplicity, we do not impose the same assumption on
X
.

(2)

If
X\in SmlSm/k
, there is a canonical morphism to the underlying scheme
p_{X}\colon X\to\underline{X}
.
For every (irreducible) closed subscheme
\underline{Z}
of
\underline{X}
, the morphism

Z=\underline{Z}\times_{\underline{X}}X\to X

is a strictly closed immersion (
Z
’s log structure is given by restricting
X
’s log structure).
Applying Construction
9.4.2
to
p_{X}
yields the morphism

p_{X}^{*}\colon H^{*}_{\rm Hodge}(\underline{X},\underline{Z})=\bigoplus_{i,j}H^{i}_{\underline{Z}}(\underline{X},\Omega_{\underline{X}}^{j})\to\bigoplus_{i,j}H^{i}_{Z}(X,\Omega^{j}_{X})=H^{*}_{\rm Hodge}(X,Z).

Here
H^{i}_{Z}(X,\Omega^{j}_{X})
is the cohomology group with support
H^{i}_{\underline{Z}}(\underline{X},\Omega^{j}_{\underline{X}}(\log\partial X))
.
The morphism
p_{X}^{*}
is induced by the inclusion of locally free sheaves

\Omega^{j}_{\underline{X}}\to\Omega^{j}_{\underline{X}}(\log\partial X)

on
\underline{X}
or, intrinsically using log geometry, is the canonical morphism discussed in
[
76
, Example IV 1.2.17]
.

(3)

We can further compose the morphism in (2) with the morphism induced by the open immersion
j\colon X-\partial X\to X
to obtain

H^{*}_{\rm Hodge}(\underline{X},\underline{Z})\xrightarrow{p_{X}^{*}}H^{*}_{\rm Hodge}(X,Z)\xrightarrow{j^{*}}H^{*}_{\rm Hodge}(X-\partial X,Z-\partial Z),

where
Z-\partial Z=Z\cap(X-\partial X)
.
The composite morphism is induced by

\Omega^{j}_{\underline{X}}\to j_{*}\Omega^{j}_{X-\partial X},

which clearly factors through the sheaf
\Omega^{j}_{\underline{X}}(\log\partial X)
.

Next, we discuss a derived pushforward functor following the script in
[
17
, §2.2]
.
Owing to Remark
9.4.3
(3) it suffices to perform our constructions in the bounded derived category
\mathbf{D}^{b}(\underline{X})
.

Construction 9.4.4
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a proper map between integral fs log schemes.
We will assume
Y\in SmlSm/k
and that
X
is normal with compactifying Deligne-Faltings log structure
\partial X\to X
,
where
\partial X
is an effective Cartier divisor on
X
.
A particular case is the boundary of
X\in lSm/k
.
Recall that
D_{\underline{X}}(-)
is the dualizing functor on
\mathbf{D}^{b}(\underline{X})
.
Using standard coherent duality theory
[
40
]
we obtain a morphism in
\mathbf{D}^{b}(\underline{Y})

f_{*}\colon Rf_{*}D_{\underline{X}}(\Omega^{q}_{X})\to D_{\underline{Y}}(\Omega^{q}_{Y}),

(9.4.2)

defined as the composition

\displaystyle Rf_{*}R\mathscr{H}\!\textit{om}_{\underline{X}}(\Omega^{q}_{X},\pi_{\underline{X}}^{!}(k))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}Rf_{*}R\mathscr{H}\!\textit{om}_{\underline{X}}(\Omega^{q}_{X},f^{!}\pi_{\underline{Y}}^{!}(k))

\displaystyle\to R\mathscr{H}\!\textit{om}_{\underline{Y}}(Rf_{*}\Omega^{q}_{X},Rf_{*}f^{!}\pi_{\underline{Y}}^{!}(k))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{Tr_{f}}R\mathscr{H}\!\textit{om}_{\underline{Y}}(Rf_{*}\Omega^{q}_{X},\pi_{\underline{Y}}^{!}(k))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{f^{*}}R\mathscr{H}\!\textit{om}_{\underline{Y}}(\Omega^{q}_{Y},\pi_{\underline{Y}}^{!}(k)).

Here
Tr_{f}
is gotten from the trace morphism
Rf_{*}f^{!}\to\operatorname{id}
, and
f^{*}\colon\Omega_{Y}^{q}\to Rf_{*}\Omega^{q}_{X}
is the pullback of forms with log poles.
If we further assume that
f
is a strict morphism of log schemes,
then we have

\Omega^{q}_{X}\otimes I_{\partial X}=\Omega^{q}_{X}\otimes f^{*}I_{\partial Y}

where
f^{*}
denotes the pullback of coherent sheaves (locally free, in this case).
Further the strictness assumption implies
f^{-1}(\partial Y)=\partial X
as effective Cartier divisors on
X
.
Thus the construction of (
) furnishes a morphism

f_{*}\colon Rf_{*}D_{\underline{X}}(\Omega^{q}_{X}\otimes I_{\partial X})\to D_{\underline{Y}}(\Omega^{q}_{Y}\otimes I_{\partial Y}).

(9.4.3)

Remark 9.4.5
.

The assumption that
Y\in SmlSm/k
and
X
has a compactifying Deligne-Faltings log structure is needed to incorporate
the ideal sheaves
I_{\partial X}
and
I_{\partial Y}
in Construction
9.4.4
,
but it is not used to define (
).
In Construction
9.5.3
we use the assumption on
X
to define push forward with
proper support.

For the proof of the following result, we refer the reader to
[
17
, Proposition 2.2.7]
.

Lemma 9.4.6
.

For the pushforward morphism in (
) we have
(\operatorname{id})_{*}=\operatorname{id}
.
If
f\colon X\to Y
and
g\colon Y\to Z
are proper maps between fs log schemes in
SmlSm/k
, then

(g\circ f)_{*}=g_{*}\circ Rg_{*}(f_{*})\colon Rg_{*}Rf_{*}D_{\underline{X}}(\Omega^{q}_{X})\to D_{\underline{Z}}(\Omega^{q}_{Z})

for every
q\geq 0
.
Moreover, if
f
and
g
are strict, the same holds for the pushforward morphism in (
).

Let
i\colon X\hookrightarrow Y
be a strict closed immersion of fs log schemes in
SmlSm/k
,
and assume that
\underline{X}
has pure codimension
c
in
\underline{Y}
,
so that
\underline{X}\hookrightarrow\underline{Y}
is a closed immersion of smooth schemes of pure codimension
c
.
If we combine (
) with Lemma
9.3.2
then for every
g\geq 0
we have the composite

i_{*}\Omega^{q}_{X}\xrightarrow{i_{*}\theta_{X}}i_{*}D_{\underline{X}}(\Omega_{X}^{d_{X}-q}\otimes I_{\partial X})[-d_{X}]\xrightarrow{i_{*}}D_{\underline{Y}}(\Omega_{Y}^{d_{X}-q}\otimes I_{\partial Y})[-d_{X}]\xrightarrow{\theta_{Y}}\Omega^{c+q}_{Y}[c].

(9.4.4)

Here
\theta_{X}
is the duality isomorphism (
) for
X
,

\theta_{Y}
is the duality isomorphism for
Y
,
and
d_{X}
is the dimension of
X
.

Lemma 9.4.7
.

Let
i\colon X\hookrightarrow Y
be a strictly closed immersion of fs log schemes in
SmlSm/k
.
Let
f\colon Y^{\prime}\to Y
be a morphism in
SmlSm/k
and assume there is a cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

where
X^{\prime}\in SmlSm/k
.
Assume that
\underline{X}
has pure codimension
c=d_{Y}-d_{X}=d_{Y^{\prime}}-d_{X^{\prime}}
, where
d_{(-)}
denotes the dimension of a scheme.

Then for all
q\geq 0
, there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}Rf^{\prime}_{*}\Omega_{X}^{\prime q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rf_{*}\Omega^{c+q}_{Y^{\prime}}[c]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}\Omega^{q}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{c+q}_{Y}[c].}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eqref{eq :push3closedimm}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f^{\prime})^{*}}
(
)
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}

Démonstration.

The proof requires an explicit description of the map (
) using local cohomology sheaves.
Since the log structure does not cause any complications,
the argument in
[
17
, Corollary 2.2.22]
applies,
mutatis mutandis
, to our situation.
∎

Assume that
\underline{X}
is integral and smooth.
Applying
R\Gamma_{X}(Y,-)
to (
), we get a morphism

R\Gamma(X,\Omega^{q}_{X})=R\Gamma_{X}(Y,Ri_{*}\Omega^{q}_{X})=R\Gamma_{X}(Y,i_{*}\Omega^{q}_{X})\to R\Gamma_{X}(Y,\Omega^{c+q}_{Y})[c]

in
\mathbf{D}^{b}(\mathbf{Ab})
.
Furthermore, on cohomology, we obtain

H(i_{*})\colon H^{*}(X,\Omega^{q}_{X})\to H^{*+c}_{X}(Y,\Omega^{q+c}_{Y}).

In particular, for
q=*=0
, there is a morphism

H(i_{*})\colon H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})\to H^{c}_{X}(Y,\Omega_{Y}^{c}).

(9.4.5)

The image of
1\in H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
along the map
H(i_{*})
of (
) is called the
cycle class
of the strict closed subscheme
X
in
Y
.
In order to extend the definition to an arbitrary strict closed subscheme, we first establish some auxiliary results.

Lemma 9.4.8
.

Let
\underline{X}
be a smooth and separated scheme over
k
, and let
\underline{Z}
be a closed subscheme of
\underline{X}
of pure codimension
c
.
Let
D
be a strict normal crossing divisor on
\underline{X}
.
Then, for any
i<c
and
j
, we have the vanishing

H_{\underline{Z}}^{i}(\underline{X},\Omega_{\underline{X}}^{j}(\log D))=0.

Démonstration.

By the localization sequence, one reduces to the case where
X
is local, and
Z
is the closed point.
In this case, the assertion follows from the vanishing of local cohomology, using that
\Omega_{\underline{X}}^{j}(\log D)
is locally free and that a regular local ring is Gorenstein,
see e.g.,
[
41
, Theorem 6.3]
.
∎

Lemma
9.4.8
immediately implies the following result.

Lemma 9.4.9
.

Let
\underline{X}
be a smooth and separated scheme over
k
,
and let

\underline{W}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu}}{{\rightarrow}}\underline{Z}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nu}}{{\rightarrow}}\underline{X}

be closed immersions such that the codimension of
\nu
(resp.
\mu
) is
c
(resp.
\geq 1
).
Then the naturally induced homomorphism

H_{\underline{Z}}^{c}(\underline{X},\Omega_{\underline{X}}^{c})\rightarrow H_{\underline{Z}-\underline{W}}^{c}(\underline{X}-\underline{W},\Omega_{\underline{X}-\underline{W}}^{c})

is injective.

We can now recall the definition of Grothendieck’s “fundamental class” for a closed subscheme of a smooth
k
-scheme.

Lemma 9.4.10
.

Let
\underline{X}
be a smooth and separated scheme over
k
, and let
\underline{Z}
be a closed subscheme of
\underline{X}
of pure codimension
c
.
Consider the open immersion
j\colon U=\underline{X}-\underline{Z}_{sing}\rightarrow\underline{X}
, and consider the induced homomorphism (
)

H(i_{*})\colon H_{\underline{Z}_{\rm reg}}^{0}(\underline{Z}_{\rm reg},\mathcal{O}_{\underline{Z}_{\rm reg}})\rightarrow H_{\underline{Z}_{\rm reg}}^{c}(U,\Omega_{U}^{c}),

where
\underline{Z}_{\rm reg}
is the regular (equivalenty, smooth, since
k
is perfect) locus of
\underline{Z}
.
Then the following statements hold.

(1)

The naturally induced homomorphism

j^{*}:H_{\underline{Z}}^{c}(\underline{X},\Omega_{\underline{X}}^{c})\rightarrow H_{\underline{Z}_{reg}}^{c}(U,\Omega_{U}^{c}).

is injective.

(2)

There exists a unique element
cl(\underline{Z})
in
H_{\underline{Z}}^{c}(\underline{X},\Omega_{\underline{X}}^{c})
such that

j^{*}(cl(\underline{Z}))=H(i_{*})(1).

Démonstration.

Assertion (1) follows from Lemma
9.4.9
and assertion (2) is explained in
[
17
, Proposition 3.1.1]

∎

Putting the above together, we arrive at the following construction of a cycle class.

Definition 9.4.11
.

Let
X
be an integral fs log scheme in
SmlSm/k
,
and let
\nu:Z\rightarrow X
be a strict closed immersion of fs log schemes such that
\underline{Z}
has pure codimension
c
in
\underline{X}
.
In this setting, we define the
cycle class
of
Z

cl(Z)\in H_{Z}^{c}(X,\Omega_{X}^{c})

as follows :
Let
j\colon X-\partial X\rightarrow X
and
p_{X}\colon X\rightarrow\underline{X}
denote the canonical morphisms to the underlying scheme of
X
.
In degree
c
we have the homomorphisms
p_{X}^{*}
and
j^{*}

H_{\underline{Z}}^{c}(\underline{X},\Omega_{\underline{X}}^{c})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{X}^{*}}}{{\rightarrow}}H_{Z}^{c}(X,\Omega_{X}^{c})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j^{*}}}{{\rightarrow}}H_{Z-\partial Z}^{c}(X-\partial X,\Omega_{X-\partial X}^{c})

from Remark
9.4.3
(3).
Since pullbacks are compatible with compositions,
the composition in question is the restriction map
\underline{j}^{*}
for cohomology with support on the underlying schemes (both
\underline{X}
and
X-\partial X
have trivial log structures).
Lemma
9.4.9
shows
\underline{j}^{*}
is injective.
It follows that also
p_{X}^{*}
is injective,
and we set

cl(Z):=p^{*}_{X}(cl(\underline{Z})).

Note that
j^{*}cl(Z)\simeq cl(Z-\partial Z)
by Lemma
9.4.10
and Lemma
9.4.7
.
By the same token, when
Z\in SmlSm/k
, the class
cl(Z)
agrees with
H(i_{*})(1)
of (
).

9.5.
Pushforward with proper support

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a strict morphism of integral fs log schemes in
SmlSm/k
,
and let
u\colon Z\hookrightarrow X
be a strictly closed immersion of fs log schemes.
We will assume that the restriction of
f
to
Z
is proper and surjective onto
Y
.

Definition 9.5.1
.

A good compactification of
f
as above is a factorization

f=\overline{f}\circ j\colon X\hookrightarrow\overline{X}\xrightarrow{\overline{f}}Y,

where
\overline{X}\to Y
is a proper and strict map,

X\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
is a dense open immersion, and

\overline{X}
is an integral normal fs log scheme with compactifying Deligne-Faltings log structure
\partial X\to X
,
where
\partial X
an effective Cartier divisor on
\underline{X}
.

Remark 9.5.2
.

A good compactification exists for every
f
as above.
Indeed,
Nagata’s compactification theorem
[
23
]
shows that every separated morphism of finite type
\underline{f}\colon\underline{X}\to\underline{Y}
between Noetherian schemes admits a compactification,
i.e.,
there exists a factorization into an open immersion followed by a proper map
\overline{f}\colon\underline{X}^{\prime}\to Y
.
Letting
\overline{X}
denote the fs log scheme with log structure
(\overline{f}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{Y})_{\rm log}
on
\underline{X}^{\prime}
we obtain a morphism
\overline{f}\colon\overline{X}\to Y
.
By a standard argument,
we may assume that
\underline{X}^{\prime}
is normal (simply take the normalization of
\underline{X}
which does not alter the interior) and that the subscheme
\partial X
,
where the log structure is nontrivial,
is the support of an effective Cartier divisor.
If we further assume that resolution of singularities holds over
k
, we may assume
\overline{X}\in SmlSm/k
.

Construction 9.5.3
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a strict morphism of integral schemes in
SmlSm/k
.
Let
u:Z\rightarrow X
be a strictly closed immersion of fs log schemes and assume that the restriction of
f
to
Z
is proper and surjective.
Set
d_{X}:=\dim X
,
d_{Y}:=\dim Y
, and
r:=d_{X}-d_{Y}
.
In this setting,
for every
i
and
j
, we will construct the pushforward morphism

H(f_{*}):H_{Z}^{i+r}(X,\Omega_{X}^{j+r})\rightarrow H^{i}(Y,\Omega_{Y}^{j}).

(9.5.1)

First recall from Lemma
9.3.2
the duality isomorphisms

\displaystyle\theta_{X}\colon H_{Z}^{i+r}(X,\Omega_{X}^{j+r})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}H_{Z}^{i+r-d_{X}}(X,D_{\underline{X}}(\Omega_{X}^{d_{X}-j-r}\otimes I_{\partial X}))

(9.5.2)

\displaystyle=H_{Z}^{i-d_{Y}}(X,D_{\underline{X}}(\Omega_{X}^{d_{Y}-j}\otimes I_{\partial X})),

and

\theta_{Y}\colon H^{i+r}(Y,\Omega_{Y}^{j+r})\xrightarrow{\simeq}H^{i-d_{Y}}(Y,D_{\underline{Y}}(\Omega_{Y}^{d_{Y}-j}\otimes I_{\partial Y})).

(9.5.3)

Here
\theta_{X}
is obtained from (
) by applying
R\Gamma_{Z}(X,-)
.
Thus we are left to construct

H_{Z}^{i-d_{Y}}(X,D_{\underline{X}}(\Omega_{X}^{d_{Y}-j}\otimes I_{\partial X}))\rightarrow H^{i-d_{Y}}(Y,D_{\underline{Y}}(\Omega_{Y}^{d_{Y}-j}\otimes I_{\partial Y})).

If
f
is proper,
this is precisely the content of (
) after applying the cohomology with support functor
(here, we use the surjectivity of the map from
Z
to
Y
; otherwise, we would have to introduce support in the target).
When
f
is not proper, choose a good compactification
\overline{f}
in the sense of Definition
9.5.1
, which is possible by Remark
9.5.2
.
Then we have a natural isomorphism

H_{Z}^{i-d_{Y}}(X,D_{\underline{X}}(\Omega_{X}^{d_{Y}-j}\otimes I_{\partial X}))\xrightarrow{\simeq}H_{Z}^{i-d_{Y}}(\overline{X},D_{\underline{\overline{X}}}(\Omega_{\overline{X}}^{d_{Y}-j}\otimes I_{\partial{\overline{X}}}))

(9.5.4)

by excision since
Z\subset X\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
is a strict closed immersion.
Applying (
) to
\overline{f}
we get

H_{Z}^{i-d_{Y}}(\overline{X},D_{\underline{\overline{X}}}(\Omega_{\overline{X}}^{d_{Y}-j}\otimes I_{\partial{\overline{X}}}))\to H^{i-d_{Y}}(Y,D_{\underline{Y}}(\Omega_{Y}^{d_{Y}-j}\otimes I_{\partial Y})).

(9.5.5)

Composing (
9.5.2
), (
), (
) and the inverse of (
), we obtain (
9.5.1
).

The following lemma is a routine check, see
[
17
, Definition 2.3.2, Proposition 2.3.3]
.

Lemma 9.5.4
.

The map (
9.5.1
) is independent of the choice of compactification.
Let
f\colon X\to Y
and
g\colon Y\to V
be two strict morphisms of integral fs log schemes in
SmlSm/k
such that the restrictions
f\circ u
and
g\circ f\circ u
are proper and surjective.
If we set
l=\dim X-\dim V
,
then

H((g\circ f)_{*})=H(g_{*})\circ H(f_{*})\colon H^{i+l}_{Z}(X,\Omega^{j+l}_{X})\to H^{i}(V,\Omega^{j}_{V}).

9.6.
The action of log correspondences

Suppose
Z
is an elementary log correspondence from
X
to
Y
in the sense of Definition
2.1.1
, where
X,Y\in SmlSm/k
.
Our aim in this subsection is to construct a pullback morphism

Z^{*}\colon\Omega^{j}(Y)\rightarrow\Omega^{j}(X).

If we extend by linearity to all finite log correspondences and verify compatibility with the composition,
this equips the logarithmic differential
\Omega^{j}
with the structure of a presheaf with log transfers.

Lemma 9.6.1
.

Let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

be a cartesian square of fs log schemes.
Suppose that
g^{\prime}
is exact.
If there exists a morphism
h:X\rightarrow T
such that
hg
is strict, then
g^{\prime}
is strict.

Démonstration.

Owing to
[
76
, Definition III.1.2.1]
there are naturally induced morphisms

g_{log}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{X^{\prime}},\;h_{log}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{T}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{X},\;(hg)_{log}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{T}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{X^{\prime}}.

Since
hg
is strict, the third morphism is an isomorphism.
This implies that the first morphism is an epimorphism,
so that

\mathcal{M}_{X^{\prime}/X}:=\operatorname{Coker}(g_{log}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{X^{\prime}})=0.

The question is Zariski local on
X^{\prime}
and
X
, so owing to
[
76
, Theorem III.1.2.7]
, we may assume that
g
has a neat chart
\theta:P\rightarrow P^{\prime}
(
[
76
, Definition II.2.4.4]
).
This shows
\theta^{\rm gp}
is an isomorphism since
\mathcal{M}_{X^{\prime}/X}=0
.
The question is also Zariski local on
Y
so that we may assume
f
admits an fs chart
P\rightarrow Q
.

By the construction of fiber products in the category of fs log schemes,
Y^{\prime}
has an fs chart

Q^{\prime}:=P^{\prime}\oplus_{P}Q,

which is an amalgamated sum in the category of fs monoids.
By
[
76
, Theorem I.1.3.4]
we have an isomorphism

Q^{\prime\rm gp}\simeq P^{\prime\rm gp}\oplus_{P^{\rm gp}}Q^{\rm gp}.

Since
\theta^{\rm gp}:P^{\rm gp}\rightarrow P^{\prime\rm gp}
is an isomorphism, we see that
Q^{\rm gp}\rightarrow Q^{\prime\rm gp}
is an isomorphism.
This implies that
Q\rightarrow Q^{\prime}
is an isomorphism since
g^{\prime}
is exact.
Thus
g^{\prime}
is strict.
∎

Construction 9.6.2
.

Let
Z
be an elementary log correspondence from
X
to
Y
where
X,Y\in SmlSm/k
.
We will first construct

Z^{*}:\Omega^{j}(Y)\rightarrow\Omega^{j}(X)

in the case when
X
is a quasi-projective scheme and has an fs chart
P
.
By definition, there is a strict finite surjective morphism
Z^{N}\rightarrow X
and a morphism
Z^{N}\rightarrow Y
.
By Theorem
A.14.13
,
there exists a log blow-up
V\rightarrow X\times Y
such that the pullback
f^{\prime}:Z^{N}\times_{(X\times Y)}V\rightarrow V
of
f\colon Z^{N}\to X\times Y
is exact.
Using Lemma
9.6.1
, we deduce that
f^{\prime}
is strict.

By Proposition
A.14.14
, there is a log modification
X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
such that the pullback

(Z^{N}\times_{X\times Y}V)\times_{X}X^{\prime}\rightarrow Z^{N}\times_{X}X^{\prime}

is an isomorphism.
Next, Corollary
9.2.5
gives an isomorphism on sections

\Omega^{j}(X)\simeq\Omega^{j}(X^{\prime}).

Let
Z^{\prime}
be the correspondence from
X^{\prime}
to
Y
defined by
Z^{N}\times_{X}X^{\prime}
.
If we can construct a morphism

Z^{\prime*}:\Omega^{j}(Y)\rightarrow\Omega^{j}(X^{\prime}),

then we obtain
Z^{*}:\Omega^{j}(Y)\rightarrow\Omega^{j}(X)
via the composite

\Omega^{j}(Y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Z^{\prime*}}}{{\rightarrow}}\Omega^{j}(X^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\Omega^{j}(X).

Hence we may replace
(X,Y,Z,V)
by
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},Z,V^{\prime})
where
V^{\prime}:=V\times_{X}X^{\prime}
.
In this case,
the projection

Z^{N}\times_{(X\times Y)}V\rightarrow Z^{N}

is an isomorphism,
and the projection
Z^{N}\times_{(X\times Y)}V\rightarrow V
gives a morphism

\nu:Z^{N}\rightarrow V.

Let
U
be the maximal open subscheme of
V
that is strict over
X
, which exists by Lemma
A.4.4
.
Since
Z^{N}
is strict over
X
,
\nu
factors through
\mu:Z^{N}\rightarrow U
, and there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{U,Y}}

Since
Z^{N}
is quasi-projective,
\mu
admits a factorization

Z^{N}\rightarrow U^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}_{U}^{m}\rightarrow U

into a closed immersion followed by an open immersion and the projection morphism for some
m\geq 0
.
We let
p_{U^{\prime}}:U^{\prime}\rightarrow U
(resp.
q_{U^{\prime}}
) be the composition
U^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}_{U}^{m}\rightarrow U
(resp.
U^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{U^{\prime}}}}{{\rightarrow}}U\rightarrow X\times Y\rightarrow X
).
Now using Constructions
9.4.11
and
9.5.3
, we have the homomorphisms

\begin{split}&H^{i}(Y,\Omega^{j})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{U,Y}^{*}}}{{\rightarrow}}H^{i}(U,\Omega^{j})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{U}^{*}}}{{\rightarrow}}H^{i}(U^{\prime},\Omega^{j})\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cup cl(Z^{N},U^{\prime})}}{{\longrightarrow}}&H_{Z}^{i+m+d_{Y}}(U^{\prime},\Omega^{j+m+d_{Y}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle q_{U*}}}{{\rightarrow}}H^{i}(X,\Omega^{j}).\end{split}

When
i=0
we deduce the desired correspondence action
Z^{*}:\Omega^{j}(Y)\rightarrow\Omega^{j}(X)
.

For a general
X
, we choose a Zariski cover
\{X_{a}\}_{a\in I}
of
X
such that each
X_{a}
is quasi-projective and has an fs chart.
Set
X_{ab}:=X_{a}\times_{X}X_{b}
,
and for each
a
and
b
we choose a Zariski cover
\{X_{abc}\}_{c\in J_{ab}}
of
X_{ab}
such that each
X_{abc}
is quasi-projective and has an fs chart.
Since
\Omega^{j}_{X}
is a Zariski sheaf, there is an exact sequence

0\rightarrow\Omega^{j}(X)\rightarrow\bigoplus_{a\in I}\Omega^{j}(X_{a})\rightarrow\bigoplus_{a,b\in I,c\in J_{ab}}\Omega^{j}(X_{abc}).

This induces an exact sequence

\begin{split}0\rightarrow&\operatorname{Hom}(\Omega^{j}(Y),\Omega^{j}(X))\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(\Omega^{j}(Y),\oplus_{a\in I}\Omega^{j}(X_{a}))\\
\rightarrow&\operatorname{Hom}(\Omega^{j}(Y),\oplus_{a,b\in I,c\in J_{ab}}\Omega^{j}(X_{abc})).\end{split}

Letting
Z_{a}\in\operatorname{lCor}(X_{a},Y)
and
Z_{abc}\in\operatorname{lCor}(X_{abc},Y)
denote the restrictions of
Z\in\operatorname{lCor}(X,Y)
,
we have constructed

Z_{a}^{*}\in\operatorname{Hom}(\Omega^{j}(Y),\Omega^{j}(X_{a})),\;Z_{abc}^{*}\in\operatorname{Hom}(\Omega^{j}(Y),\Omega^{j}(X_{abc})).

By Lemma
9.6.3
below, we obtain the desired element

Z^{*}\in\operatorname{Hom}(\Omega^{j}(Y),\Omega^{j}(X))

(9.6.1)

using the above exact sequence.
Our construction extends to the log setting the correspondence action given by Rülling in
[
51
, §A.4]
.

Lemma 9.6.3
.

Let
Z\in lCor(X,Y)
be a log correspondence where
X,Y\in SmlSm/k
, let
j:U\rightarrow X
be an open immersion, and let
Z_{U}\in lCor(U,Y)
be the restriction of
Z
.
Consider the naturally induced map

j^{*}:\Omega^{j}(X)\rightarrow\Omega^{j}(X).

If
X
is quasi-projective and has an fs chart
P
,
then we have

j^{*}\circ Z^{*}=Z_{U}^{*}.

Démonstration.

This is immediate from the construction.
∎

Theorem 9.6.4
.

The presheaf
\Omega^{j}
on
lSm/k
admits a log transfer structure.

Démonstration.

We will first show that
\Omega^{j}
on
SmlSm/k
admits a log transfer structure.
Let
V\in lCor(X,Y)
and
W\in lCor(Y,Z)
be log correspondences.
From Construction
9.6.2
we have

V^{*}\in\operatorname{Hom}(\Omega^{j}(Y),\Omega^{j}(X)),\;W^{*}\in\operatorname{Hom}(\Omega^{j}(Z),\Omega^{j}(Y)),

and

(W\circ V)^{*}\in\operatorname{Hom}(\Omega^{j}(Z),\Omega^{j}(X)).

It remains to check that

(W\circ V)^{*}=V^{*}\circ W^{*}.

Due to Construction
9.6.2
, we may assume that
X
,
Y
, and
Z
are quasi-projective schemes equipped with fs charts.
Consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{j}(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{j}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{j}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{j}(Z-\partial Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{j}(Y-\partial Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{j}(X-\partial X).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(W^{\circ})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V^{\circ})^{*}}

The correspondences
V^{\circ}
and
W^{\circ}
are as in Lemma
2.3.2
.

The vertical morphisms are injective by definition of the differential forms with log poles as subsheaf of the pushforward of the sheaf of differential forms
from the open subset where the log structure is trivial.
By Lemma
9.6.3
and by the construction
[
51
, §A.4]
,
the pullbacks
(V^{\circ})^{*}
and
(W^{\circ})^{*}
render the two squares commutative.
By
[
51
, Theorem A.4.1]
,
the lower horizontal composition
(W^{\circ}\circ V^{\circ})^{*}
agrees with
(V^{\circ})^{*}\circ(W^{\circ})^{*}
.
Since the vertical maps are injective and
(W\circ V)^{\circ}=W^{\circ}\circ V^{\circ}
by Lemma
2.3.3
,
we conclude that
(W\circ V)^{*}=V^{*}\circ W^{*}
by a diagram chase.
This completes the proof that
\Omega^{j}
admits a log transfer structure on
SmlSm/k
.

Thanks to Lemma
4.7.6
, the presheaf on
lSm/k
given by

X\mapsto\mathop{\rm colim}_{Y\in X_{div}^{Sm}}\Omega^{j}(Y)

admits a log transfer structure.
To conclude, observe that this presheaf is isomorphic to
\Omega^{j}
since
\Omega^{j}
is a dividing sheaf.
∎

Remark 9.6.5
.

Let
X
be a smooth
k
-scheme.
By
[
46
, Theorem 3.6]
the sections of the
h
-sheafification
a_{h}(\Omega^{j})
of
\Omega^{j}
on
X
agree with
\Omega^{j}(X)
.
This implies that the sheaves
\Omega^{j}
on
Sm/k
admit a unique transfer structure.
Thus the transfer structure defined by Rülling and extended in this section to log schemes agrees with the transfer structure defined in
[
61
]
,
at least with rational coefficients and in characteristic zero.

Proposition 9.6.6
.

For every integer
j\geq 0
, the differential

d:\Omega^{j}\rightarrow\Omega^{j+1}

is a morphism of presheaves with log transfers.

Démonstration.

We need to show that for every finite log correspondence
V\in\operatorname{lCor}(X,Y)
, with
X,Y\in lSm/k
, the back square in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{j}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{j+1}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{j}(Y-\partial Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{j+1}(Y-\partial Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{j}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{j+1}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{j}(X-\partial X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{j+1}(X-\partial X).}

commutes.
The top and bottom squares commute since
d:\Omega^{j}\rightarrow\Omega^{j+1}
is a morphism of presheaves.
The left and right squares commute since
\Omega^{j}
is a presheaf with log transfers by Theorem
9.6.4
.
In the proof of Theorem
9.6.4
, we have observed that the homomorphisms

\Omega^{j}(X)\rightarrow\Omega^{j}(X-\partial X)\text{ and }\Omega^{j}(Y)\rightarrow\Omega^{j}(Y-\partial Y)

are injective.
Thus we are reduced to showing that the front square commutes, i.e., we may assume that
X,Y\in Sm/k
.
This case is checked in
[
51
, Theorem B.2.1]
.
∎

Remark 9.6.7
.

In Sections
9.3
-
9.6
we have made a detour through the work of Chatzistamatiou-Rülling
[
17
]
(adapted to the log setting) in order to define an action of the log correspondences on the sheaves of differentials. In fact (as in
[
17
]
), the formalism of Grothendieck duality allows one to construct an action of log correspondences on the cohomology groups of
\Omega^{j}
, as implied by the argument used in Construction
9.6.2
.

The referee and Kay Rülling independently pointed out to us that if one is simply willing to establish an action of correspondences on
\Omega^{j}
(and not on the cohomology), a shortcut is possible.

The aim is to define for every
X,Y\in SmlSm/k
, and every elementary log correspondence
Z
from
X
to
Y
, a dashed arrow which makes the following diagram commutative

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{j}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{j}(Y-\partial Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{j}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{j}(X-\partial X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Z^{\circ})^{*}}

where
(Z^{\circ})^{*}
is the correspondence action on differentials defined in
[
17
]
. Since the horizontal maps are injective (both
X
and
Y
are in
SmlSm/k
), it is then enough to show that for every section
a\in\Omega^{j}(Y)
, the pullback
(Z^{\circ})^{*}(a)\in H^{0}(\underline{X},j_{*}\Omega^{j}_{X-\partial X})
belongs to
H^{0}(\underline{X},\Omega^{j}_{X})
, where
j\colon\underline{X}-\partial X\hookrightarrow\underline{X}
is the open immersion. Since this property can be checked around each generic point of
\partial X
(the differentials are locally free), one can reduce to the case where
X
is local and 1-dimensional. The elementary log correspondence
Z
induces by restriction a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

where
\underline{Z}^{N}
is the normalization of
\underline{Z}
and
p
is a strict, finite and surjective morphism. It is then enough to show that the pushforward
p_{*}^{\circ}\colon\Omega^{j}(Z^{N}-\partial Z^{N})\to\Omega^{j}(X-\partial X)
(induced by the transpose of the graph of
p^{\circ}
) restricts to a morphism between the subsheaves of log forms. This can be checked locally in coordinates.

We have decided to keep the original argument since it gives a bit more, as explained above. Nevertheless, we will come back to this approach in future work, in which we will construct the action of log correspondences on the sheaves of Hodge-Witt differentials. As a byproduct, this implies that logarithmic crystalline cohomology is representable in
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
.

9.7.
Hodge cohomology and cyclic homology

After having established the action of log correspondences on the sheaves of logarithmic differentials,
we are ready to establish the representability of Hodge cohomology and cyclic homology in
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
.

Theorem 9.7.1
.

For
t=dNis
,
d\acute{e}t
, and
l\acute{e}t
, the sheaves
\Omega^{j}
are strictly
\overline{\Box}
-invariant
t
-sheaves with log transfers.
Thus for every
X\in lSm/k
and
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is a natural isomorphism

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)}(M(X),\Omega^{j}[i])\simeq H^{i}_{Zar}(X,\Omega^{j}).

Démonstration.

Lemma
9.1.1
,
Proposition
9.2.6
,
Corollary
9.2.8
,
and Theorem
9.6.4
imply that
\Omega^{j}
is a strictly
\overline{\Box}
-invariant
t
-sheaves with log transfers on
SmlSm/k
.
For the isomorphisms

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)}(M(X),\Omega^{j}[i])

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf logDM}_{d\acute{e}t}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)}(M(X),\Omega^{j}[i])

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf logDM}_{l\acute{e}t}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)}(M(X),\Omega^{j}[i])

\displaystyle\simeq H^{i}_{Zar}(X,\Omega^{j}),

see (
5.4.2
) and (
5.4.3
).
∎

Corollary 9.7.2
.

With the above notations, there is a natural isomorphism

\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)}(M(X),\Omega^{\bullet}[i])\simeq\mathbf{H}^{i}_{Zar}(X,\Omega^{\bullet}).

Hence the de Rham cohomology, which is not
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant in positive characteristic, is representable in
{\bf logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\Lambda)
.

Démonstration.

Suppose
t
is
dNis
or
Zar
.
The Hodge to de Rham spectral sequence

E_{1}^{pq}=H^{q}_{t}(X,\Omega^{p})\Rightarrow\mathbf{H}^{p+q}_{t}(X,\Omega^{\bullet})

is strongly convergent, so Theorem
9.7.1
implies that
\mathbf{H}^{i}_{Zar}(X,\Omega^{\bullet})
is
\overline{\Box}
-invariant for every integer
i\geq 0
.
Furthermore, Proposition
9.2.6
implies
\mathbf{H}^{i}_{dNis}(X,\Omega^{\bullet})\simeq\mathbf{H}^{i}_{Zar}(X,\Omega^{\bullet})
.
Hence we obtain the desired isomorphism.
∎

Example 9.7.3
.

Suppose
k
is a field of positive characteristic
p>0
and let
\Lambda
be an
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-algebra.
Then the Artin-Schreier sequence

0\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}/p\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{a}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{a}\rightarrow 0

is an exact sequence of étale sheaves on
SmlSm/k
, where by definition we set
\Gamma(X,\mathbb{G}_{a})=\Gamma(\underline{X},\mathbb{G}_{a})
.
By Theorem
9.7.1
the sheaf
\mathbb{G}_{a}
on
SmlSm/k
is a strictly
\overline{\Box}
-invariant dividing étale sheaf with log transfers.
Consequently,
\mathbb{Z}/p
is a strictly
\overline{\Box}
-invariant dividing étale sheaf with log transfers.
Applying (
5.4.3
) we see that for every
X\in SmlSm/k
, there is an isomorphism

\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf logDM}_{d\acute{e}t}^{\rm eff}(k,\mathbb{Z})}(M(X),\mathbb{Z}/p)\simeq H^{0}_{Zar}(\underline{X},\mathbb{Z}/p).

In particular, if
X
is nonempty, this is a nontrivial group.
It follows that also
\mathbb{Z}/p
is nontrivial in
{\bf logDM}_{d\acute{e}t}^{\rm eff}(k,\mathbb{Z})
.
Similarly,
\mathbb{Z}/p
is nontrivial in
{\bf logDM}_{l\acute{e}t}^{\rm eff}(k,\mathbb{Z})
.

Assume now that
k
has characteristic zero and let
\Lambda=\mathbb{Q}
.
Let
HC_{n}(X)
denote the cyclic homology groups of a smooth
k
-scheme
X
.
The Zariski descent method of
[
96
]
and the Hochschild-Kostant-Rosenberg Theorem
[
63
, Theorem 3.4.12]
provide a natural isomorphism

HC_{n}(X)\simeq\bigoplus_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbf{H}_{Zar}^{2p-n}(X,\Omega^{\leq 2p}).

(9.7.1)

Proposition
9.6.6
shows that the differentials in the complex
\Omega^{\bullet}
on
SmlSm/k
are compatible with the log transfer structure,
so that
\Omega^{\leq 2p}
is a complex of Nisnevich sheaves with (log) transfers.
Theorem
9.7.1
and (
) imply representability of cyclic homology in
{\mathbf{logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\mathbb{Q})}
.

Theorem 9.7.4
.

Suppose
k
has characteristic zero and let
\Lambda=\mathbb{Q}
.
For every
{X}\in Sm/k
and
n\geq 0
, there is a natural isomorphism

\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathbf{logDM}^{\rm eff}(k,\mathbb{Q})}}(M({X}),\bigoplus_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}\Omega^{\leq 2p}[2p-n])\simeq HC_{n}({X}).

(9.7.2)

Note that for
X\in lSm/k
,
the left hand side of (
) can be taken as definition of cyclic homology for
fs log smooth log schemes over a field of characteristic zero.
In a future work, we plan to compare the above with log Hochschild and log cyclic homology.

Annexe A
Logarithmic geometry

In this section,
we provide a minimal reminder of the basic definitions regarding logarithmic geometry and state results
that are used in the main body of our work.
One can intuitively view the category of log schemes as an extension of the category of schemes given by
essentially allowing “schemes with boundary” meaning pairs
(X,\partial X)
,
where
X
is a scheme, and
\partial X\subset X
is a normal crossing divisor,
In many ways, the log geometry of
(X,\partial X)
models the geometry on the complement
X\setminus\partial X
:
for example,
they have the same de Rham cohomology in characteristic 0 and étale sites.
Many classical invariants and properties of schemes extend naturally to log schemes.
There are notions of smooth and étale maps, pullbacks, forms and differentials, and coherent sheaves.
The Kato-Nakayama or Betti analytification functor provides an important bridge between log schemes and topological spaces ;
in our motivic setting,
this is clearly expressed in the construction of Thom spaces.

The structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{X}
of
X
is a sheaf of rings in the Zariski topology.
Its subsheaf of invertible elements is a Zariski sheaf of commutative monoids under multiplication
\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\times}
.
Moreover,
it is related to the sheaf of differentials via the logarithmic differential

\text{dlog}\colon\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\times}\to\Omega_{X}

(A.0.1)

defined locally by

f\mapsto\frac{df}{f}.

A foundational insight of log geometry is that for a scheme with boundary,
(
) extends to a morphism

\text{dlog}\colon\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\times}(\partial X)\to\Omega_{X}(\partial X)

(A.0.2)

with values in the sheaf of logarithmic differentials with respect to the normal crossing divisor
\partial X
.
Here,

\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\times}(\partial X)
denotes the monoid of functions which are invertible outside of
\partial X
(and may have arbitrary order of vanishing at
\partial X
).
Much of the geometry of the pair
(X,\partial X)
is captured by the enhanced logarithmic differential map in (
).
In log geometry, one generalizes the above to the “logarithmic boundary data” of a scheme together with a submonoid sheaf.

A textbook reference for logarithmic geometry is
[
76
]
.
Some of the first accounts of the subjects include
[
55
]
, and
[
47
]
.

A.1.
Monoids

Definition A.1.1
.

A monoid is a commutative semigroup equipped with a unit.
A homomorphism of monoids is required to preserve the unit element.
Let
\mathbf{Mon}
denote the category of monoids.

Given a monoid
P
, we can associate the group completion

P^{\mathrm{gp}}:=\{(a,b)|(a,b)\sim(c,d)\ \mbox{if}\ \exists\ p\in P\ \mbox{such that}\ p+a+d=p+b+c\}.

Note that the canonical morphism
P\to P^{\mathrm{gp}}
is universal among the homomorphisms of monoids
P\to G
such that the target
G
is an abelian group.

Let
P^{\ast}
denote the set of all
p\in P
such that
p+q=0
for some
q\in P
. It is called the
unit group
of
P
.

Definition A.1.2
.

For a monoid
P
the image of the homomorphism
P\rightarrow P^{\mathrm{gp}}
sending
a
to
(a,0)
is denoted by
P^{\rm int}
.
A monoid
P
is said to be
integral
if
P\rightarrow P^{\rm int}
is an isomorphism and finitely generated if there exists a surjective homomorphism
\mathbb{N}^{k}\to P
for some
k
.
It is called
fine
if it is integral and finitely generated.
For an integral monoid
P
we denote by
P^{\rm sat}
the submonoid of
P^{\mathrm{gp}}
such that
whenever
p\in P^{\mathrm{gp}}
and
n
is a positive integer such that
np\in P
then
p\in P^{\rm sat}
.
An integral monoid
P
is said to be
saturated
if
P\rightarrow P^{\rm sat}
is an isomorphism.
A monoid
P
is
sharp
if its unit group
P^{*}
is trivial.

We abbreviate the combined condition “fine and saturated” to “fs.”

Definition A.1.3
.

For homomorphisms of monoids (resp. integral monoids, resp. saturated monoids)
P\rightarrow Q
and
P\rightarrow P^{\prime}
, the amalgamated sum
Q\oplus_{P}^{\rm mon}P^{\prime}
(resp.
Q\oplus_{P}^{\rm int}P^{\prime}
, resp.
Q\oplus_{P}P^{\prime}
) in the category of monoids (resp. integral monoids, resp. saturated monoids) is the colimit of the diagram
Q\leftarrow P\rightarrow P^{\prime}
.

In general, the monoids
Q\oplus_{P}^{\rm mon}P^{\prime}
,
Q\oplus_{P}^{\rm int}P^{\prime}
, and
Q\oplus_{P}P^{\prime}
are not isomorphic to each other even if
P
,
P^{\prime}
, and
Q
are saturated.
The monoid
Q\oplus_{P}^{\rm int}P^{\prime}
is isomorphic to the submonoid of the abelian group
Q^{\mathrm{gp}}\oplus_{P^{\mathrm{gp}}}P^{\prime{\mathrm{gp}}}
generated by the images of
Q
and
P^{\prime}
.
There is an isomorphism

Q\oplus_{P}P^{\prime}\simeq(Q\oplus_{P}^{\rm int}P^{\prime})^{\rm sat}.

For an explicit description of
Q\oplus_{P}^{\rm mon}P^{\prime}
we refer to
[
76
, Proposition I.1.1.5]
.

Definition A.1.4
.

For a monoid
P
, we set

\overline{P}:=P\oplus_{P^{\ast}}^{\rm mon}0.

Note that
\overline{P}
is always sharp.

Definition A.1.5
.

A homomorphism of integral monoids
\theta:P\rightarrow Q
is called Kummer if it is injective and
\theta\otimes\mathbb{Q}:P\otimes\mathbb{Q}\rightarrow Q\otimes\mathbb{Q}
is surjective.

Definition A.1.6
.

A
face

F
of a monoid
P
is a submonoid of
P
such that for every
p,q\in P

p+q\in F\Rightarrow p,q\in F.

An
ideal

I
of a monoid
P
is a subset of
P
satisfying the condition

p\in I,q\in P\Rightarrow p+q\in I.

For an element
p
of
P
, let
\langle p\rangle
denote the smallest face containing
p
, and let
(p)
denote the smallest ideal containing
p
.

Definition A.1.7
.

For a face
F
of a monoid
P
we set

P_{F}:=\{(a,b)\in P\times F|(a,b)\sim(c,d)\ \mbox{if}\ \exists\ p\in F\ \mbox{such that}\ p+a+d=p+b+c\}.

With this definition we have
P_{P}=P^{\mathrm{gp}}
.
We also set

P/F:=P\oplus_{F}^{\rm mon}0.

There is an isomorphism
\overline{P_{F}}\simeq P/F
.
Finally, for an element
f\in P
, we set

P_{f}:=P_{\langle f\rangle}.

A.2.
Logarithmic structures

For a scheme
X
, let
\mathcal{O}_{X}
be the structure sheaf. When we refer to it as a sheaf of monoids, we always consider its multiplicative structure.

Definition A.2.1
.

Let
X
be a scheme.
A pre-logarithmic structure on
X
is a sheaf of monoids
\mathcal{M}_{X}
on the small Zariski site of
X
combined with a morphism of sheaves of monoids

\alpha\colon\mathcal{M}_{X}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}

called the structure morphism.
A pre-logarithmic structure is called a logarithmic structure if
\alpha
induces an isomorphism

\alpha^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{X}^{*})\simeq\mathcal{O}^{*}_{X}.

The pair
(X,\mathcal{M}_{X})
is called a log scheme.
For notational convenience, whenever we abbreviate
(X,\mathcal{M}_{X})
to
Y
we set
\underline{Y}:=X
and
\mathcal{M}_{Y}:=\mathcal{M}_{X}
.
The scheme
\underline{Y}
is called the underlying scheme of
Y
.

Note that the last condition in Definition
A.2.1
gives us a canonical embedding
\mathcal{O}_{X}^{*}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{X}
as a subsheaf of groups.
The quotient sheaf of monoids

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{X}\colon=\mathcal{M}_{X}/\mathcal{O}_{X}^{*}

is called the characteristic monoid of the log structure
\mathcal{M}_{X}
.

Remark A.2.2
.

The morphism
\alpha
is sometimes denoted
\exp
for “exponential” and an inverse
\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\ast}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{X}
is denoted
\log
for “logarithmic.”
Any scheme
X
is a log scheme with
\mathcal{M}_{X}=\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\ast}
and
\alpha
the inclusion.
This is the trivial log structure on
X
.

Definition A.2.3

(
[
76
, Definition III.1.1.1]
)
.

Let
{\bf Log}_{X}
denote the category of log structures on a scheme
X
.

Definition A.2.4

(
[
76
, Definition III.1.1.5]
)
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a morphism of schemes.
For a log structure
\mathcal{M}_{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}
on
X
we set

f_{*}^{log}\mathcal{M}_{X}:=f_{*}\mathcal{M}_{X}\times_{f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{O}_{Y}.

For a log structure
\mathcal{M}_{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{Y}
on
Y
,
let
f_{log}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{Y}
be the log structure induced by the prelog structure

f^{-1}(\mathcal{M}_{Y})\rightarrow f^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{Y})\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}.

With these definitions, there exists an adjoint pair of functors

f_{log}^{*}:{\bf Log}_{X}\rightleftarrows{\bf Log}_{Y}:f_{*}^{log}.

Definition A.2.5
.

A morphism of log schemes
(X,\mathcal{M}_{X})\to(Y,\mathcal{M}_{Y})
is a pair
(f,\varphi)
,
where
f\colon X\to Y
is a morphism of schemes and
\varphi\colon f^{-1}\mathcal{M}_{Y}\to\mathcal{M}_{X}
is a homomorphism of sheaves of monoids such that the diagram

\textstyle{f^{-1}\mathcal{M}_{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{\mathcal{M}_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{f^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{X}}

(A.2.1)

commutes.
Note that giving a morphism
\varphi:f^{-1}\mathcal{M}_{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{X}
is equivalent to giving a morphism
f_{log}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{X}
.

We write
\mathbf{LogSch}
for the category of log schemes.

Remark A.2.6
.

A log morphism between schemes equipped with their trivial log structures is just a morphism of schemes.

Example A.2.7
.

Let
X
be a smooth scheme with an effective divisor
D\subset X
.
Then we have a standard logarithmic structure on
X
associated with the pair
(X,D)
or the divisorial log structure induced by
D
,
where

\mathcal{M}_{X}:=\{f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}\ |\ f|_{X\setminus D}\in\mathcal{O}^{*}_{X}\}

with the structure morphism
\mathcal{M}_{X}\to\mathcal{O}_{X}
given by the canonical inclusion.
Note that any section of
\mathcal{O}^{*}_{X}
is already in
\mathcal{M}_{X}
.
In this case, the log structure encodes the intuition that
D
is a sort of “boundary” in
X
.
With reference to Section
A.5
,
picking local equations for the branches of
D
passing through a point
x\in X
gives local charts for the log structure with monoid
\mathbb{N}^{n}
,
where
n
is the number of branches.

Example A.2.8
.

To a monoid
P
one associates the monoid ring
\mathbb{Z}[P]
of
P
over the integers and the affine toric variety
\underline{\mathbb{A}_{P}}:=\text{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[P])
.
The standard logarithmic structure
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{A}_{P}}
on
\underline{\mathbb{A}_{P}}
is induced from the pre-logarithmic structure

P\to\mathbb{Z}[P]=\Gamma(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}_{P}})

defined by the obvious inclusion.
This is called the canonical log structure on
\underline{\mathbb{A}_{P}}
, and we set
\mathbb{A}_{P}:=(\underline{\mathbb{A}_{P}},\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{A}_{P}})
.
For example,
we can consider
\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{N}}
as
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}])
with the log structure associated to the origin.
More generally,
we may replace
\mathbb{Z}
with a ring
R
.
In this way obtain a contravariant functor from
\mathbf{Mon}
to
\mathbf{LogSch}
.

Of particular interest is the case when
P
is a toric monoid.

Example A.2.9
.

Any open subscheme of a log scheme admits a restricted log structure.

Example A.2.10
.

Let
Q
be a sharp monoid.
Define the homomorphism
\alpha\colon Q\to k
by
\alpha(0)=1
and
\alpha(q)=0
if
q\neq 0
.
The associated log scheme
(\operatorname{Spec}(k),Q)
is a called a log point in general and a standard log point when
Q=\mathbb{N}
.
This log structure is equivalent to that of
\alpha\colon Q\oplus k^{\ast}\to k
given by
\alpha(0,x)=x
and
\alpha(q,x)=0
if
q\neq 0
.
When
Q=\{0\}
we obtain the trivial log structure.
If
k
is algebraically closed, every log structure on
\operatorname{Spec}(k)
is equivalent to a log point.

A log structure isolates the “singular” information carried by a pre-log structure,
i.e.,
it provides logarithmic behavior for functions that are not already invertible.
Any pre-log structure can be turned into a log structure.
The following provides a left adjoint of the inclusion functor from pre-log structures into log structures.

Definition A.2.11
.

Let
\alpha\colon\mathcal{M}_{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}
be a pre-logarithmic structure.
Then the associated logarithmic structure
M^{a}_{X}
is the pushout of the diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{X}^{*}}
\textstyle{\alpha^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{X}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{M}_{X}}

in the category of sheaves of monoids on the small Zariski site of
X
, endowed with

M^{a}_{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}\qquad(a,b)\mapsto\alpha(a)b\qquad\qquad(a\in M,b\in\mathcal{O}_{X}^{*}).

Example A.2.12
.

Any morphism of schemes
f\colon X\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[P])
induces a log structure on
X
as follows :

f
gives a morphism of monoids
P\to\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
that induces
\widetilde{\alpha}\colon\underline{P}\to\mathcal{O}_{X}
,
where
\underline{P}
is the constant sheaf.
It is typically not true that
\widetilde{\alpha}
induces an isomorphism between
\widetilde{\alpha}^{-1}\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{X}
and
\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{X}
,
but the procedure above fixes the behaviour of the units, and this produces a log structure
\alpha\colon M^{a}_{X}\to\mathcal{O}_{X}
.
The quotient
M^{a}_{X}/\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\times}
is obtained from
\underline{P}
by locally killing the sections of
\underline{P}
that become invertible in
\mathcal{O}_{X}
.
In particular,
all the stalks of
M^{a}_{X}/\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\times}
are quotients of the monoid
P
.

A.3.
Monoschemes and fans

In this section, we review the definition of fans and the relation between monoschemes and fans.

Definition A.3.1
.

A
monoidal space
is a topological space
X
equipped with a sheaf of monoids
\mathcal{M}_{X}
.

A monoidal space
X
is
quasi-compact
(resp.
quasi-separated
, resp.
connected
) if the underlying topological space is quasi-compact
(resp. quasi-separated, resp. connected).

Definition A.3.2
.

Let
P
be a monoid.
Its monoidal space
\operatorname{Spec}(P)
is defined as follows.
The underlying set of
\operatorname{Spec}(P)
consists of the faces of
P
.
For an element
f\in P
, we set

D(f):=\{F\in\operatorname{Spec}(P):f\in F\}.

The
Zariski
topology on
\operatorname{Spec}(P)
is the topology generated by

\mathscr{B}:=\{D(f):f\in P\},

i.e.,
\mathscr{B}
is a base for
\operatorname{Spec}(P)
.
The assignment

D(f)\mapsto P_{f}

defines a presheaf on
\mathscr{B}
, and its sheafification defines a sheaf
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{Spec}(P)}
on
\operatorname{Spec}(P)
.
The pair

\operatorname{Spec}(P):=(\operatorname{Spec}(P),\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{Spec}(P)})

is a monoidal space.

Remark A.3.3
.

The meaning of the notation
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
,
either as the affine scheme associated with the ring
\mathbb{Z}
or as the monoidal space associated with the monoid
\mathbb{Z}
,
should always be clear from the context.

Definition A.3.4
.

A monoidal space
X
is an fs
monoscheme
if there is an open cover
\{X_{i}\}
of
X
such that for some fs monoid
P_{i}
there is an isomorphism

X_{i}\simeq\operatorname{Spec}(P_{i}).

If
\mathcal{M}_{X,x}^{\rm gp}
is torsion free for every point
x\in X
, we say that
X
is
toric
.
If
X\simeq\operatorname{Spec}(P)
for some fs monoid
P
, we say that
X
is
affine
.

The category of fs monoschemes has fiber products, see
[
76
, Proposition II.1.3.5]
.

Definition A.3.5

(
[
76
, Definition II.1.6.1]
)
.

A quasi-compact and quasi-separated fs monoscheme
X
is
separated
(resp.
proper
) if the naturally induced homomorphism

\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{N}),X)\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}),X)

is injective (resp. bijective).

Definition A.3.6
.

We follow standard notation for toric varieties and set

N:=\mathbb{Z}^{d},\;M:=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(N,\mathbb{Z}),\;N_{\mathbb{R}}:=N\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R},\;M_{\mathbb{R}}:=M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}.

We also often use the notation
N^{\vee}:=M
.
If
\sigma\subset N_{\mathbb{R}}
is a strictly convex rational polyhedral cone,
we denote its dual cone by

\sigma^{\vee}:=\{m\in M_{\mathbb{R}}|\langle m,n\rangle=0,\ \forall\ n\in\sigma\}.

Gordan’s lemma tells us that
M\cap\sigma^{\vee}
is a finitely generated monoid.

A
fan

\Sigma
in
N
is a finite set of cones in
N
satisfying the following conditions :

(i)

If
\sigma\in\Sigma
, then every face of
\sigma
is in
\Sigma
.

(ii)

If
\sigma,\tau\in\Sigma
, then
\sigma\cap\tau\in\Sigma
.

Let
\underline{\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma}}
be the toric variety associated to a fan
\Sigma
in
M
.
By gluing the log structures associated with the homomorphisms

M\cap\sigma^{\vee}\to\mathbb{Z}[M\cap\sigma^{\vee}]

for every cone
\sigma
in
\Sigma
,
we obtain an fs log scheme
\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma}
over
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
.
This is in fact the divisorial log structure on
\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma}
coming form the toric boundary
\partial\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma}\subseteq\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma}
,
i.e.,
the complement of the torus orbit in
X
.

Definition A.3.7
.

For a fan
\Sigma
in a lattice
N
, the
support

\lvert\Sigma\rvert
of
\Sigma
is the union of all cones of
\Sigma
.

Definition A.3.8
.

For fans
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
in lattices
N
and
N^{\prime}
,
a morphism
\Sigma^{\prime}\rightarrow\Sigma
of fans is a homomorphism of lattices
f:N^{\prime}\rightarrow N
such that for every cone
\sigma^{\prime}
of
N^{\prime}
,

f(\sigma^{\prime})\subset\sigma
for some cone
\sigma
of
N
.
A morphism
\Sigma^{\prime}\rightarrow\Sigma
of fans is a
partial subdivision
if the associated homomorphism
f:N^{\prime}\rightarrow N
of lattices is an isomorphism.
A partial subdivision
\Sigma^{\prime}\rightarrow\Sigma
is a
subdivision
if
\lvert\Sigma^{\prime}\rvert=\lvert\Sigma\rvert
.
If
\Sigma^{\prime}\rightarrow\Sigma
is a morphism of fans, then we can naturally associate a morphism
\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{\Sigma}
of fs log schemes.

Let
\mathbf{Fan}
denote the category of fans, and let
\mathbf{MSch}^{fs}
denote the category of fs monoschemes.

Theorem A.3.9
.

There is a fully faithful functor

\mathbf{Fan}\rightarrow\mathbf{MSch}^{fs}.

(A.3.1)

The essential image consists of all separated, connected, and toric fs monoschemes whose underlying topological spaces are finite sets.

Démonstration.

We refer to
[
76
, Theorem II.1.9.3]
.
∎

Construction A.3.10
.

Here we give an explicit description of the functor (
A.3.1
).
Let
\Sigma
be a fan with the cones
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{r}
in a lattice
N
.
Consider the dual cones
\sigma_{1}^{\vee},\ldots,\sigma_{r}^{\vee}
.
We can glue
\operatorname{Spec}(\sigma_{1}^{\vee}),\ldots,\operatorname{Spec}(\sigma_{r}^{\vee})
altogether to canonically obtain an fs monoscheme.
Thus we can regard fans as fs monoschemes and morphisms of fans as morphisms of monoschemes.

If
P
is an fs monoid such that
P^{\mathrm{gp}}
is a lattice
N
, then
\operatorname{Spec}(P)
corresponds to the fan with a single maximal cone
P^{\vee}
in a lattice
N^{\vee}
.

Lemma A.3.11
.

Let
f\colon\Theta\rightarrow\Sigma
be a subdivision (resp. partial subdivision) of fans in a lattice
N
,
and let
g\colon\Sigma^{\prime}\rightarrow\Sigma
be a morphism of fans with a lattice homomorphism
\theta\colon N^{\prime}\rightarrow N
.
Then the fiber product
\Theta^{\prime}:=\Theta\times_{\Sigma}\Sigma^{\prime}
in the category of fs monoschemes is a fan in
N^{\prime}
,
and the projection
f^{\prime}\colon\Theta^{\prime}\rightarrow\Sigma^{\prime}
is a subdivision (resp. partial subdivision).

Démonstration.

Let
\sigma=\operatorname{Spec}(P)
be a cone of
\Sigma
,
where
P
is an fs monoid,
and let
\tau=\operatorname{Spec}(Q)
and
\sigma^{\prime}=\operatorname{Spec}(P^{\prime})
be cones of
\Theta
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
where
P^{\prime}
and
Q
are fs monoids.
Suppose that
f(\tau)=\sigma
and
g(\sigma^{\prime})=\sigma
.
Then
Q^{\prime}:=Q\oplus_{P}P^{\prime}
is the submonoid of
N^{\prime\vee}
generated by
\theta^{\vee}(Q)
and
P^{\prime}
,
i.e.,

Q^{\prime}=\theta^{\vee}(Q)+P^{\prime}
.
Thus we have
Q^{\prime\vee}=(\theta^{\vee}(Q))^{\vee}\cap P^{\prime\vee}
.
From this local description of
\Theta^{\prime}
, we see that
\Theta^{\prime}
is the fan in
N^{\prime}
whose cones are of the form

\{\theta^{\vee}(\tau)\cap\sigma^{\prime}:\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\prime}\text{ and }\tau\in\Theta\}.

(A.3.2)

Since
\Sigma^{\prime}
and
\Theta^{\prime}
are in the same lattice
N^{\prime}
,
f^{\prime}\colon\Theta^{\prime}\rightarrow\Sigma^{\prime}
is a partial subdivision.
If
f
is a subdivision, then
\lvert\Theta\rvert=\lvert\Sigma\rvert
.
Using (
A.3.2
) we deduce that
\lvert\Theta^{\prime}\rvert=\lvert\Sigma^{\prime}\rvert
.
∎

Lemma A.3.12
.

Let
f:\Sigma^{\prime}\rightarrow\Sigma
be a subdivision of fans in a lattice
N
.
Then
f
is a monomorphism in the category of fans.

Démonstration.

Let
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
are the generic points of the monoschemes
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
.
There are isomorphisms

N\simeq\mathcal{M}_{\Sigma,\xi}\simeq\mathcal{M}_{\Sigma^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}}.

Here
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
can be viewed as separated fs monoschemes,
and we may apply
[
76
, Corollary II.1.6.4]
to conclude that
f
is a monomorphism in the category of fs monoschemes.
In particular,
f
is a monomorphism in the category of fans.
∎

Proposition A.3.13
.

Let
\Sigma
be a fan in a lattice
N
.
Then there is a canonical bijection

\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{N}),\Sigma)\simeq\lvert\Sigma\rvert.

Démonstration.

A homomorphism
f:\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow N
gives a morphism
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{N})\rightarrow\Sigma
precisely when
f(\mathbb{N})
lies in a cone of
\Sigma
,
i.e.,
when
f(\mathbb{N})
lies in
\lvert\Sigma\rvert
.
This implies the bijection.
∎

Definition A.3.14

(
[
25
, Definition 3.3.17]
)
.

Suppose
\Sigma
is a fan in
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
,
and let
\tau
be a cone of
\Sigma
such that for any cone
\sigma
containing
\tau
,
\sigma
is smooth.
Express
\tau
as an
r
-dimensional cone
{\rm Cone}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{r})
.
Then for each maximal cone
\sigma={\rm Cone}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{r},a_{r+1},\ldots,a_{n})
of
\Sigma
containing
\tau
,
replace
\sigma
by the fan with maximal cones

\begin{split}&{\rm Cone}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{r-1},a_{r+1},\ldots,a_{n},a_{1}+\cdots+a_{r}),\\
&{\rm Cone}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{r-2},a_{r},\ldots,a_{n},a_{1}+\cdots+a_{r}),\cdots,{\rm Cone}(a_{2},\ldots,a_{n},a_{1}+\cdots+a_{r}).\end{split}

The resulting new fan
\Sigma^{*}(\tau)
is called the
star subdivision
of
\Sigma
relative to
\tau
.

Lemma A.3.15
.

Let
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
be two smooth fans with
|\Sigma|=|\Sigma^{\prime}|
.
Then there is a subdivision
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
of
\Sigma
obtained by a finite succession of star subdivisions relative to two-dimensional cones such that
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
is a subdivision of
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

Démonstration.

See
[
27
]
and
[
75
, pp. 39-40]
.
∎

Lemma A.3.16
.

Let
\Sigma
be a fan in a lattice
N
.
Then there exists a fan
\Sigma^{\prime}
in
N
containing
\Sigma
such that
\lvert\Sigma^{\prime}\rvert=N
.

Démonstration.

See
[
75
, p. 18]
.
∎

A.4.
Strict morphisms

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a morphism of schemes.
Given a logarithmic structure
\mathcal{M}_{Y}
on
Y
,
we have defined a logarithmic structure

f_{log}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{Y}
on
X
, called the inverse image of
\mathcal{M}_{Y}
or the pullback log structure on
X
.
Using the inverse image of logarithmic structures, we arrive at the following definition incorporated in our notion of finite log correspondences.

Definition A.4.1
.

A morphism of log schemes
(X,\mathcal{M}_{X})\to(Y,\mathcal{M}_{Y})
is called strict if the induced homomorphism
f_{log}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{Y}\to\mathcal{M}_{X}
is an isomorphism,
and it is said to be a strict closed immersion if it is strict and
X\to Y
is a closed immersion.

Example A.4.2
.

In Example
A.2.8
the log structure on
\operatorname{Spec}(R[P])
can be viewed as the pullback log structure on
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[P])
along the morphism induced by
\mathbb{Z}\to R
.

Example A.4.3
.

Let
f\colon X\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
be a smooth morphism.
Then
f
is a strict morphism of log schemes with respect to the divisorial log structures given by
0\in\mathbb{A}^{1}
and
f^{-1}(0)\subseteq X
.

Lemma A.4.4
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a morphism of fs log schemes.
Then there exists a maximal open subscheme
U
of
X
such that the restriction
f_{|U}
of
f
to
U
is strict over
Y
.

Démonstration.

In
[
53
, Lemma 1.5]
it is shown that if
X
is strict at a point
x
, then
f
restricts to a strict morphism over an étale neighborhood.
The same argument applies to fs log schemes equipped with Zariski log structures,
∎

A.5.
Charts of logarithmic structures

Definition A.5.1
.

Let
X
be a log scheme and
P
be a monoid.
A
chart
for
\mathcal{M}_{X}
is a morphism
P\rightarrow\Gamma(X,\mathcal{M}_{X})

such that the induced map of logarithmic structures
P^{a}\to\mathcal{M}_{X}
is an isomorphism.
Here,

P^{a}
is the logarithmic structure associated to the pre-logarithmic structure given by

P\rightarrow\Gamma(X,\mathcal{M}_{X})\rightarrow\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{X}).

If
P
is an fs monoid, the chart is said to be an fs chart.

A chart for
\mathcal{M}_{X}
is equivalent to a strict morphism

f\colon X\to\mathbb{A}_{P}.

In fact, by
[
76
, III.1.2.9]
,
there is a bijection

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{LogSch}}(X,\mathbb{A}_{P})\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{Mon}}(P,\Gamma(X,\mathcal{M}_{X}))

associating to
f
the composition

\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma(X,P_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma(X,\mathcal{M}_{X}).}

The morphism
f\colon X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{P}
also gives a morphism of monoidal spaces

X=(X,\mathcal{M}_{X})\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}(P).

Definition A.5.2
.

A chart for a morphism of log schemes
(X,\mathcal{M}_{X})\to(Y,\mathcal{M}_{Y})
is a triple
(P\to\mathcal{M}_{X},Q\to\mathcal{M}_{Y},Q\to P)
,
where
P\to\mathcal{M}_{X}
and
Q\to\mathcal{M}_{Y}
are charts,
and
Q\to P
is is a homomorphism such that the diagram

\textstyle{Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{f_{log}^{\ast}\mathcal{M}_{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{M}_{X}}

(A.5.1)

commutes.

Definition A.5.3
.

A log scheme
X
is said to be
coherent

if étale locally there is a chart
P\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{X}
with
P
a monoid.
If moreover
P
can be chosen to be integral (resp. fine, resp. saturated, resp. fs), then
X
is called an
integral
(resp.
fine
, resp.
saturated
, resp.
fs
) log scheme.
If such a chart exists Zariski locally, we say that
X
has a
Zariski log structure
.
Finally, if
P
can be chosen isomorphic to
\mathbb{N}^{k}
, the logarithmic structure is called locally free.

We are particularly interested in Zariski log structures, which locally arise from pullbacks of monoid algebras.
The property of being fine and saturated is a logarithmic analog of the property of being normal and of finite type
[
76
, Chapter IV.1.2]
.

Definition A.5.4
.

Suppose that
Y\rightarrow X
and
X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
are morphisms of coherent (resp. integral, resp. saturated) log schemes.
The fiber product in the category of coherent (resp. integral, resp. saturated) log schemes is denoted by

Y\times_{X}^{\rm coh}X^{\prime}\text{ (resp.\ }Y\times_{X}^{\rm int}X^{\prime},\text{ resp.\ }Y\times_{X}X^{\prime}\text{)}.

Example A.5.5
.

Suppose that
Q\rightarrow P
and
P^{\prime}\rightarrow P
are homomorphisms of monoids (resp. integral monoids, resp. saturated monoids).
Then there exists an isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{Q}\times_{\mathbb{A}_{P}}^{\rm coh}\mathbb{A}_{P^{\prime}}\simeq\mathbb{A}_{Q\oplus_{P}^{\rm mon}P^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\text{ (resp.\ }\mathbb{A}_{Q}\times_{\mathbb{A}_{P}}^{\rm int}\mathbb{A}_{P^{\prime}}\simeq\mathbb{A}_{Q\oplus_{P}^{\rm int}P^{\prime}},\text{ resp.\ }\mathbb{A}_{Q}\times_{\mathbb{A}_{P}}\mathbb{A}_{P^{\prime}}\simeq\mathbb{A}_{Q\oplus_{P}P^{\prime}}\text{)}.

Lemma A.5.6
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow S
be a strict morphism of fs log schemes.
Then for any morphism
S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
of fs log schemes, the induced morphism

\underline{X\times_{S}S^{\prime}}\rightarrow\underline{X}\times_{\underline{S}}\underline{S^{\prime}}

is an isomorphism.

Démonstration.

From the construction of fiber products in the category of fs log schemes,
see
[
76
, Corollary III.2.1.6]
,
it suffices to show that the fiber product
X\times_{S}^{\rm coh}S^{\prime}
in the category of coherent log schemes is an fs log scheme.
This question is Zariski local on
S^{\prime}
,
so we may assume that
S^{\prime}
has an fs chart
S^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{P}
.
Since
f
is strict,
the projection
X\times_{S}^{\rm coh}S^{\prime}\rightarrow X
is also strict.
Then
X\times_{S}^{\rm coh}S^{\prime}
has an fs chart.
Thus
X\times_{S}^{\rm coh}S^{\prime}
is an fs log scheme.
∎

Definition A.5.7

(
[
76
, Definition II.2.3.1]
)
.

Suppose
X
is a coherent log scheme with a chart
\beta:P\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{X}
, and let
x\in X
be a point.
Then
\beta
is called exact at
x
if

\overline{\beta_{x}}:\overline{P}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{X,x}

is an isomorphism,
and
\beta
is called neat at
x
if it is exact at
x
and
P
is sharp.

Remark A.5.8
.

(1) Let
X
be a fine log scheme with a fine chart
\beta:P\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{X}
, and let
x\in X
be a point.
Then, Zariski locally on
X
,
\beta
factors through an exact chart at
x
by
[
76
, Remark II.2.3.2]
.

(2) Let
X
is an fs log scheme, and let
x\in X
be a point. Then Zariski locally on
X
there exists a neat chart at
x
by
[
76
, Proposition II.2.3.7]
.

Lemma A.5.9
.

Suppose
X
is an fs log scheme log smooth over a scheme
S
.
Then Zariski locally on
X
, there is a neat fs chart
P
such that the induced morphism
X\rightarrow S\times\mathbb{A}_{P}
is strictly smooth.
If
\underline{X}
is smooth over
S
, then
P\simeq\mathbb{N}^{r}
for some integer
r\in\mathbb{N}
.

Démonstration.

Since the question is Zariski local on
X
, we may assume that
X
admits a neat chart
P
at a point
x\in X
,
see Remark
A.5.8
(2).
In particular, since
P
is sharp,

P^{\rm gp}
is torsion-free by
[
76
, Proposition I.1.3.5(2)]
.
Owing to
[
76
, Proposition II.2.3.7]
there is an isomorphism

\mathcal{M}_{X,x}^{\rm gp}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{X,x}^{*}\oplus P.

This implies that the order of the torsion subgroup of
\mathcal{M}_{X,x}^{\rm gp}
is invertible in
k
.
Hence we can apply
[
76
, Theorem IV.3.3.1(3)]
to conclude that
X\rightarrow S\times\mathbb{A}_{P}
is strictly smooth.

If
\underline{X}
is smooth over
S
, then
S\times\underline{\mathbb{A}_{P}}
is smooth over
S
by
[
30
, Proposition IV.17.7.7]
.
This shows that
P\simeq\mathbb{N}^{r}
for some
r
by
[
25
, Theorem 1.3.12]
because
P
is sharp.
∎

We refer to Definition
7.2.1
for the notion of a strict normal crossing divisor on a scheme.

Lemma A.5.10
.

Suppose that
X\in SmlSm/S
.
Then there exists a strict normal crossing divisor
Z
on
\underline{X}
and an isomorphism

X\simeq(\underline{X},Z).

Démonstration.

The question is Zariski local on
\underline{X}
.
Owing to Lemma
A.5.9
there is an fs chart
P\simeq\mathbb{N}^{r}
of
X
such that the induced morphism
f:X\rightarrow S\times\mathbb{A}_{P}
is strict smooth.
That is,

\mathbb{A}_{P}\simeq(\underline{\mathbb{A}_{P}},W)
,
where
W
is the strict normal crossing divisor induced by the log structure on
\mathbb{A}_{P}
.
Using the strict smooth morphism
f:X\rightarrow S\times\mathbb{A}_{P}
,
we see the claimed isomorphism follows for the strict normal crossing divisor
Z:=f^{*}W
.
∎

Definition A.5.11
.

A morphism of fs log schemes
f:X\rightarrow Y
is a
closed immersion
if the following two properties are satisfied :

(i)

The underlying morphism of schemes
\underline{f}:\underline{Z}\rightarrow\underline{X}
is a closed immersion.

(ii)

The homomorphism
f^{\flat}:f_{log}^{*}(\mathcal{M}_{Y})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{X}
is surjective.

A.6.
Logarithmic smoothness

Log smoothness is one of the central concepts in log geometry.
Kato
[
55
]
showed that every log smooth morphism factors étale locally as a usual smooth morphism composed with a morphism induced by a homomorphism of monoids,
which essentially determines the log structure.

Consider the following commutative diagram of log schemes illustrated with solid arrows :

\textstyle{T_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{T_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{Y}

(A.6.1)

Here
i
is a strictly closed immersion defined by a square zero ideal (note that
T_{0}
and
T_{1}
have the same underlying topological spaces).
An infinitesimal lifting property defines logarithmic smoothness.

Definition A.6.1
.

A morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
of fine log schemes is logarithmically smooth (resp. logarithmically étale) if the underlying morphism
\underline{X}\rightarrow\underline{Y}

is locally of finite presentation and for any commutative diagram (
),
étale locally on
T_{1}
there exists a (resp. there exists a unique) morphism
g:T_{1}\rightarrow X
such that
\varphi=g\circ i
and
\psi=f\circ g
.

We have the following helpful criterion for smoothness from
[
55
, Theorem 3.5]
.

Theorem A.6.2

(K. Kato)
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a morphism of fine log schemes.
Assume there is a chart
Q\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{Y}
, where
Q
is a finitely generated integral monoid.
Then the following are equivalent :

(1)

f
is logarithmically smooth (resp. logarithmically étale).

(2)

Étale locally on
X
,
there is a chart
(P\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{X},Q\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{Y},Q\rightarrow P)
extending
Q\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{Y}
and satisfying the following properties.

(a)

The kernel and the torsion part of the cokernel (resp. the kernel and the cokernel) of
Q^{{\mathrm{gp}}}\rightarrow P^{{\mathrm{gp}}}
are finite groups of orders invertible on
X
.

(b)

The induced morphism
X\rightarrow Y\times_{\text{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[Q])}\text{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[P])
is étale in the usual sense.

Remark A.6.3
.

In the second part of Theorem
A.6.2
, the étale locality condition is used to ensure that
X
admits a chart.
Thus, since we are working with Zariski log structures,
we may assume the required chart exists Zariski locally on
X
.

Example A.6.4
.

Suppose
P
is an fs monoid such that
P^{\mathrm{gp}}
is torsion-free.
Let
X=\mathbb{A}_{P}
and
Y=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
be equipped with the trivial logarithmic structure.
Then,
according to the theorem,

X
is logarithmically smooth relative to
Y
, though the underlying toric variety might be singular.
This is Kato’s “magic of log.”

Example A.6.5
.

Let
\sigma
be the cone of
M_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
generated by the standard basis vectors
e_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq d
.
For integers
a_{j}>0
,
1\leq i\leq d
,
let
\tau
be the subcone of
\sigma
generated by
a_{1}e_{1}+\dots+a_{d}e_{d}
.
We set
P:=M\cap\sigma
and
Q:=M\cap\tau
.
Assuming the greatest common divisor
N:=\text{gcd}(a_{1},\dots,a_{d})
is invertible in the field
k
,
we can identify the fiber product

\operatorname{Spec}(k)\times_{\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[1/N][Q])}\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[1/N][P])

with the normal crossing variety

X:=\operatorname{Spec}(k[x_{1},\dots,x_{d}]/(x_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots x_{d}^{a_{d}})).

There are morphisms

\textstyle{\mathbb{N}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{k\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{N}^{d}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{k[x_{1},\dots,x_{d}]/(x_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots x_{d}^{a_{d}}),}

(A.6.2)

where
\varphi(1)=a_{1}e_{1}+\dots+a_{d}e_{d}
and
\psi(e_{i})=x_{i}
.
The induced morphism

(X,\mathbb{N}^{d})\to(\operatorname{Spec}(k),\mathbb{N})

is log smooth.

Example A.6.6
.

Suppose
(X,\mathcal{M}_{X})
is an fs log scheme which is log smooth over
\operatorname{Spec}(k)
.
Then
X
is covered étale locally by schemes which are smooth over toric varieties,
and there exists a divisor
D
of
X
such that the log structure is equivalent to that of
j^{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{X\setminus D}\cap\mathcal{O}_{X}
,
where
j\colon X\setminus D\to X
is the inclusion.

Example A.6.7
.

The endomorphism of
\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{N}}
induced by
t\mapsto t^{n}
is a Kummer morphism of log schemes,
and a log étale morphism in case
n
and
\text{char}(k)
are coprime.

A.7.
Logarithmic differentials

One basic idea in log geometry is that the sheaves of monoids specify where
d\log
makes sense.
To form sheaves of logarithmic differentials,
one adds to the sheaf of differentials symbols of the form
d\log\alpha(m)
for all elements
m\in\mathcal{M}_{X}
.
