Theorem A.14.13
.

Let
f:Y\rightarrow X
be a morphism in
lSm/k
.
Then there exists a log modification
X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
such that the projection

f^{\prime}:Y\times_{X}X^{\prime}\rightarrow X^{\prime}

is integral. In particular,
f^{\prime}
is exact.

Démonstration.

Due to Proposition
A.10.2
, we reduce to the case when
X
is in
SmlSm/k
.
There exists a Zariski cover
\{U_{1},\ldots,U_{n}\}
of
X
and log modifications
U_{i}^{\prime}\to U_{i}
such that the projection
Y\times_{X}U_{i}^{\prime}\to U_{i}^{\prime}
is integral for every
i
, see
[
54
]
.
Thanks to Theorem
A.14.12
, we may assume that
U_{i}^{\prime}
is obtained by taking a sequence of star subdivisions of
\Delta_{U_{i}}
.
The sequence of star subdivions can be naturally extended to a sequence of star subdivisions of
\Delta_{X}
.
It follows that there exists a log modification
X_{i}^{\prime}\to X
such that
U_{i}^{\prime}\simeq U_{i}\times_{X}X_{i}^{\prime}
for every
i
.

Take
X^{\prime}:=X_{1}^{\prime}\times_{X}\cdots\times_{X}X_{n}^{\prime}
.
Since the projection
Y\times_{X}U_{i}^{\prime}\to U_{i}^{\prime}
is integral for every
i
, the projection
f^{\prime}\colon Y\times_{X}X^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}
is integral Zariski locally on
X^{\prime}
.
This implies that
f^{\prime}
is integral.
∎

Proposition A.14.14
.

Let
f\colon X\rightarrow S
be an exact morphism in
lSm/k
.
Then for every log modification
g\colon Y\rightarrow X
, there is a log modification
S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
such that the pullback

r\colon Y\times_{S}S^{\prime}\longrightarrow X\times_{S}S^{\prime}

is an isomorphism.

Démonstration.

There exists a log modification
S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
such that the projection

q\colon Y\times_{S}S^{\prime}\longrightarrow S^{\prime}

is exact owing to Theorem
A.14.13
.
Since
f
is exact, the projection
p\colon X\times_{S}S^{\prime}\rightarrow S^{\prime}
is exact.
It follows that

r:Y\times_{S}S^{\prime}\rightarrow X\times_{S}S^{\prime}

is exact owing to
[
76
, Proposition III.3.2.1(1)]
.
We deduce that
r
is an open immersion owing to Proposition
A.11.13
.
Since
r
is surjective and proper, it is an isomorphism.
∎

Corollary A.14.15
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow S
be an exact morphism in
lSm/k
.
Then the functor

f^{*}:S_{div}\rightarrow X_{div}

mapping
S^{\prime}\in S_{div}
to
S^{\prime}\times_{S}X
is cofinal.

Démonstration.

Immediate from Proposition
A.14.14
.
∎

Annexe B
Model structures on the category of complexes

B.1.
Descent structures

In this appendix, we recall the construction of model structures on chain complexes.
We follow the approach in
[
19
]
using descent structures.

Definition B.1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a triangulated category, and let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category.

(i)

An object
X
of
\mathcal{T}
is called
compact
if the functor

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{T}}(X,-)

commutes with small sums.

(ii)

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a family of objects of
\mathcal{A}
.
We say that
\mathcal{F}

generates

\mathcal{A}
if, for any object
F
of
\mathcal{A}
, there is an epimorphism
\oplus X\rightarrow F
from a small sum of objects of
\mathcal{F}
.

(iii)

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a family of objects of
\mathcal{T}
. We say that
\mathcal{F}

generates

\mathcal{T}
if the family of functors

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{T}}(X[n],-)

for
X\in\mathcal{F}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
is conservative.

(iv)

We say that
\mathcal{T}
is
compactly generated
if there is a family that generates
\mathcal{T}
and consists of compact objects.

(v)

A
localizing subcategory
of
\mathcal{T}
is a triangulated subcategory of
\mathcal{T}
that is stable by small sums. For a family
\mathcal{F}
of objects of
\mathcal{A}
, we write
\langle\mathcal{F}\rangle
for the smallest localizing subcategory of
\mathcal{T}
containing
\mathcal{F}
.

Definition B.1.2

(
[
19
, Definition 2.2]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a Grothendieck abelian category.
For
\mathcal{G}
an essentially small set of objects of
\mathcal{A}
, and
\mathcal{H}
an essentially small set of complexes of
\mathcal{A}
we make the following definitions.

(i)

The class of
\mathcal{G}
-
cofibrations
(or simply
cofibrations
) is the smallest class of morphisms in
\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{A})

that contains the morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[}0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F{]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F{]},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm id}

where
F\in\mathcal{G}
and
0
sits in degree
-1
, and is closed under shifts, pushouts, retracts, and transfinite compositions.

(ii)

A complex
K
of
\mathcal{A}
is called
\mathcal{G}
-
cofibrant
(or simply
cofibrant
) if
0\rightarrow K
is a
\mathcal{G}
-cofibration.

(iii)

A complex
K
of
\mathcal{A}
is called
\mathcal{G}
-
local
(or simply
local
) if for any
\mathcal{G}
-cofibrant complex
L
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
the induced homomorphism

\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf K}(\mathcal{A})}(L[n],K)\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{A})}(L[n],K)

is an isomorphism of abelian groups.

(iv)

A complex
K
of
\mathcal{A}
is called
\mathcal{H}
-
flasque
(or simply
flasque
) if for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
L\in\mathcal{H}
we have

\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf K}(\mathcal{A})}(L[n],K)=0.

We say that
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
is a
descent structure
for
\mathcal{A}
if
\mathcal{G}
generates
\mathcal{A}
and any
\mathcal{H}
-flasque complex is
\mathcal{G}
-local.

Assume that
\mathcal{A}
has a closed symmetric monoidal structure.
Then
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
is called
weakly flat
if
\mathcal{G}
is closed under tensor products up to isomorphism, the unit object of
\mathcal{A}
is in
\mathcal{G}
,
and
K\otimes L
is acyclic for any
K\in\mathcal{G}
and
L\in\mathcal{H}
.

Next we recall the
injective model structure
on chain complexes in Grothendieck abelian categories.

Definition B.1.3
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a Grothendieck abelian category.
A monomorphism in
\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{A})
is called an
injective cofibration
.
By
[
19
, Theorem 2.1]
,
\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{A})
is a proper cellular model category with quasi-isomorphisms as weak equivalences and injective cofibrations as cofibrations.
A fibration with respect to the injective model structure is called an
injective fibration
.

We shall refer to the following model structure as the
descent model structure
.

Proposition B.1.4
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a Grothendieck abelian category with a descent structure
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
.
Then the following properties hold.

(1)

\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{A})
is a proper cellular model category with quasi-isomorphisms as weak equivalences and
\mathcal{G}
-cofibrations as cofibrations.

(2)

A complex
K
is
\mathcal{G}
-local if and only if it is
\mathcal{H}
-flasque.

(3)

A morphism of complexes is a fibration if and only if it is a degreewise surjection with a
\mathcal{G}
-local kernel.

(4)

Assume that
\mathcal{A}
has a closed symmetric monoidal structure. If
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
is weakly flat, then
\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{A})
is a symmetric monoidal model category.

Démonstration.

By
[
19
, Theorem 2.5]
(resp.
[
19
, Corollary 5.5]
, resp.
[
19
, Proposition 3.2]
), we have (1) and (2) (resp. (3), resp. (4)).
∎

Proposition B.1.5
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a Grothendieck abelian category, and let
\mathcal{G}
be an essentially small set of objects of
\mathcal{A}
generating
\mathcal{A}
.
Then the following properties hold.

(1)

\mathcal{G}
generates
\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{A})
.

(2)

\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{A})=\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle
.

Démonstration.

(1) Let
f:F\rightarrow G
be a morphism in
\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{A})
. Assume that the induced map

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{A})}(X[n],F)\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{A})}(X[n],G)

is an isomorphism for any
X\in\mathcal{G}
and
n\in Z
.
For a cone
H
of
f
, we have

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{A})}(X[n],H)=0.

Choose an injective fibrant resolution
H\rightarrow I
.
If
I
is not quasi-isomorpic to
0
, then
H^{n}(I)\neq 0
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Choose an epimorphism
J\rightarrow\operatorname{Ker}d^{n}
from a small sum
J
of objects of
\mathcal{G}
, where
d^{i}
is the differential map
I^{i}\rightarrow I^{i+1}
.
Then the
J\rightarrow\operatorname{Ker}d^{n}
does not factor through
\operatorname{Im}d^{n-1}
, and we reach the contradiction

\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf D}(\mathcal{A})}(J[-n],H)\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf K}(\mathcal{A})}(J[-n],I)\neq 0.

Thus
I
is quasi-isomorphic to
0
, and we conclude that
f
is an isomorphism.

(2) Since
\mathcal{G}
generates
\mathcal{A}
, for any object
F
of
\mathcal{A}
, there is a quasi-isomorphism
P\rightarrow F
where
P
is a bounded above complex of objects that are small sums of objects of
\mathcal{G}
.
Thus we have an inclusion
\mathcal{A}\subset\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle
,
which readily implies
\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{A})=\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle
.
∎

Definition B.1.6
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a Grothendieck abelian category with a descent structure
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
, and let
\mathcal{W}
be an essentially small set of
\mathcal{G}
-cofibrant complexes of
\mathcal{A}
.
Recall the following definitions from
[
19
, §4]
.

(i)

A complex
G
is called
\mathcal{W}
-local
if for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
F\in\mathcal{W}
we have

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{A})}(F[n],G)=0.

(ii)

A morphism
F\rightarrow F^{\prime}
of complexes is called a
\mathcal{W}
-equivalence
if for any
\mathcal{W}
-local complex
G
the induced homomorphism

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{A})}(F^{\prime}[n],G)\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{A})}(F[n],G)

is an isomorphism of abelian groups.

(iii)

A morphism of complexes is called a
\mathcal{W}
-fibration
if it is a fibration with respect to the descent model structure with a
\mathcal{W}
-local kernel.

Example B.1.7

(
[
19
, Example 2.3]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category with a topology
t
and sheafification functor
a_{t}^{*}
.
The category of sheaves
\mathbf{Shv}_{t}(\mathcal{C},\Lambda)
of
\Lambda
-modules has a descent structure
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
given as follows.
Let
\Lambda(X)
denote the free presheaf of
\Lambda
-modules generated by
X\in\mathcal{C}
.
For a hypercover
\mathscr{X}\rightarrow X
we let
\Lambda(\mathscr{X})
denote the associated complex of sheaves.
Now let
\mathcal{G}
be the family of objects
a_{t}^{*}\Lambda(X)
for all
X\in\mathcal{C}
,
and let
\mathcal{H}
be the family of cones of the morphisms
a_{t}^{*}\Lambda(\mathscr{X})\rightarrow a_{t}^{*}\Lambda(X)
for all hypercovers
\mathscr{X}\rightarrow X
.
With these definitions
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
gives a descent structure of
\mathbf{Shv}_{t}(\mathcal{C},\Lambda)
.

Note that a complex
\mathcal{F}\in\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{Shv}_{t}(\mathcal{C},\Lambda))
is
\mathcal{H}
-flasque if and only if for every hypercover
\mathscr{X}\rightarrow X
we have that

\mathcal{F}(X)\simeq{\rm Tot}^{\pi}\mathcal{F}(\mathscr{X}).

(B.1.1)

This is equivalent to saying that for every hypercover
\mathscr{X}\rightarrow X
and integer
i\in\mathbb{Z}
we have that

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{K}(\mathbf{Shv}_{t}(\mathcal{C},\Lambda))}(a_{t}^{*}\Lambda(X),\mathcal{F}[i])\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{K}(\mathbf{Shv}_{t}(\mathcal{C},\Lambda))}(a_{t}^{*}\Lambda(\mathscr{X}),\mathcal{F}[i]).

(B.1.2)

Proposition B.1.8
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a Grothendieck abelian category with a descent structure
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
, and let
\mathcal{W}
be an essentially small set of
\mathcal{G}
-cofibrant complexes of
\mathcal{A}
.

(1)

The category
\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{A})
is a proper cellular model category with
\mathcal{W}
-equivalences as weak equivalences,

\mathcal{G}
-cofibrations as cofibrations,
and
\mathcal{W}
-fibrations as fibrations.
This model structure is the left Bousfield localization of the descent model structure by the maps
0\rightarrow T[n]
for
T\in\mathcal{W}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Its homotopy category
\mathbf{D}_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathcal{A})
is equivalent to the Verdier quotient
\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{A})/\langle\mathcal{W}\rangle
,
where
\langle\mathcal{W}\rangle
is the smallest localizing subcategory of
\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{A})
containing
\mathcal{W}
.

(2)

Assume that
\mathcal{A}
has a closed symmetric monoidal structure and that
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
is weakly flat.
If
T\otimes G\in\mathcal{W}
for any
T\in\mathcal{W}
and
G\in\mathcal{G}
,
then
\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{A})
is a symmetric monoidal model category and the localization functor
\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{A})\rightarrow\mathbf{D}_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathcal{A})
is a symmetric monoidal functor.
This model structure is called the
\mathcal{W}
-local descent model structure
.

Démonstration.

According to
[
19
, Propositions 4.3, 4.7]
(resp.
[
19
, Proposition 4.11]
), we have (1) (resp. (2)).
∎

Proposition B.1.9
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
(resp.
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
) be a Grothendieck abelian category with a descent structure
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
(resp.
(\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mathcal{H}^{\prime})
), and let

f^{*}:\mathcal{A}\rightleftarrows\mathcal{A}^{\prime}:f_{*}

be a pair of adjoint additive functors.
Assume that

(i)

for any
K
in
\mathcal{G}
,
f^{*}K
is a direct sum of elements of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
,

(ii)

for any
K
in
\mathcal{H}
,
f^{*}K
is in
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
.

Then the induced pair of adjunctions

f^{*}:\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{A})\rightleftarrows\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}):f_{*}

is a Quillen pair with respect to the descent model structures.

Démonstration.

This is the content of
[
19
, Proposition 2.14]
.
∎

Proposition B.1.10
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a Grothendieck abelian category with a descent structure
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
, let
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
be another Grothendieck abelian category, and let

f^{*}:\mathcal{A}\rightleftarrows\mathcal{A}^{\prime}:f_{*}

be a pair of adjoint additive functors.
Assume that

(i)

f_{*}
is exact,

(ii)

for any complex
H
in
\mathcal{H}
,
f^{*}H
is quasi-isomorphic to
0
.

Then
(f^{*}\mathcal{G},f^{*}\mathcal{H})
is a descent structure for
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and the induced pair of adjunctions

f^{*}:\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{A})\rightleftarrows\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}):f_{*}

is a Quillen pair with respect to the descent model structures.

Démonstration.

Since
f^{*}
and
f_{*}
are additive functors, there is an induced adjoint functor pair

f^{*}:{\bf K}(\mathcal{A})\rightleftarrows{\bf K}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}):f_{*}

Let us consider the injective model structures on
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.
For a
f^{*}\mathcal{H}
-flasque complex
L
we choose an injective fibrant replacement
p:L\rightarrow L^{\prime}
in
\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
.
For any
\mathcal{G}
-cofibrant object
K
in
\mathcal{A}
, we have the induced commutative diagram of abelian groups :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})}(f^{*}K,L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})}(f^{*}K,L^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf K}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})}(f^{*}K,L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf K}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})}(f^{*}K,L^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf K}(\mathcal{A})}(K,f_{*}L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf K}(\mathcal{A})}(K,f_{*}L^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{A})}(K,f_{*}L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{A})}(K,f_{*}L^{\prime})\ }
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w^{\prime}}

In the following, we show that
u
is an isomorphism.
It follows that
L
is a
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
-local complex.

Since
p
is a quasi-isomorphism,
w
is an isomorphism.
Moreover,

f_{*}p
is also an isomorphism since
f_{*}
is exact.
It follows that
w^{\prime}
is an isomorphism.
Since
L^{\prime}
is an injectively fibrant object, we deduce
u^{\prime}
is an isomorphism.
For any
H\in\mathcal{H}
,
f^{*}H
is quasi-isomorphic to
0
, so
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})}(f^{*}H,L^{\prime})=0
.
Using that
u^{\prime}
is an isomorphism, we obtain

\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf K}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})}(f^{*}H,L^{\prime})=0.

Thus we have

\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf K}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})}(H,f_{*}L^{\prime})=0.

That is,

f_{*}L^{\prime}
is
\mathcal{H}
-flasque and hence
f_{*}L^{\prime}
is
\mathcal{G}
-local since
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
is a descent structure for
\mathcal{A}
.
This implies
v^{\prime}
is an isomorphism.
Since
L
is a
f^{*}\mathcal{H}
-flasque complex we have

\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf K}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})}(f^{*}H,L)=0.

That is,

\operatorname{Hom}_{{\bf K}(\mathcal{A})}(H,f_{*}L)=0
and hence
f_{*}L
is a
\mathcal{H}
-flasque complex.
This implies
v
is an isomorphism.

To summarize, we have shown that
u^{\prime}
,
v
,
v^{\prime}
,
w
, and
w^{\prime}
are isomorphisms.
This implies
u
is an isomorphism and that
(f^{*}\mathcal{G},f^{*}\mathcal{H})
is a descent structure for
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.
The remaining assertion follows from Proposition
B.1.9
.
∎

Proposition B.1.11
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
(resp.
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
) be a Grothendieck abelian category with a descent structure
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
(resp.
(\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mathcal{H}^{\prime})
),
let
\mathcal{W}
(resp.
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
) be an essentially small set of
\mathcal{G}
-cofibrant (resp.
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
-cofibrant) complexes of
\mathcal{A}
(resp.
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
),
and let

f^{*}:\mathcal{A}\rightleftarrows\mathcal{A}^{\prime}:f_{*}

be a pair of adjoint additive functors.
Assume that

(i)

for any
K
in
\mathcal{G}
,
f^{*}K
is a direct sum of elements of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
,

(ii)

for any
K
in
\mathcal{H}
,
f^{*}K
is in
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
,

(iii)

for any
K
in
\mathcal{W}
,
f^{*}
is in
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
.

Then the induced pair of adjunctions

f^{*}:\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{A})\rightleftarrows\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}):f_{*}

is a Quillen pair with respect to the
\mathcal{W}
-local descent model structures.

Démonstration.

This is the content of
[
19
, Theorem 4.9]
.
∎

Proposition B.1.12
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a Grothendieck abelian category with a descent structure
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
and let
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
be a Grothendieck abelian category.
Suppose
\mathcal{W}
is an essentially small set of
\mathcal{G}
-cofibrant complexes of
\mathcal{A}
,
and let

f^{*}:\mathcal{A}\rightleftarrows\mathcal{A}^{\prime}:f_{*}

be a pair of adjoint additive functors.
Assume that

(i)

f_{*}
is exact,

(ii)

for any complex
H
in
\mathcal{H}
,
f^{*}H
is quasi-isomorphic to
0
.

Then the induced pair of adjunctions

f^{*}:\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{A})\rightleftarrows\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}):f_{*}

is a Quillen pair with respect to the
\mathcal{W}
-local descent model structure on
\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{A})
and the
f^{*}\mathcal{W}
-local model structure on
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
.

Démonstration.

This follows from Propositions
B.1.10
and
B.1.11
.
∎

Definition B.1.13
.

A descent structure
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
on a Grothendieck abelian category is called
bounded
if every object of
\mathcal{H}
is a bounded complexes of objects that are finite sums of objects of
\mathcal{G}
.

Proposition B.1.14
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a Grothendieck abelian category with a bounded descent structure
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
.
If
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(X,-)
commutes with small sums for any
X\in\mathcal{G}
, then
X[n]
is a compact object in
\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{A})
for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
X\in\mathcal{G}
.

Démonstration.

See
[
19
, Theorem 6.2]
.
∎

Proposition B.1.15
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a Grothendieck abelian category with a bounded descent structure
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
, and let
\mathcal{W}
be an essentially small set of bounded complexes of objects that are finite sums of objects of
\mathcal{G}
.
If
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(X,-)
commutes with small sums for any
X\in\mathcal{G}
, then
\mathcal{G}
generates
\mathbf{D}_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathcal{A})
and
X[n]
is a compact object in
\mathbf{D}_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathcal{A})
for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
X\in\mathcal{G}
.

Démonstration.

For any family
\mathcal{F}
of objects of
\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{A})
, let
\langle\mathcal{F}\rangle
denote the smallest localizing subcategory of
\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{A})
containing
\mathcal{F}
.
Note that
\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle=\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{A})
by Proposition
B.1.5
(2).
Owing to Proposition
B.1.8
(1), we have
\mathbf{D}_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathcal{A})\simeq\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle/\langle\mathcal{W}\rangle
.
Proposition
B.1.14
shows
X[n]
is a compact object in
\mathbf{D}_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathcal{A})
for any
n\in\mathcal{Z}
and
X\in\mathcal{G}
.
Thus any bounded complex of objects that are finite sums of objects of
\mathcal{G}
is compact in
\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{A})
.
In particular,
any object of
\mathcal{W}
is compact in
\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{A})
.
To conclude we apply Thomason’s localization theorem
[
71
, Theorem 4.4.9]
—
in loc. cit.,

\mathcal{T}^{\aleph_{0}}
denotes the class of compact objects in a triangulated category
\mathcal{T}
.
∎

Annexe C
Categorical toolbox

In this appendix we collect some useful yoga of triangulated categories.

C.1.
Category of squares

We fix a category
\mathcal{A}
and a symmetric monoidal triangulated category
\mathcal{T}
.

Non-canonicity of the cone construction is well-known in triangulated categories.
To impose canonicality for cones of morphisms in
\mathcal{A}
, we first introduce the notion of
n
-squares, which helps us deal with iterated cones.

Definition C.1.1
.

For
n\geq 0
, let
[n]
denote the
n
th cubical category,
i.e.,
the (nonfull) subcategory of the category
\mathsf{Fin}
of finite sets whose objects are
n+1
-uples
\underline{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\{0,1\}^{n}
.
The set of morphisms
{\rm hom}_{[n]}(\underline{a},\underline{b})
for
\underline{a}\neq\underline{b}
consists of a single element if
a_{i}\leq b_{i}
for every index
i
,
and it is empty otherwise.
We note that all morphisms in
[n]
are generated by morphisms of the form
a\rightarrow b
for which there exists an index
1\leq i\leq n
such that

b_{j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}a_{j}+1&\text{if }j=i\\
a_{j}&\text{if }j\neq i.\end{array}\right.

We define the face functors

s_{i,0},s_{i,1}\colon[n-1]\to[n],\quad i=1,\ldots,n

by inserting
0
(resp.
1
) in the
i
-th coordinate.

Definition C.1.2
.

An
n
-square in a category
\mathcal{A}
is a functor
C\colon[n]\to\mathcal{A}
, and a morphism of
n
-squares is a natural transformation of functors
C_{1}\Rightarrow C_{2}
between
n
-squares.
Note that a
0
-square is simply an object of
\mathcal{A}
,
a
1
-square is an arrow
C_{0}\to C_{1}
where
C_{0}
and
C_{1}
are objects of
\mathcal{A}
, and so on.
We write
C_{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})}\in{\rm Ob}(\mathcal{A})
for
C((a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}))
.
We denote by

\mathbf{Square}_{\mathcal{A}}

the coproduct of the categories of
n
-squares for all integer
n\geq 0
.

Definition C.1.3
.

For an
n
-square
C
with
n\geq 1
, let
s_{i,0}^{*}C
(resp.
s_{i,1}^{*}
) denote the
(n-1)
-square that is the restriction of
C
to all the coordinates of the form

\displaystyle(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1},0,a_{i+1},\ldots,a_{n})

\displaystyle\text{(resp.\ }(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1},1,a_{i+1},\ldots,a_{n})).

or, equivalently, the composition
[n-1]\xrightarrow{s_{i,\varepsilon}}[n]\xrightarrow{C}\mathcal{A}
for
\varepsilon=0,1
.
Note that if
C
is an
(n+1)
-square, then there is a natural morphism of
n
-squares

s_{i,0}^{*}C\rightarrow s_{i,1}^{*}C.

Definition C.1.4
.

If
\mathcal{A}
is monoidal, for an
n
-square
C
and
m
-square
D
we denote by

C\otimes D

the
m+n
square such that the object at the coordinate
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},b_{1},\ldots,b_{m})
is given by the tensor product of the objects at the coordinates

(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
in
C
and
(b_{1},\ldots,b_{m})
in
D
.
The morphisms in
C\otimes D
are naturally induced by the morphisms in
C
and
D
.
The above defines a monoidal structure on
\mathbf{Square}_{\mathcal{A}}
.

We shall often assume that there exists a monoidal functor

M\colon\mathbf{Square}_{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}

satisfying the following conditions.

(Sq)

Square condition.

For every integer
n\geq 1
and
n
-square
C
, there exist functorial morphisms

d_{i,C}\colon M(s_{i,1}^{*}C)\rightarrow M(C),\;\partial_{i,C}\colon M(C)\rightarrow M(s_{i,0}^{*}C)[1]

such that

M(s_{i,0}^{*}C)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle M(\theta_{C,i})}}{{\longrightarrow}}M(s_{i,1}^{*}C)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{i,C}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M(C)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{i,C}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M(s_{i,0}^{*}C)[1]

is a distinguished triangle, where
\theta_{C,i}\colon s_{i,0}^{*}C\rightarrow s_{i,1}^{*}C
is the canonical morphism induced by
C
.

For every integer
n\geq 2
with
1\leq i<j\leq n
and
n
-square
C
, we require that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(s_{i,1}^{*}s_{j,1}^{*}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(s_{i,1}^{*}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M({s_{i,1}^{*}s_{j,0}^{*}C[1]})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(s_{j,1}^{*}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M({s_{j,0}^{*}C[1]})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M({s_{i,0}^{*}s_{j,1}^{*}C[1]})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M({s_{i,0}^{*}C[1]})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(s_{i,0}^{*}s_{j,0}^{*}C[2])}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{i,s_{j,1}^{*}C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{j,s_{i,1}^{*}C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{i,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{j,s_{i,1}^{*}C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{i,s_{j,0}^{*}C}[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{j,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{i,s_{j,1}^{*}C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{i,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{j,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\partial_{i,s_{j,0}^{*}C}{[1]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{j,s_{i,1}^{*}C}{[1]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\partial_{j,s_{i,1}^{*}C}{[1]}}

commutes, where we use
s_{i,1}^{*}s_{j,1}^{*}=s_{j,1}^{*}s_{i,1}^{*}
etc.

(MSq)

Monoidal square condition.
For every
n
-square
C
and
m
-square
D
, let

T_{C,D}:M(C)\otimes M(D)\rightarrow M(C\otimes D)

be the canonical morphism obtained by the monoidality of
M
.
If
n\geq 1
, then for every
1\leq i\leq n
we require that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(s_{i,0}^{*}C)\otimes M(D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(s_{i,1}^{*}C)\otimes M(D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(C)\otimes M(D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(s_{i,0}^{*}C)\otimes M(D)[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(s_{i,0}^{*}C\otimes D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(s_{i,1}^{*}C\otimes D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(C\otimes D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(s_{i,0}^{*}C\otimes D)[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{s_{i,0}^{*}C,D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(\theta_{i,C})\otimes M(D)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{i,C}\otimes M(D)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{s_{i,1}^{*}C,D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{i,C}\otimes M(D)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{C,D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{s_{i,0}^{*}C,D}{[1]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(\theta_{i,C\otimes D})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{i,C\otimes D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{i,C\otimes D}}

commutes.
Similarly, if
m\geq 1
, then for every
1\leq j\leq m
, we require that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(C)\otimes M(s_{j,0}^{*}D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(C)\otimes M(s_{j,1}^{*}D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(C)\otimes M(D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(C)\otimes M(s_{j,0}^{*}D)[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(C\otimes s_{j,0}^{*}D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(C\otimes s_{j,1}^{*}D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(C\otimes D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(C\otimes s_{j,0}^{*}D)[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{C,s_{j,0}^{*}D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(C)\otimes M(\theta_{j,D})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(C)\otimes d_{j,D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{C,s_{j,1}^{*}D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(C)\otimes\partial_{j,D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{C,D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{C,s_{j,0}^{*}D}{[1]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(\theta_{n+j,C\otimes D})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{n+j,C\otimes D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{n+j,C\otimes D}}

commutes.

The conditions (Sq) and (MSq) are often automatically satisfied if
\mathcal{T}
arises from a simplicial, cofibrantly generated, and locally presentable monoidal model category (such as the category of sheaves of complexes that we consider in this text).

Let us give some basic consequences of (Sq).

Proposition C.1.5
.

Let
f:Y\rightarrow X
be a morphism in
\mathscr{S}/S
.
Then the morphism

M(f):M(Y)\rightarrow M(X)

is an isomorphism if and only if
M(Y\rightarrow X)=0
.

Démonstration.

By (Sq), there is a canonical distinguished triangle

M(Y)\rightarrow M(X)\rightarrow M(Y\rightarrow X)\rightarrow M(Y)[1],

which implies the claim.
∎

Proposition C.1.6
.

Let
C\rightarrow D
be a morphism of
n
-squares.
If the naturally induced morphism

M(C_{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})})\rightarrow M(D_{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})})

is an isomorphism for every coordinate
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, then the naturally induced morphism

M(C)\rightarrow M(D)

is an isomorphism.

Démonstration.

We use induction on
n
.
The case
n=1
is clear, so suppose that
n>1
.
By (Sq), there is a morphism of distinguished triangles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(s_{1,0}^{*}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(s_{1,1}^{*}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(s_{1,0}^{*}C)[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(s_{1,0}^{*}D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(s_{1,1}^{*}D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(s_{1,0}^{*}D)[1].}

By induction, all the vertical morphisms except for
M(C)\rightarrow M(D)
are isomorphisms.
We conclude using the five lemma.
∎

Proposition C.1.7
.

For any commutative square in
\mathscr{S}/S

C=\hbox to83.68pt{\vbox to48.08pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 41.83865pt\lower-24.03725pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-41.83865pt}{-20.37752pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

such that

M(Y^{\prime}\rightarrow X^{\prime})\rightarrow M(Y\rightarrow X)

is an isomorphism, also

M(Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Y)\rightarrow M(X^{\prime}\rightarrow X)

is an isomorphism.

Démonstration.

The condition (Sq) implies that there are canonical distinguished triangles

\displaystyle M(Y^{\prime}\rightarrow X^{\prime})\rightarrow M(Y\rightarrow X)\rightarrow M(C)\rightarrow M(Y^{\prime}\rightarrow X^{\prime})[1],

(C.1.1)

\displaystyle M(Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Y)\rightarrow M(X^{\prime}\rightarrow X)\rightarrow M(C)\rightarrow M(Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Y)[1].

(C.1.2)

By assumption, (
C.1.1
) gives
M(C)=0
.
Now use (
C.1.2
) to conclude.
∎

C.2.
Calculus of fractions

We record a result on the calculus of fractions which is used repeatedly in this text.
In applications, we are usually interested in the case when
S
is a base scheme,
\mathcal{C}=lSm/S
or
SmlSm/S
,
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{T}
, and
F=M
.

Lemma C.2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{A}
be classes of morphisms in a category
\mathcal{C}
such that both classes admit a calculus of right fractions in
\mathcal{C}
,
and let
F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
be a functor.
Assume that for any morphism
f:Y\rightarrow X
in
\mathcal{A}
, there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

in
\mathcal{C}
, where
g\in\mathcal{B}
.
If

F(g):F(Y^{\prime})\rightarrow F(X)

is an isomorphism for every
g
in
\mathcal{B}
, then

F(f):F(Y)\rightarrow F(X)

is an isomorphism for every
f
in
\mathcal{A}
.

Démonstration.

The right Ore condition (dual to
[
38
, I.2.2.c]
) for
\mathcal{B}
implies there is a commutative diagram in
\mathcal{C}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

where
g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}
.
Since
fhf^{\prime}=gf^{\prime}=fg^{\prime}
and
f\in\mathcal{A}
,
there is a morphism
r:Z\rightarrow Y^{\prime\prime}
in
\mathcal{A}
such that
hf^{\prime}r=g^{\prime}r
by the right cancellation condition for
\mathcal{A}
.
By assumption, there is a morphism
s:Z^{\prime}\rightarrow Y^{\prime\prime}
in
\mathcal{B}
that factors through
r
.
Thus
hf^{\prime}s=g^{\prime}s
, and there is a commutative diagram in
\mathcal{C}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Now by assumption,
F(g)
and
F(g^{\prime}s)
are isomorphisms since
g
and
g^{\prime}s
are in
\mathcal{B}
.
Since
F(g)F(f^{\prime}s)=F(f)F(g^{\prime}s)
, we have that

F(f^{\prime}s)F(g^{\prime}s)^{-1}=F(g)^{-1}F(f),

and therefore

{\rm id}=F(g^{\prime}s)F(g^{\prime}s)^{-1}=F(h)F(f^{\prime}s)F(g^{\prime}s)^{-1}=F(h)F(g)^{-1}F(f),

{\rm id}=F(g)F(g)^{-1}=F(f)F(h)F(g)^{-1}.

This shows that
F(f)
is an isomorphism.
∎
